Polyfold fundamental classes and globally structured multivalued perturbations Thanks: This material is based upon work supported by the National Science Foundation under Grants No.DMS-1708916, and DMS-1440140 while the first author was in residence at the Mathematical Sciences Research Institute in Berkeley, California, during the Spring 2018 semester.
Dusa McDuff, Katrin Wehrheim

Abstract
. Work of Hofer–Wysocki–Zehnder has shown that many spaces of pseudoholomorphic curves that arise when studying symplectic manifolds may be described as the zero set of a polyfold Fredholm section. This framework has many analytic advantages. However the methods they develop to extract useful topological information from it are rather cumbersome. This paper develops a general construction of a finite dimensional space of multivalued perturbations of a polyfold Fredholm section such that almost all elements are regularizing. These perturbation are globally structured and explicitly described, and, in cases where the moduli space has no formal boundary, permit a transparent definition of its (rational Čech) fundamental class.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
46,53,58

1.
Introduction

Polyfold theory, initiated by Hofer-Wysocki-Zehnder
in the early 2000s, generalizes the notion of a smooth section of an orbibundle to obtain a nonlinear Fredholm theory for functors between groupoids that promises to make the use of pseudoholomorphic curves in symplectic geometry more widely accessible by combining the following features:

(a)

The theory applies to compactified moduli spaces
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
of pseudoholomorphic curves in the sense that
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}\simeq\sigma^{-1}(0)
is homeomorphic to the solution set of
a so-called scale-smooth (or sc-) Fredholm section
\sigma:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
of a strong polyfold bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
.

1
1

1

While expected for all known types of compact moduli spaces in symplectic geometry, only Gromov-Witten moduli spaces currently have a published description
[
HWZ2
]

of this form.
There is work in progress
[
FH
,
L
]
on the moduli spaces of SFT, Floer theories, and Fukaya categories.

(b)

The theory provides a transparent approach to regularizing such moduli spaces by (multivalued) perturbations of the section
\sigma
that achieve transversality while preserving compactness
and most geometric characteristics
of the perturbed solution set.
2
2

2

This theory is fully developed and documented in
[
HWZ
]
and earlier publications cited therein.

Two key points about this theory contrast sharply with the usual treatments of Fredholm operators on Banach spaces.
3
3

3

For an introduction to scale calculus – the notion of differentiability on Banach spaces used by
[
HWZ
]
– see e.g.
[
FFGW
]
.

\bullet
It is based on a new notion of sc-smoothness, which is designed firstly so that there is an appropriate analog of Fredholm theory, and secondly so that reparametrization groups (such as
{\rm PSL}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
act sc-smoothly on spaces
E
of sc-smooth maps
u:{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\to M
. This is in sharp contrast to the action of such groups on Banach spaces. For example the rotation group
S^{1}
does not act even continuously on the space of
L^{2}
-maps
S^{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

\bullet
The fact that
M
-polyfolds are modelled on the image
r(U)
of an open subset
U\subset E
under a scale smooth retraction (rather than simply on the open set
U
itself) allows one to describe a neighbourhood of a nodal curve that includes stable maps with topologically different domains as an open subset of a single
M
-polyfold.

Thus polyfold theory provides good local models for the moduli space (i.e. the solution set of the given operator), and the challenge is to understand the global structure. There are other theories with analogous features for different notions of “Fredholm section”, in particular various versions of Kuranishi theory
[
FOOO
,
MW3
,
Pa
]
which combines local finite dimensional models into a global section functor
\sigma
between categories (that are generally neither étale nor groupoids).
Although the current ideas may be used to construct a (tame) smooth Kuranishi atlas that models the zero set of
{\sigma}
,
in this paper, we work only in the infinite dimensional setting, constructing a global stabilization of the operator
{\sigma}
, namely a finite dimensional family of multi-sections such that a generic element is a transverse perturbation.

Beyond Gromov-Witten theory, most moduli spaces in symplectic geometry have formal boundary, and in fact come in ‘coherent families’ whose boundary strata are Cartesian or fiber products of other moduli spaces in the family. Extracting algebraic invariants – such as Floer homology, or a well defined SFT algebra – from such families of moduli spaces requires ‘coherent perturbations’ that preserve the identifications of boundary strata. The corresponding challenge of extending multisections prescribed on boundaries is present in both polyfold and Kuranishi theory, and is resolved in
[
HWZ
]
by a class of so-called ‘structurable multisections’.
However, this involves rather elaborate definitions and constructions, and at present does not yield a transparent general existence result for coherent perturbations.

This problem of constructing coherent multivalued perturbations is simplified by our construction of a global Fredholm stabilization in Theorem
2.1.2
.
This provides a finite dimensional family of perturbations that is sufficiently large to achieve transversality, yet is multivalued in the simplest possible way: each perturbation is the orbit of a single-valued section under a global finite group action.
This type of multisection has natural extensions from boundaries and corners, so that a general existence proof of coherent perturbations reduces to the project of extending the global stabilization construction to coherent setups.

1.1.
Overview of constructions

As defined in
[
HWZ
, §16]
, a polyfold
{\mathcal{B}}
is a topological space that is homeomorphic to the realization of a topological groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
that is equipped with a special kind of smooth structure called a sc-smooth structure.
A local model (without formal boundary) for a global Fredholm section
\sigma:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
with zero set
\sigma^{-1}(0)={\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
is given by the quotient by a finite group
G
of a
G
-equivariant scale-smooth section

U\to{\mathcal{R}}=\bigcup_{x\in U}\{x\}\times\Pi_{x}({\mathbb{F}}),x\mapsto(x,f(x)).

Its base
U=r(\widetilde{U})
is the image of a scale-smooth retraction
r:\widetilde{U}\to\widetilde{U}
,
r\circ r=r
, on an open subset
\widetilde{U}\subset{\mathbb{E}}
of a scale-Banach space. The total space
{\mathcal{R}}

is the image of a scale-smooth retraction
\widetilde{U}\times{\mathbb{F}}\to\widetilde{U}\times{\mathbb{F}},(x,y)\mapsto(r(x),\Pi_{x}(y))
given by linear projections
\Pi_{x}:{\mathbb{F}}\to{\mathbb{F}}
on another scale-Banach space. The polyfold-Fredholm property of this section requires the existence of a scale-Fredholm
4
4

4

The Fredholm property in scale calculus implies classical Fredholmness of the linearizations, but due to the lack of classical differentiability requires a ‘contraction germ normal form’. In practice
[
HWZ2
,
W
]
it follows from ‘classical differentiability in all but finitely many directions’.

extension
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{U}\to{\mathbb{F}}
with

\widetilde{f}^{-1}(0)=f^{-1}(0),\quad\ker{\rm d}_{x}\widetilde{f}=\ker{\rm d}_{x}f,\quad\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Pi_{x}({\mathbb{F}})\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}{\rm d}_{x}f$}}\simeq\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}}\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}{\rm d}_{x}\widetilde{f}$}}\,,

and the resulting local chart for the moduli space is an embedding
f^{-1}(0)/G\subset\sigma^{-1}(0)={\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.

The crucial construction that yields both transverse perturbations of the polyfold-Fredholm section
\sigma
and its finite dimensional reductions (Kuranishi charts) is that of a
local Fredholm stabilization
near
x\in\sigma^{-1}(0)
. Here we construct a finite dimensional vector space
E
as the product of
a representative of the cokernel
\scriptstyle\Pi_{x}({\mathbb{F}})\!
/
\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}{\rm d}_{x}f
with the group
G
that acts on
U
as above, so that
G
acts diagonally on

U\times E
. We then define a
G
-equivariant bundle map

\tau:U\times E\to{\mathcal{R}}
covering the identity, so that
\sigma-\tau
is transverse to the zero section of
{\mathcal{R}}
near
(x,0)\in U\times E
. (For details see Lemma
5.4.1
.)
Each
e\in E
then yields a
local multivalued perturbation

U\ni x\mapsto\bigl(\tau(x,ge)\bigr)_{g\in G}\subset{\mathcal{R}}
, which yields
G
-equivariant transversality of
\sigma|_{U}-\bigl(\tau(\cdot,ge)\bigr)_{g\in G}
for regular values of the map
(\sigma|_{U}-\tau)^{-1}(0)\to E
. Alternatively, the same construction can be viewed as giving a
local finite dimensional reduction

\displaystyle f_{\tau}\,:

\displaystyle\;U_{\tau}:=(\sigma|_{U}-\tau)^{-1}(0)\to E,\quad(e,x)\mapsto e,

\displaystyle\quad\text{with}\quad f_{\tau}^{-1}(0)/G\simeq(\sigma^{-1}(0)\cap U)/G\subset{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}.

Once this local structure is understood, the main difficulty with both the Kuranishi atlas and polyfold approaches is to patch such local perturbations into a globally transverse perturbation, while preserving compactness of the perturbed solution space.

The key idea of the current paper is to model the base polyfold
{\mathcal{B}}
by a groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
whose structure is so transparent that the pullback of the Fredholm section
\sigma:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
has a natural family of globally transverse perturbations. Here are the main constructions:

(i)

Given finitely many local polyfold charts
U_{i}/G_{i}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
covering a compact subset
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}\subset\bigcup_{i}U_{i}/G_{i}
, Theorem
5.2.6
constructs an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with explicitly described object and morphism spaces and
whose realization
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is equivalent as polyfold to an open neighbourhood
|V|\subset{\mathcal{B}}
of
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.

Moreover, Theorem
5.3.1
constructs a Fredholm section functor
f_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
such that the perturbation theory for
|f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)|\simeq{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
is equivalent to that for
\sigma
in the sense that appropriate perturbations for each section have naturally identified solution sets.

(ii)

Given data as in (i) and local Fredholm stabilizations
\tau_{i}:U_{i}\times E_{i}\to{\mathcal{E}}|_{U_{i}}
, Theorem
2.1.2
constructs a global Fredholm stabilization as follows:
There is an étale (but generally not proper) subgroupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
obtained by removing all morphisms that give rise to isotropy.
It carries an action of
G:=\prod G_{i}
such that
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|\,/G\simeq|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
.
We then construct a finite dimensional vector space
E:=\prod_{i}E_{i}
and a
G
-equivariant functor

\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
so that
f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau
is transverse
to the zero section.

(iii)

Given data as in (ii), Corollary
2.1.3
shows that
\tau
applied to generic, sufficiently small
e\in E
induces canonically structured sc
+
-multisection functors
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
and
{\Lambda}_{{\underline{e}}}:{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
that satisfy the requirements of the polyfold perturbation scheme and have easily described solution sets.

(iv)

When
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
has no formal boundary, Theorem
5.1.4
defines a polyfold fundamental class in the rational Çech homology of
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
as the inverse limit of the fundamental classes of the perturbed solution sets.
In this setting, (iii) identifies the polyfold fundamental classes
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{f_{\mathcal{V}}}=[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{\sigma}
, where the latter depends on the initial polyfold Fredholm section
{\sigma}:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
.
5
5

5

The book
[
HWZ
]
does not attempt to define such a fundamental class, instead showing in this situation that there is a well defined notion of integration of sc-smooth forms over
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.

As outlined in
[
Mc2
, Rmk.1.3.8]
,
the current results easily adapt to construct a Kuranishi atlas

6
6

6

Another abstract construction of this type, without identification of perturbations, can be found in
[
Y
]
.

{\mathcal{K}}_{\tau}
for
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
whose perturbation theory is equivalent to the polyfold perturbation theory
for
f_{\mathcal{V}}
and thus for
\sigma
.

Note to the reader:
This paper contains many technical details that readers should not attempt to master until the outlines of the construction are clear. The main theorems are clearly stated in §
2.1
. The language here is very formal; readers can consult
§
2.2
and §
3.1
for background definitions. We recommend that
the reader goes next to §
4.1
, where the main constructions are defined in an abstract setting. This section uses the language developed in §
3.1
–
3.3
concerning
étale categories and groupoid completions. In §
5
, we first show in Theorem
5.1.4
that the perturbed zero sets of polyfold Fredholm sections carry a fundamental class in rational Čech homology, and then complete the proofs of the main theorems by applying the abstract constructions developed in §
4
to the polyfold setting.

2.
Terminology and main results

2.1.
Statement of main theorems

A sc-Fredholm section
\sigma:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
of a strong polyfold bundle
is a
continuous map between topological spaces together with an equivalence class of functors
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
between topological groupoids, whose realization
|f|:|{\mathcal{X}}|\to|{\mathcal{W}}|
together with homeomorphisms
|{\mathcal{X}}|\simeq{\mathcal{B}}
,
|{\mathcal{W}}|\simeq{\mathcal{E}}
induces
\sigma
.
Throughout this paper, we will work with a fixed representative
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
that is a sc-Fredholm section functor of a strong bundle
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
over an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
modeled on M-polyfolds.
7
7

7

Questions of equivalence between such representatives are addressed in
[
HWZ
, §10-11]
.

To summarize these notions we use conventions of
[
HWZ
]
in denoting object and morphism spaces as
{\rm Obj}_{\mathcal{X}}=X
and
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}={\bf X}
; but we will differentiate between object space
X
and groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})
. In particular, the realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|=X/\!\!\sim\,

is the quotient of
X
by the equivalence relation generated by the morphisms
{\bf X}
, equipped with the quotient topology.
The following generalizes the notions of orbifolds, orbibundles, and smooth sections to infinite dimensions and scale calculus. The underlying analytic notions of M-polyfold, sc-Fredholm, etc. are reviewed in §
2.2
along with the finite dimensional cases.

Definition 2.1.1
.

(i)

An
ep-groupoid
as in
[
HWZ
, Def.7.1.3]
is a groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})
equipped with M-polyfold structures
8
8

8

As in other regularization constructions of polyfold theory, we assume that
X

admits sc-smooth partitions of unity. This can be guaranteed by working with M-polyfold charts in Hilbert spaces; see
[
HWZ
, §5.5]
. In classical PDE applications that means working with Sobolev spaces of class
L^{p}
with
p=2
.
Moreover, the boundaries of
X
and
{\bf X}
are assumed to be tame in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §2.5]
.
In the known applications, this is guaranteed by retracts of splicing form; see
[
HWZ
, Lemma 2.5.5]
.
on the object and morphism sets so that

•

source and target maps
s,t:{\bf X}\to X
are local sc-diffeomorphisms and the unit
X\to{\bf X},q\mapsto{\rm id}_{q}
, inverse
{\bf X}\to{\bf X},m\mapsto m^{-1}
, and composition
{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}{\bf X}\to{\bf X},(m,m^{\prime})\mapsto m\circ m^{\prime}
are sc-smooth maps (i.e. the groupoid is
étale
);

•

every
x\in X
has a neighbourhood
N(x)
so that
t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to X
is
proper
.

In addition, we will throughout assume that the realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is paracompact so that
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is metrizable by

[
HWZ
, Thm.7.3.1]
.
We call an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})

nonsingular
if the
isotropy groups

G_{x}:=s^{-1}(x)\cap t^{-1}(x)\subset{\bf X}
are trivial for all
x\in X
.
Finally, we will say that
{\mathcal{X}}
has no formal boundary if the boundary
\partial X
of its object set as defined in
[
HWZ
, §2.4]
is empty.

(ii)

A
strong bundle
as in

[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.1]
9
9

9

This summarizes the discussion following
[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.1]
; for more details see Definition
3.4.1
.

over the ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
is a pair
(P,\mu)
of a strong bundle
P:W\to X
and a strong bundle map
\mu:{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W\to W

so that

P
lifts to a functor
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
given by
{\mathcal{P}}(w)=P(w)
,
{\mathcal{P}}(m,w)=m
.
Here
{\mathcal{W}}
is the ep-groupoid given by

{\rm Obj}_{\mathcal{W}}=W
,
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{W}}={\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W
,

s(m,w)=w
,
t(m,w)=\mu(m,w)
,
{\rm id}_{w}=({\rm id}_{P(w)},w)
, and

(m,w)\circ(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})=\bigl(m\circ m^{\prime},w\bigr)

if
w^{\prime}=\mu(m,w)
.

We will thus often denote strong bundles by
({\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
or simply
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
.

(iii)

A
sc-Fredholm section functor
of the strong bundle
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}

as in
[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.4]
is a functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
that is sc-smooth on object and morphism spaces, satisfies
{\mathcal{P}}\circ f={\rm id}_{\mathcal{X}}
, and is such that
f:X\to W
is sc-Fredholm on the M-polyfold
X
.

(iv)

An
orientation
of a sc-Fredholm section functor
f
as in
[
HWZ
, Def.12.5.4]
is a continuous section
{\mathfrak{o}}:X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
of the orientation bundle over the dense subset of smooth objects
X_{\infty}\subset X
, which is compatible with morphisms as specified in §
2.3
.

Given a sc-Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
that describes a compact moduli space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}\simeq|f^{-1}(0)|
, Lemma
5.4.1
constructs local Fredholm stabilizations near any
x\in f^{-1}(0)
, as explained in §
1.1
.
Now a finite covering of
|f^{-1}(0)|
by such local stabilizations can be used in two ways:
On the one hand, the various fattened solution sets resulting from each local stabilization can be related by appropriate transition data to form a Kuranishi atlas for
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.On the other hand, after an appropriate refinement of the polyfold model, we can combine a covering of
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}\simeq|f^{-1}(0)|
by local Fredholm stabilizations into a global finite dimensional stabilization.
This is our first main result (proven in §
5.2
–
5.4
):

Theorem 2.1.2

(
Global stabilization).

Let
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
be a sc-Fredholm section functor
with compact solution set,
Then choices of local uniformizers and Fredholm stabilizations

(U_{i},G_{i},\tau_{i}:X\times E_{i}\to{\mathcal{W}})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}
covering
|f^{-1}(0)|
, a partition of unity, compatible cover reductions as specified in Theorem
5.4.2
, and suitable
compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
as in Definition
3.5.1

induce

-

an open subset
V\subset X
such that

|V|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is an open neighbourhood of
|f^{-1}(0)|
that contains
|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})|
,

-

a finite group
G:=\prod_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}
and
a finite dimensional vector space
E:=\prod_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}
with
G
-action, and
and

-

a commutative diagram of étale groupoids and functors

\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\scriptstyle{\tau\;\;\;}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}:=\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\;\Psi}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}\;\subset\;{\mathcal{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{P}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\iota}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathcal{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}\;\subset\;{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}

with the following properties:

(i)

{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
and
{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}
are the full subcategories of
{\mathcal{X}}
resp.
{\mathcal{W}}
with objects
V
resp.
W|_{V}=P^{-1}(V)
as in Lemma
3.4.4
.
In particular their realizations are naturally identified with
|{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}|\simeq|V|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
and
|{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}|\simeq|{\mathcal{P}}|^{-1}(|V|)\subset|{\mathcal{W}}|
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an ep-groupoid constructed in Theorem
5.2.6
, covered by finitely many local uniformizers
(V_{I},G_{I})_{I\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}}
so that

{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}=\bigsqcup_{I}V_{I}
and
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{I})=G_{I}\times V_{I}
with
G_{I}=\prod_{i\in I}G_{i}
.
It is equipped with an inner action of
G
in the sense of Definition
3.3.4
,
in particular lifting the trivial action on
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
.
Further, the action on each
V_{I}\subset{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
is given by the natural projection from
G\times V_{I}

to the local uniformizer morphisms

G_{I}\times V_{I}={\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{I})
induced by the projection
G\to G_{I}
.

The functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
, given by natural maps
V_{I=\{i_{1}<\cdot\cdot\}}\to U_{i_{1}}
, is finite-to-one, surjective on objects, and
an equivalence of ep-groupoids in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.10.1.1]
, in particular inducing a homeomorphism

{|\psi|:|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}|=|V|}
.

(iii)

The bundle
{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}:=\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and its section
f_{\mathcal{V}}=\psi^{*}f
are the pullbacks in the sense of Lemma
3.4.4
of
{\mathcal{P}}
and
f
, as spelled out in Theorem
5.3.1
.
The bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is equipped with the natural lift of the inner
G
action on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
from Lemma
3.3.6
, with respect to which both
{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
f_{\mathcal{V}}
are equivariant.
The natural lift of
\psi
to the pullback bundle functor
\Psi:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}
of Lemma
3.4.4
(iii) is a strong bundle equivalence
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.10.4.1]
, and
|\psi|
restricts to a homeomorphism
|f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|f^{-1}(0)|
.
The section
f_{\mathcal{V}}
is sc-Fredholm, and the strong bundle equivalence
\Psi
pulls back any choice of orientation of
f
to an orientation of
f_{\mathcal{V}}
.

(iv)

The étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
constructed in Proposition
4.1.2

is a (nonproper) nonsingular subgroupoid of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with the same objects
{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}}=\bigsqcup_{I}V_{I}
but where enough morphisms have been removed so that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
has at most one morphism between any two objects.
10
10

10

Although we do not attempt to formulate a sense in which
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
is unique, it has a very simple structure: It is the groupoid completion of an étale poset

{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
, that is, the object space

\bigsqcup_{I}V_{I}
has a natural partial order
\preccurlyeq
such that
{\rm Mor}_{{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}(x,y)
is nonempty exactly if
x\preccurlyeq y
; see Lemma
4.2.1
.

The removed morphisms are parts of the group action.
Indeed,
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}

is equipped with an action of
G
so that the inclusion functor
{\iota}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is
G
-equivariant in the sense of Definition
3.3.1
and induces a homeomorphism
|{\iota}|:|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|\,/G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
.

(v)

The category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E
is the product of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
with the category with object space
E
and only identity morphisms.
Then
the functor
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
constructed in Theorem
5.4.2

is
G
-equivariant, a strong bundle map in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.2.6.1]
, and a compactness-controlled Fredholm stabilization in the following sense:

The data
\bigl(N_{\mathcal{V}}:=N\circ\Psi,{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}:=\psi^{-1}({\mathcal{U}})\bigr)
controls compactness of
f_{\mathcal{V}}
and there exists a neighbourhood
O_{\tau}\subset E
of
0
such that the family of maps
\bigl(\tau_{\underline{e}}:{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}},{\underline{o}}\mapsto\tau({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)_{{\underline{e}}\in E}
– while generally not functorial – is
(N_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}})
-regular as follows:

(1)

N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau_{\underline{e}}({\underline{o}}))<1
for all
{\underline{e}}\in O_{\tau}
,
{\underline{o}}\in{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}={\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}}
;

(2)

{\rm supp\,}\tau_{\underline{e}}={\rm cl}\{{\underline{o}}\in{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\,|\,\tau_{\underline{e}}({\underline{o}})\neq 0_{\underline{o}}\}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}
for all
{\underline{e}}\in O_{\tau}
;

(3)

the map
{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\times O_{\tau}\to{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}},({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})\mapsto f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}})-\tau({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})
is transverse to the zero section of
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and in general position over the boundary.

Full details and proofs are given in Theorems
5.2.6
,
5.3.1
, and
5.4.2
.
The construction of such a global finite dimensional stabilization is the common technical work behind two major points of view on how to construct the “virtual” fundamental class of a compact moduli space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}\simeq|f^{-1}(0)|
without formal boundary:
Moreover, as sketched in
[
Mc2
, Rmk.1.3.8]
,
the global stabilization leads directly to the construction of a Kuranishi atlas as well as other finite dimensional models.
On the other hand the following Corollary shows how the stabilization also
yields a collection of multivalued perturbations whose solution sets are compact weighted branched orbifolds and can be used for an inverse limit construction of
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]
in Theorem
5.1.4
.

This construction of transverse perturbations from a global stabilization is the same line of argument as in both general polyfold theory and more classical regularization approaches such as
[
MS
]
.
11
11

11

For example, the stabilization in the trivial isotropy case is the map
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\times X\to W
in the proof of
[
HWZ
, Thm.5.3.5]
, and is the section
{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathcal{J}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
in the proof of
[
MS
, Prop.3.2.1]
.

In polyfold theory (and any other abstract construction of perturbations) the main technical complications arise from nontrivial isotropy. This is resolved locally by working with multivalued perturbations, but their patching and extension from the boundary is surprisingly nontrivial; see
[
HWZ
, §14]
.
We avoid the technicalities of patching or extending multivalued perturbations by following the strategy of
[
MW3
, §3.2]
: Our technical work is in the construction of an appropriate atlas, given by the étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
that is both reduced (to organize overlaps of charts) and pruned (to remove isotropy), and the global action of a group
G
that captures all isotropy.
Then the global stabilization
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
gives rise to a family of single-valued sections

f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}):X_{\mathcal{V}}:={\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}}

of the topological bundle formed by the object spaces of the bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, which fail functoriality only in their lack of
G
-equivariance.
12
12

12

Note here that, while we construct the stabilization functor
\tau
to be
G
-equivariant,
the single-valued perturbation
f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}})
is typically not itself
G
-equivariant because
G
acts nontrivially on
E
.

Taking the
G
-orbit of such a section yields a transverse multivalued perturbation of
f_{\mathcal{V}}
which is more globally symmetric and structured than the complicated hierarchy of
[
HWZ
, §13]
.
Our notion of a globally structured multisection is given in Definition
3.4.5
.
This notion should allow considerable simplification of the intricate extension constructions in
[
HWZ
, §14]
.

The following is a detailed description of these perturbations and perturbed zero sets.
Here we denote by
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
the category with objects
{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,\infty)
and only identity morphisms.
13
13

13

This category is denoted
{\mathbb{Q}}^{+}
in
[
HWZ
]
.

Corollary 2.1.3
.

Let
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}
be a stabilization functor as in Theorem
2.1.2
.

(i)

Each
{\underline{e}}\in E
induces sc
+
-multisection functors
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
given by
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}(w):=\tfrac{1}{\#G}\ \#\{{\underline{h}}\in G\,|\,\tau(P_{\mathcal{V}}(w),{\underline{h}}*{\underline{e}})=w\}

and
{\Lambda}_{{\underline{e}}}:=\Psi_{*}{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
given by pushforward
[
HWZ
, Lemma 11.5.2]
.
Their support (i.e. “graph”) is

\displaystyle{\rm supp\,}{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}

\displaystyle=G*\tau(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times\{{\underline{e}}\})={\textstyle\bigcup_{{\underline{g}}\in G}}\;\tau(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times\{{\underline{g}}*{\underline{e}}\}),

\displaystyle{\rm supp\,}{\Lambda}_{\underline{e}}

\displaystyle=\Psi({\rm supp\,}{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}).

These multisections are structurable in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §13.3]
, and
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}

is globally structured in the sense of Definition
3.4.5
.

(ii)

There is a comeagre (and thus dense) subset
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}\subset O_{\tau}
such that
for all
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
the functors

\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:={\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}\circ f_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
and
\Theta_{\underline{e}}:={\Lambda}_{{\underline{e}}}\circ f:{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
are transversal and in general position over the boundary in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §15.2]
.
This gives both the structure of compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoids
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §9.1]
.

(iii)

If
f
is oriented, then for every
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
both
\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}

and
\Theta_{\underline{e}}
inherit natural orientations.

(iv)

Suppose, in addition to (iii), that
f
has constant Fredholm index
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
and
\psi_{f}:|f^{-1}(0)|\to{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
is a homeomorphism to a compact
Hausdorff space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.
Then the perturbed solution sets

\displaystyle|{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}|=|\{{\underline{x}}\in X_{\mathcal{V}}\,|\,\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}({\underline{x}})>0\}|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|,

\displaystyle|{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{\underline{e}}|=|\{x\in X|_{V}\,|\,\Theta_{\underline{e}}(x)>0\}|\subset|V|

carry natural fundamental classes denoted
[\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}],[\Theta_{\underline{e}}]
that belong to

\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
if
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
has no formal boundary, and to the relative group

\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\smallsetminus}{\partial}{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
if
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
has formal boundary
{\partial}{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.
14
14

14

See (
A.3
) for notation.

Further, if there is no formal boundary
\psi_{f_{\mathcal{V}}}:=\psi_{f}\circ|\psi|
is a homeomorphism
|f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)|\to{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
, and
the perturbations in (ii) induce the same fundamental classes as defined in Theorem
5.1.4
,

[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{f}\;=\;(\psi_{f})_{*}\;\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,(\psi_{k})_{*}\bigl[\Theta_{{\underline{e}}_{k}}\bigr]\;=\;(\psi_{f_{\mathcal{V}}})_{*}\;\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,(\psi_{{\mathcal{V}},k})_{*}\bigl[\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}_{k}}\bigr]\;=\;[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{f_{\mathcal{V}}}\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}}).

(v)

In particular, in the situation of (iv) there is a well defined notion of
integration over the perturbed solution sets, which satisfies Stokes’ Theorem in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.4.3]
, and the results can be identified via the homeomorphism
|\psi|\,|_{|{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}|}:|{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}|\to|{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{\underline{e}}|
.

In this Corollary, the identity for the support of the multisection in (i) follows from
G
-equivariance of the functor
\tau
; the rest of the proof is given at the end of §
5.4
.

2.2.
Scale Calculus

The purpose of this subsection is to give a very brief summary of the key notions of scale calculus (M-polyfold, strong bundle, and sc-Fredholm) that were developed in
[
HWZ
]
to replace the notions of Banach manifolds, Banach bundles, and Fredholm sections – which turned out not to be good fits for global descriptions of moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic curves. For a more detailed exposition see
[
FFGW
]
; for an application-centered introduction see
[
BDSVW
]
.

An
M-polyfold
without boundary, as defined in
[
HWZ
, Def.2.3.4]
, is analogous to the notion of a Banach manifold: While the latter are locally homeomorphic to open subsets of a Banach space, an M-polyfold is locally homeomorphic to the image of a retract
r:U\to U
of an open subset
U\subset E
of a Banach space
E
.
15
15

15

To ensure sc-smooth partitions of unity, we assume
E
to be a Hilbert space as in
[
HWZ
, Cor.5.5.17]
.
While
r
is generally not classically differentiable, it is required to be scale-smooth (or ‘sc-smooth’) with respect to a scale structure (or ‘sc-structure’) on
E
– see
[
FFGW
, §2.2]
for an introduction.

Unless otherwise specified, we will allow M-polyfolds to have “tame” boundary, arising from local models in
[0,\infty)^{k}\times E
satisfying the tameness condition
[
HWZ
, Def.2.5.2]
. Each such M-polyfold
16
16

16

Any (M-)polyfold in this paper is a “tame (M-)polyfold” in the language of
[
HWZ
]
. See
[
FFGW
, §5.3]
for an explanation of the additional condition – previously called “neatness”.

carries a well defined degeneracy index
d_{X}:X\to{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
given in local models by counting the number of coordinates in
[0,\infty)^{k}
that equal to zero. With that, the
boundary
of an M-polyfold is defined as
\partial X:=\{x\in X\,|\,d_{X}(x)\geq 1\}
, and each connected component
Y\subset\{x\in X\,|\,d_{X}(x)=1\}
inherits the structure of an M-polyfold with degeneracy
d_{Y}=d_{X}-1
by
[
HWZ
, Thm.2.5.10]
. We will call such
Y\subset\partial X
a
boundary stratum
, while its closure is called a face in
[
HWZ
]
. For
l\geq 2
, the connected components of
\{x\in X\,|\,d_{X}(x)=l\}
– called
corner strata
– then inherit sc-smooth structures by local descriptions as intersections of local faces as in
[
HWZ
, Prop.2.5.16]
.

As with boundary and corners, most other notions of scale calculus arise by generalizing classical differential geometry with the two notions of sc-calculus and retracts. For example, a sc-smooth map
f:X\to Y
between M-polyfolds
X,Y
is called
local sc-diffeomorphism
if for every
x\in X
and sufficiently small open neighbourhood
O\subset X
of
x
(we can assume a retract chart
O\simeq r(U)
) the image
f(O)\subset Y
is open and there is a sc-smooth map
f_{O}^{-1}:f(O)\to O
that is inverse to
f
, i.e.
f_{O}^{-1}\circ f={\rm id}_{O}
and
f\circ f_{O}^{-1}={\rm id}_{f(O)}
.

Given the notion of ep-groupoid in Definition
2.1.1
, we can also view M-polyfolds as the realizations
|{\mathcal{X}}|
of nonsingular ep-groupoids
{\mathcal{X}}
.
On the other hand, the following remark specializes Definition
2.1.1
to scale calculus on finite dimensional Banach spaces.

Remark 2.2.1
.

The classical notion of manifolds (with boundary and corners)
is the class of M-polyfolds whose local models use finite dimensional Banach spaces
E
. In that case scale calculus coincides with classical calculus and retracts are necessarily trivial by
[
HWZ
, Prop.2.1.2]
.

Thus the definition of an
orbifold
as the realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|
of an étale proper groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
is the special case of Definition
2.1.1
(i) in which the object space
X
and morphism space
{\bf X}
are finite dimensional manifolds.
Indeed, sc-smoothness on finite dimensional manifolds is classical smoothness by
[
HWZ
, Prop.1.2.1]
),
the étale property implies that unit, inverse, and composition are sc-diffeomorphisms by Lemma
3.1.5
,
and the properness condition on locally compact object spaces is equivalent to the classical condition of
s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
being proper by Lemma
3.2.5
.

Similarly, the definition of an orbibundle as the realization
|{\mathcal{P}}|:|{\mathcal{W}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
of a smooth functor between étale proper groupoids is the special case of Definition
2.1.1
(ii) where
W\to X
is a finite rank vector bundle over a manifold. Here finite rank bundles are the special case of the strong bundle notion below for which the fiber ambient space
F
is finite dimensional, and hence a smooth family of projections on
F
has images of constant dimension.

In this special case of an orbibundle
|{\mathcal{P}}|:|{\mathcal{W}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
, the sc-Fredholm sections in Definition
2.1.1
(iii), as specified below, are precisely the classically smooth sections.

{\Diamond}

Back to trivial isotropy but general M-polyfolds, a
strong bundle
, as defined in
[
HWZ
, Def.2.6.5]
, is a sc-smooth surjection
P:W\to X
between M-polyfolds with linear structures on each fiber
W_{x}=P^{-1}(x)
for
x\in X
, and an equivalence class of compatible strong bundle charts.
Here the notion of ‘strong’ encodes a subbundle
W\supset W[1]\to X
equipped with another topology and sc-smooth structure such that the fiber inclusions
W[1]_{x}\hookrightarrow W_{x}
are dense and compact in the following sense:
Local trivializations of
W
are of the form
\bigcup_{u\in O}{\rm im\,}\Gamma(u)\to O
, where
O=r(U)\subset E
is a retract as above and
\Gamma(u):F\to F
is a family of projections on another Banach space
F
.
Scale-smoothness of
(u,h)\mapsto\Gamma(u)h
is required with respect to the product sc-structure on
E\times F
, but also with respect to a shifted sc-structure on
E\times F_{1}
, where
F_{1}\subset F
is a compact dense embedding of another Banach space (part of the sc-structure on
F
).
In this local trivialization,
W[1]\subset W
is the subset
\bigcup_{u\in O}\Gamma(u)F_{1}
, equipped with the topology induced by its inclusion in
E\times F_{1}
.

Local trivializations are compatible if their transition maps are strong bundle maps. Here a sc-smooth map
\Phi:W\to W^{\prime}
between two strong bundles (for example local models as above) is called a
strong bundle map
17
17

17

This notion generalizes
[
HWZ
, Def.2.6.1]
as described on
[
HWZ
, p.67]
.
if it covers a sc-smooth map on the base, is linear on fibers, and restricts to a sc-smooth map
W[1]\to W^{\prime}[1]
.

Finally, a
sc-Fredholm section

f:X\to W
of a strong bundle over an M-polyfold as in
[
HWZ
, Def.3.1.16]
is a sc-smooth map satisfying
P\circ f={\rm id}_{X}
and sc-Fredholm properties which encode elliptic regularity and a nonlinear contraction property
[
HWZ
, Def.3.1.6, 3.1.7, 3.1.11]
. The latter is a stronger condition than the classical notion of the linearized operator being Fredholm,
and is crucial to ensure an implicit function theorem as shown in
[
FWZ
]
– since the differentials of a sc-smooth section with Fredholm linearizations may not vary continuously with the basepoint.

Now an
M-polyfold model for a compact moduli space

{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
(which we generalize to allow for nontrivial isotropy in Definition
5.1.2
) would consist of a sc-Fredholm section
f:X\to W
and a homeomorphism
\psi:f^{-1}(0)\to{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
. The regularization of such (relatively simple) moduli spaces is achieved in
[
HWZ
, §5.3]
by showing that different transverse perturbations of
f
have cobordant solution spaces
(f-s)^{-1}(0)\sim(f-s^{\prime})^{-1}(0)
when perturbations are restricted to compactness-controlled (see §
3.5
) sc
+
-sections
s,s^{\prime}:X\to W
.
This serves as introduction to the last crucial scale calculus notion: An
sc
+
-section
of a strong bundle
W\to X
is a section that is sc-smooth
X\to W[1]
relative to the shifted sc-structure on the dense subbundle
W[1]\subset W
. Since the fibers of
W[1]
embed compactly into
W
, these sections can be viewed as compact perturbations of the sc-Fredholm section
f
. In particular, adding them does not affect the Fredholm index by
[
HWZ
, Thm.3.1.18]
.

2.3.
Orientation Conventions

The goal of this subsection (which can be omitted on first reading) is to spell out the notion of an orientation in
[
HWZ
, Def.12.5.4]
for a sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
over an ep-groupoid in sufficient detail to make sense of the constructions in this paper.
This will mainly be a matter of constructing the orientation bundle over the M-polyfold of objects
X
, which will only be possible over a dense subset of so-called “smooth points”:

M-polyfolds inherit from their sc-smooth structure a filtration
X=X_{0}\supset X_{1}\supset\ldots\supset X_{\infty}=\bigcap_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}X_{i}
of subsets with their own (metrizable, paracompact) topology
[
HWZ
, Thm.2.3.10]
. Viewed as subsets, each of them are dense in
X

[
HWZ
, p.289]
.
18
18

18

This density holds by definition
[
HWZ
, Def.1.1.1]
for
X={\mathbb{E}}=(E_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
a sc-Banach space. In a local chart
X\supset O\simeq r(U)
it holds since every
x\in O
is an image
x=r(y)
of a map
r:U\to U
on an open subset
U\subset E_{0}
of a sc-Banach space, where
r
is sc-continuous in the sense that
r:U\cap E_{m}\to U\cap E_{m}
is continuous for all
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
Now an approximating sequence
E_{m}\ni y_{i}\to y
lies in
U
for sufficiently large
i
and yields an approximation
E_{m}\cap r(U)\ni r(y_{i})\to r(y)=x
.

The same is true for any polyfold – the realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|
of an ep-groupoid – since the equivalence relation by sc-diffeomorphisms preserves the filtration on the object set, resulting in a filtration
|{\mathcal{X}}|=|X_{0}|\supset|X_{1}|\supset\ldots\supset|X_{\infty}|=\bigcap_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}|X_{i}|
of dense subsets.

Now the
orientation bundle
of a sc-Fredholm section
f:X\to W
on object level – where
W
is a strong bundle over an M-polyfold
X
– is constructed in
[
HWZ
, §6, §12.5]
as a local homeomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}_{f}\to X_{\infty}
between Hausdorff spaces, where the fiber of
{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
over
x\in X_{\infty}
consists of the two possible orientations of the line bundle
{\rm DET}(f,x)\to{\rm LIN}(f,x)
over a convex set of admissible linearizations of
f
at
x

[
HWZ
, Def.6.2.1]
.

Here the fiber of
{\rm DET}(f,x)
over each admissible linearization
S:{\rm T}_{x}X\to W_{x}
is the classical determinant line
\det(S)=\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\ker S\otimes\bigl(\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\bigl(\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle W_{x}\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}S$}}\bigr)\bigr)^{*}
.
When
f(x)=0
then the canonical linearization
S={\rm D}f(x):{\rm T}_{x}X\to W_{x}
is admissible, and all other admissible linearizations differ by a compact operator (see the discussion of sc
+
-operators after
[
HWZ
, Def.1.1.5]
).
When
f(x)\neq 0
then all admissible linearizations can be expressed as canonical linearizations
S={\rm D}(f-s)(x):{\rm T}_{x}X\to W_{x}
for some nonlinear sc
+
-section
s:X\to W
with
s(x)=f(x)
(see
[
FFGW
, Def.6.1.8]
for the notion of sc
+
-section).

Next, each
{\rm DET}(f,x)
is given the structure of a line bundle by viewing it as the restriction of the classical determinant line bundle to a subset of the Fredholm operators
{\rm T}_{x}X\to W_{x}
. From the abundance of more or less natural conventions for topologizing this determinant bundle (as discussed in
[
Z
]
) we use the conventions in
[
HWZ
, Prop.6.5.8]
:
Given a Fredholm operator
T:E\to F
between Banach spaces
E,F
, we can topologize the determinant line bundle
\bigcup_{\|S-T\|<{\varepsilon}}\det(S)
in a neighbourhood of
T
as follows:
The choice of any good left-projection
P:F\to F
– satisfying in particular
{\rm im\,}PT={\rm im\,}P
, see
[
HWZ
, Def.6.4.3]
– induces an exact sequence in
[
HWZ
, (6.9)]
,

E_{(T,P)}\;:\quad 0\to\ker(T)\to\ker(PT)\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle F\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}PT$}}\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle F\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}T$}}\to 0.

Then the isomorphism
\Phi_{E}
of
[
HWZ
, Lemma 6.3.3]
applied to
E=E_{(T,P)}
yields an isomorphism
{\gamma}_{T}^{P}:\det(T)\to\det(PT)
as in
[
HWZ
, (6.12)]
.
This extends to isomorphisms
{\gamma}_{S}^{P}:\det(S)\to\det(PS)
for
\|S-T\|\leq{\varepsilon}
guaranteed by
[
HWZ
, Prop.6.5.1 (1)]
, and these are used to pull back the local determinant bundle structure of
\bigcup_{\|S-T\|<{\varepsilon}}\{S\}\times\det(PS)
constructed in
[
HWZ
, Prop.6.5.4]
. Independence of choices and continuity of transition maps is proven in
[
HWZ
, §6.4-5]
.
19
19

19

Here we must take into account the fact that the sign in
[
HWZ
, Prop.6.4.15]
needs to be corrected to
\det(c_{1},...,c_{m},-r_{1},...,-r_{l})
.
This correction makes this proposition coherent with the rest of
[
HWZ
, §6.4]
as well as
[
MW2
, (8.1.9)]
.

This defines the orientation bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
as a set with a 2-to-1 map to
X_{\infty}
. Topologizing it requires a notion of continuous extension of orientations when the basepoint
x\in X_{\infty}
varies, which is given in
[
HWZ
, Thm.6.6.11]
.

Now an
orientation
of a sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
over an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
is a continuous section
{\mathfrak{o}}:X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{f},x\mapsto{\mathfrak{o}}_{x}
of the orientation bundle over the smooth objects
X_{\infty}
, that is compatible with the morphisms of
{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense that
\phi_{*}{\mathfrak{o}}_{s(\phi)}={\mathfrak{o}}_{t(\phi)}
for all
\phi\in{\bf X}
.
Here
\phi_{*}
is the second of the following conventions worth spelling out:

General isomorphism convention:

Any commutative diagram

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}}

of Fredholm operators
S:E\to F,S^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to F^{\prime}
and isomorphisms
I:E\to E^{\prime}
,
J:F\to F^{\prime}

induces a commutative diagram for each choice of good left-projection
P:F\to F
for
S

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\ker(PS)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle F\!
/
\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}PS
\scriptstyle{J}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Id}-P}
\scriptstyle F\!
/
\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}S
\scriptstyle{J}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker(S^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker(P^{\prime}S^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle F^{\prime}\!
/
\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}P^{\prime}S^{\prime}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Id}-P^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle F^{\prime}\!
/
\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}S^{\prime}
\textstyle{0}

Here
P^{\prime}:=JPJ^{-1}
is the corresponding good left-projection for
S^{\prime}
, which combines the exact sequences
E_{(S,P)}
and
E_{(S^{\prime},P^{\prime})}
of type
[
HWZ
, (6.9)]
into a diagram of type
[
HWZ
, (6.5)]
.
Now
[
HWZ
, Lemma 6.3.4]
induces a natural commutative diagram intertwining the isomorphisms
\gamma^{P}_{S}:\det(S)\to\det(PS)
and
\gamma^{P^{\prime}}_{S^{\prime}}:\det(S^{\prime})\to\det(P^{\prime}S^{\prime})
used in the construction of the determinant bundles near
S
and
S^{\prime}
.
We denote the natural isomorphism
\det(S)\to\det(S^{\prime})
in this diagram by

\displaystyle(I,J)_{*}:\;\det(S)

\displaystyle\to\det(S^{\prime}),\;\;

\displaystyle(e_{1}\wedge\ldots e_{k})\otimes(f_{1}\wedge\ldots f_{c})^{*}

\displaystyle\mapsto(I(e_{1})\wedge\ldots I(e_{k}))\otimes(J(f_{1})\wedge\ldots J(f_{c}))^{*}.

Note that this map is well defined since we assumed
JS=S^{\prime}I
, and hence
I
resp.
J
restrict to isomorphisms between kernels resp. cokernels of the two Fredholm operators.

Morphism convention:

Since source and target maps
{\bf X}\to X
are local sc-diffeomorphisms, every morphism
\phi\in{\bf X}
extends to a local sc-diffeomorphism
\phi:U_{x}\to U_{y}
between neighbourhoods
U_{x},U_{y}\subset X
of
x=s(\phi)
and
y=t(\phi)
as in
[
HWZ
, §7.1]
. Its differential
{\rm d}\phi(x):{\rm T}_{x}X\to{\rm T}_{y}X
is a sc-isomorphism for smooth points
x\in X_{\infty}
.
At smooth points,
\phi
also lifts to a strong bundle isomorphism
\mu(\phi,\cdot):W|_{U_{x}}\to W|_{U_{y}}
, and functoriality of
f
implies
f\circ\phi=\mu(\phi,f)
on
U_{x}
.
Now we can combine these maps to obtain a bijection between admissible linearizations

\phi_{*}:{\rm LIN}(f,x)\to{\rm LIN}(f,y),S\mapsto\mu(\phi,\cdot)\circ S\circ({\rm d}\phi(x))^{-1}.

Since these linearizations satisfy the commuting diagram relation
\mu(\phi,\cdot)\circ S=\phi_{*}S\circ{\rm d}\phi(x)
with isomorphisms
I={\rm d}\phi(x)
between their domains and
J=\mu(\phi,\cdot)
between their target spaces, we can use the general isomorphism convention to lift
\phi_{*}
to a bundle isomorphism
\phi_{*}:{\rm DET}(f,x)\to{\rm DET}(f,y)
given by
(I,J)_{*}:\det(S)\to\det(\phi_{*}S)
on each fiber.
This in turn induces a bijection of orientations
\phi_{*}:({\mathcal{O}}_{f})_{x}\to({\mathcal{O}}_{f})_{y}
given by restricting the isomorphisms
I={\rm d}\phi(x)
and
J:=\mu(\phi,\cdot)
to the kernels and cokernels of the linearizations.

Equivalence convention:

If
\psi:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
is an equivalence of ep-groupoids
[
HWZ
, Def.10.1.1]
and
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is sc-Fredholm, then
any orientation of
f
has a natural pullback to an orientation of the pullback section
\widetilde{f}:=\psi^{*}f:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}
as follows:

The section
\widetilde{f}
satisfies
\Psi\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ\psi
by Lemma
3.4.4
and is sc-Fredholm by Lemma
3.5.3
. Here the pullback bundle functor
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is a strong bundle equivalence, in particular restricts to isomorphisms between the fibers
\Psi_{y}:(\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}})_{y}\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\psi(y)}
, whereas
\psi
is a local sc-diffeomorphism, hence induces isomorphisms
{\rm d}\psi(y):{\rm T}_{y}Y\to{\rm T}_{\psi(y)}X
between the domains of the linearizations of
\widetilde{f}
and
f
.
For each
y\in Y
these induce a bijection between the sets of admissible linearizations

\psi^{*}:{\rm LIN}(f,\psi(y))\to{\rm LIN}(\widetilde{f},y),S\mapsto\Psi_{y}^{-1}\circ S\circ{\rm d}\psi(y).

Indeed, given a sc
+
-section
s
with
s(\psi(y))=f(\psi(y))
, its pullback
\widetilde{s}:=\psi^{*}s
is a sc
+
-section with
\widetilde{s}(y)=\widetilde{f}(y)
, and the corresponding linearizations satisfy

\Psi_{y}\circ{\rm D}_{y}(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})={\rm D}_{\psi(y)}(f-s)\circ{\rm d}\psi(y).

Since this pullback of linearizations satisfies the commuting diagram relation
\Psi_{y}\circ\psi^{*}S=S\circ{\rm d}\psi(y)

with isomorphisms
I={\rm d}\psi(y)
between their domains and
J=\Psi_{y}
between their target spaces, the general isomorphism convention lifts
\psi^{*}
to a bundle isomorphism
\psi^{*}:{\rm DET}(f,\psi(y))\to{\rm DET}(\widetilde{f},y)
given by
{(I,J)_{*}}^{-1}:\det(S)\to\det(\psi^{*}S)
on each fiber.
This then induces a bijection of orientations
\psi^{*}:({\mathcal{O}}_{f})_{\psi(y)}\to({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{f}})_{y}
given by restricting the isomorphisms
I={\rm d}\psi(y)
and
J=\Psi_{y}
to the kernels and cokernels of the linearizations.

Now any orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}:X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
of
f
induces a section

\psi^{*}{\mathfrak{o}}:=\psi^{*}\circ{\mathfrak{o}}\circ\psi:Y_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{f}},

which is an orientation of
\widetilde{f}
by
[
HWZ
, Thm.12.5.7 (2)]
– called the pullback orientation.

Solution set convention:

Given an oriented sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
and a suitably transverse – possibly multi-valued – perturbation of
f
, the perturbed solution set inherits a natural orientation.
In the ep-groupoid context this is formulated precisely and proven in
[
HWZ
, §15.4]
, with local orientations constructed from the following single-valued, trivial isotropy case in
[
HWZ
, Thm.6.6.18]
20
20

20

Here we generalize the conventions of
[
HWZ
, §6.6]
as indicated in the beginning of
[
HWZ
, §15.4]
.
:

Suppose
f:X\to W
is an oriented sc-Fredholm section over an M-polyfold,
s:X\to W
is a sc
+
-section, and
x\in(f-s)^{-1}(0)\subset X_{\infty}
is a perturbed solution.
21
21

21

The inclusion of perturbed solution sets in
X_{\infty}
is guaranteed by the regularizing property of sc-Fredholm sections, which is preserved by sc
+
-perturbations.
If
f-s
is transverse at
x
, that is if the linearization
{\rm D}_{x}(f-s):{\rm T}_{x}X\to W_{x}
is surjective, then
\ker{\rm D}_{x}(f-s)
inherits a natural orientation since
S:={\rm D}_{x}(f-s)\in{\rm LIN}(f,x)
is an admissible linearization and so the given orientation of

\det(S)=\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\ker S\otimes(\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle W_{x}\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle{\rm im\,}S$}})^{*}\cong\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\ker{\rm D}_{x}(f-s)

transfers to the kernel by canonical identification.
If
(f-s)^{-1}(0)
also inherits a manifold structure (which requires transversality at all perturbed solutions and good or general position for solutions at the boundary), then the induced pointwise orientations of its tangent spaces
{\rm T}_{x}(f-s)^{-1}(0)=\ker{\rm D}_{x}(f-s)
form a continuous orientation of the tangent bundle since the orientation propagation that topologizes
{\mathcal{O}}_{f}\to X_{\infty}
in
[
HWZ
, Thm.6.6.11]
agrees with classical parallel transport on the tangent bundle over
(f-s)^{-1}(0)
.

More precisely, let
\phi:[0,1]\to(f-s)^{-1}(0)
be a smooth path along which
f-s
is transverse (for boundary considerations see
[
HWZ
]
). Then the polyfold construction of orientation propagation
\phi_{*}:({\mathcal{O}}_{f})_{\phi(0)}\to({\mathcal{O}}_{f})_{\phi(1)}
in
[
HWZ
, Lemma 6.6.4-6]
simplifies:

-

We can choose the sc
+
-section
[0,1]\times X\to W,(t,x)\mapsto s(x)
since
s(\phi(t))=f(\phi(t))
.

-

The section
F:[0,1]\times X\to W,(t,x)\mapsto f(x)-s(x)
is transverse along the graph of
\phi
.

-

On the line bundle
\Lambda^{\rm max}\,L=\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}\{t\}\times\Lambda^{\rm max}\,L_{t}
with
L_{t}=\ker{\rm D}_{\phi(t)}(f-s)
the parallel transport is the classical orientation propagation along the path
\phi:[0,1]\to(f-s)^{-1}(0)
.

-

The identification of
\det(\ker{\rm D}_{\phi(t)}(f-s))\to\Lambda^{\rm max}\,L_{t}\otimes(\Lambda^{\rm max}\,{\mathbb{R}}^{k})^{*}=\Lambda^{\rm max}\,L_{t}
in
[
HWZ
, (6.28)]
is the identity map since
k=0
.

So the polyfold orientation propagation
\phi_{*}
in this setting agrees with classical orientation propagation on
(f-s)^{-1}(0)
, as claimed. Thus an orientation of
f
induces an orientation of any appropriately transverse perturbed solution set
(f-s)^{-1}(0)
.

Remark 2.3.1
.

The orientation conventions for equivalences and solution sets interact as follows:
Consider a sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
with orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}:X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
.
Then any local perturbed solution set
(f-s)^{-1}(0)\cap U
inherits the structure of an oriented manifold when
s:X\to W
is a (not necessarily functorial) sc
+
-section such that
(f-s)|_{U}
is transverse over an open subset
U\subset X
.

Now consider an equivalence of ep-groupoids
\psi:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
, and the pullback section
\widetilde{f}:=\psi^{*}f:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}
with pullback orientation
\psi^{*}{\mathfrak{o}}=\psi^{*}\circ{\mathfrak{o}}\circ\psi:Y_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{f}}
. Suppose that the above perturbation is given on a set
U=\psi(\widetilde{U})\subset V
that is the image of a sufficiently small open subset
\widetilde{U}\subset Y
on which
\psi
restricts to a sc-diffeomorphism. Then
\widetilde{s}:=\psi^{*}s:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}
is a sc
+
-section with
(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})|_{\widetilde{U}}
transverse, and
\psi
restricts to an diffeomorphism between the manifolds
(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})^{-1}(0)\cap\widetilde{U}\to(f-s)^{-1}(0)\cap U
that preserves orientations.

Indeed, the pullback construction guarantees that
\psi
maps solutions

y\in(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})^{-1}(0)\cap\widetilde{U}\subset Y_{\infty}

to solutions

x:=\psi(y)\in(f-s)^{-1}(0)\cap U\subset X_{\infty},

sc-diffeomorphisms restrict to diffeomorphisms in finite dimensions, and its linearization
{\rm d}\psi(y):{\rm T}_{y}(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})^{-1}(0)\to{\rm T}_{x}(f-s)^{-1}(0)
is compatible with the orientations induced by
\psi^{*}{\mathfrak{o}}
and
{\mathfrak{o}}
as follows:

In the absence of cokernels,
{\rm T}_{x}(f-s)^{-1}(0)=\ker S
is oriented by the natural identification
\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\ker S=\det(S)
with the determinant of the linearization
S:={\rm D}_{x}(f-s)\in{\rm LIN}(f,{\psi(y)})
, which is oriented by
{\mathfrak{o}}(x)
.
Similarly,
{\rm T}_{y}(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})^{-1}(0)=\ker\psi^{*}S
is oriented by the natural identification
\Lambda^{\rm max}\,\ker\psi^{*}S=\det(\psi^{*}S)
with the determinant of the linearization
\psi^{*}S=\Psi_{y}^{-1}\circ S\circ{\rm d}\psi(y)\in{\rm LIN}(\widetilde{f},y)
, which is oriented by
(\psi^{*}{\mathfrak{o}})(y)=\psi^{*}({\mathfrak{o}}(\psi(y)))
.
Here, due to the trivial cokernel,
\psi^{*}:({\mathcal{O}}_{f})_{\psi(y)}\to({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{f}})_{y}
is given by restricting the isomorphism
{\rm d}\psi(y)
to the kernels. So the polyfold-theoretic identification
\psi^{*}
between the orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}(x)
of
{\rm T}_{x}(f-s)^{-1}(0)
and the orientation
(\psi^{*}{\mathfrak{o}})(y)=\psi^{*}({\mathfrak{o}}(x))
of
{\rm T}_{y}(\widetilde{f}-\widetilde{s})^{-1}(0)
coincides with the differential-geometric identification induced by
{\rm d}\psi(y)
.

{\Diamond}

Product convention:
Given
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
sc-Fredholm with orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}:X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
, its constant extension
\widehat{f}:[0,1]\times{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,1]\times{\mathcal{W}},(t,x)\mapsto(t,f(x))
inherits a natural orientation
\widehat{\mathfrak{o}}:[0,1]\times X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{f}}
specified in
[
HWZ
, Rmk.6.6.19]
as follows:

The map on linearizations
{\rm LIN}(f,x)\to{\rm LIN}(\widehat{f},(t,x)),S\mapsto S\Pi
given by composition with the projection
\Pi:{\rm T}_{(t,x)}[0,1]\times X={\mathbb{R}}\times{\rm T}_{x}X\to{\rm T}_{x}X
lifts to a bundle map
{\rm DET}(f,x)\to{\rm DET}(\widehat{f},(t,x))
given on each fiber by

\det(S)\to\det(S\Pi),(a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}\mapsto(1\wedge a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*},

where
1\in{\mathbb{R}}={\rm T}_{t}[0,1]
represents the standard orientation of the interval
[0,1]
.

Boundary convention:
Given an oriented sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
and a boundary stratum
Y\subset\partial X
, the restriction
f|_{Y}
inherits a natural orientation as follows:

Each
y\in Y_{\infty}
, considered as boundary point of
X
has a local model
(0,0)\in r(U)
, where
r:U\to U
is a sc-smooth retraction on an open subset
U\subset[0,\infty)\times E
.
Here tameness guarantees
U_{\partial}:=U\cap\{0\}\times E=\{u\in U\,|\,d_{U}(u)=1\}
to be
r
-invariant, inducing a local model
(0,0)\in r(U_{\partial})
for
y\in Y
with
{\rm T}_{y}Y\simeq{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r(\{0\}\times E)\subset{\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r\simeq{\rm T}_{y}X
.
To find an outward vector
\nu\in{\rm T}_{y}X
that splits
{\rm T}_{y}X={\mathbb{R}}\nu\oplus{\rm T}_{y}Y
we need the second tameness condition: This guarantees a subspace
A\subset\{0\}\times E
such that
{\mathbb{R}}\times E={\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r\oplus A
, and hence we can define
0\neq\nu\in{\rm T}_{y}X\simeq{\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r
to be the unique vector in the decomposition
(-1,0)=\nu+a
with
a\in A
. Here a different choice of complement
A^{\prime}\neq A
induces another vector
\nu^{\prime}\in{\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r
with
\nu^{\prime}-\nu\in{\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r\cap\{0\}\times E\simeq{\rm T}_{y}Y
.

To restrict the orientation of
f
to an orientation of
f|_{Y}
at the point
y\in Y
, choose a linearization
S={\rm D}_{y}(f-s)\in{\rm LIN}(f,y)
with
{\rm D}_{y}(f-s)(-\nu)=0
using Lemma
2.3.3
below and the fact that the direct sum
{\mathbb{R}}\times E={\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r\oplus A
is in fact a sc-splitting, so that
(-1,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times E_{\infty}
yields
-\nu=(1,a)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times E_{\infty}
.
This linearization induces a restricted linearization
S_{\partial}:={\rm D}_{y}(f-s)|_{{\rm T}_{y}Y}\in{\rm LIN}(f|_{Y},y)
with
{\mathbb{R}}\nu\oplus\ker S_{\partial}=\ker S
and
{\rm im\,}S_{\partial}={\rm im\,}S
, so that we can define an isomorphism
\det S_{\partial}\to\det S
by mapping
(a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}
to
(\nu\wedge a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}
.

A different choice of outward vector
\nu^{\prime}\in\nu+{\rm T}_{y}Y
gives rise to a continuous family
\nu_{\tau}:=\nu+\tau(\nu^{\prime}-\nu)\in({\rm T}_{y}X)_{\infty}
and the construction of Lemma
2.3.3
yields a continuous family of sc
+
-sections
s_{\tau}
with
s_{\tau}|_{Y}
independent of
\tau\in[0,1]
and
{\rm D}_{y}(f-s_{\tau})(\nu_{\tau})=0
.
So we obtain a continuous family of isomorphism
\det S_{\partial}\to\det S_{\tau}
mapping

(a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}

to

(\nu_{\tau}\wedge a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*},

and can conclude that the induced (inverse) map from orientations of
f
to orientations of
f|_{Y}
at
y
is independent of choices – except for the crucial ordering and outward convention that we fixed here.
This convention is moreover compatible with the boundary orientation convention in
[
HWZ
, Def.9.3.8]
for solution sets (resulting from multi-valued perturbations).

An analogous extension construction for the stabilizations in
[
HWZ
, Lemma 6.6.4-6]
shows that this pointwise convention is compatible with orientation propagation, thus induces a well defined orientation of
f|_{Y}
.

Remark 2.3.2
.

The orientation conventions for products and boundary restrictions interact as follows:
Given a sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
with orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}:X_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{f}
, the restrictions of its constant extension
\widehat{f}:[0,1]\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
to the boundary strata
{\mathcal{X}}\to\{i\}\cong{\mathcal{X}}
for
i=0,1
are

\widehat{f}|_{\{0\}\times{\mathcal{X}}}\cong f
with orientation
-{\mathfrak{o}}
and
\widehat{f}|_{\{1\}\times{\mathcal{X}}}\cong f
with orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}
.
This identification results from the isomorphisms

\det(S)\to\det(S\Pi=\widehat{S})
and
\det(\widehat{S})\to\det(\widehat{S}_{\partial}=S)

mapping

(a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}\mapsto(1\wedge a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}

and

(\nu\wedge a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}\mapsto(a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{c})^{*}

with outward vector
\nu=-1
for
i=0\in\partial[0,1]
and outward vector
\nu=1
for
i=1\in\partial[0,1]
.

{\Diamond}

To state and prove existence of the sc
+
-sections used in the boundary convention we assume familiarity with
[
HWZ
, ch.1-2]
.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Consider a strong bundle retraction

R:U\times F\to U\times F,(u,f)\mapsto(r(u),{\Gamma}(u)f)

covering a sc-smooth retraction
r:U\to U
on an open neighbourhood
U\subset[0,1)\times E
of
(0,0)=r(0,0)
, and a sc-smooth section of this local bundle given by
f:r(U)\to F
satisfying
f(u)\in{\Gamma}(u)F
.
Given any
\nu=(1,a)\in{\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r\cap{\mathbb{R}}\times E_{\infty}
there exists a sc
+
-section
s
of this local bundle such that
s(0,0)=f(0,0)
and
{\rm D}_{(0,0)}(f-s)(\nu)=0
.

Proof.

Note that we have
\nu=\dot{\gamma}(0)
for the sc-smooth path
\gamma:[0,{\varepsilon})\to r(U),t\mapsto r(t\nu)
as in
[
HWZ
, Lemma 2.4.9]
for some
{\varepsilon}>0
. Since
[0,{\varepsilon})
consists of smooth points and sc-continuous maps preserve sc-levels, this path takes values in the smooth points,
\gamma(t)=r(t\nu)\in[0,1)\times E_{\infty}
.
Moreover, sc-smoothness with this finite dimensional domain is equivalent to smoothness as map
[0,{\varepsilon})\to[0,1)\times E_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
So composing this path with the sc-smooth map
f
yields a path
[0,{\varepsilon})\to F_{\infty},t\mapsto f(\gamma(t))
that is sc-smooth w.r.t. any shift of the sc-structure on
F
.

We now construct a map
s:r(U)\to F
by
s(t,e):={\Gamma}(t,e)f(\gamma(t))
for
(t,e)\in r(U)
.
This is a section since
s(u)\in{\Gamma}(u)F
. It is sc-smooth and sc
+
since its extension
\widetilde{s}:U\to F,u\mapsto s(r(u))
can be viewed for each
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
as the composition of sc-smooth maps
r:U\to U
, the linear map
U\to U\times{\mathbb{R}},(t,e)\mapsto((t,e),t)
,
the map
U\times{\mathbb{R}}\to U\times F_{l},(u,t)\mapsto(u,f(\gamma(t)))
, and
U\times F_{l}\to F_{l},(u,f)\mapsto\Gamma(u)f
.
It satisfies the base point condition

s(0,0)={\Gamma}(0,0)f(\gamma(0))={\Gamma}(0,0)f(0,0)=f(0,0),

and we can compute with
g(t):={{\rm pr}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma(t))

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{(0,0)}(f-s)(\nu)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\big|_{t=0}(f-s)(\gamma(t))

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\tfrac{f(\gamma(t))-s(\gamma(t))}{t}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}{\Gamma}(\gamma(t))\tfrac{f(\gamma(t))-f(\gamma(g(t)))}{t}=0.

To prove the last step, we first claim that
\gamma(t)=r(t\nu)=t\nu+O(t^{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times E_{0}
and hence
g(t)=t+O(t^{2})
.
To check this note that
r:U\cap{\mathbb{R}}\times E_{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times E_{0}
is continuously differentiable so that
r(t\nu)=r(0,0)+t{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r(\nu)+O(t^{2})
. Here
r(0,0)=(0,0)
and
{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r(\nu)=\nu
follow from
r\in r(U)
resp.
\nu\in{\rm im\,}{\rm D}_{(0,0)}r
and the retraction property
r\circ r=r
.
Next, sc-smoothness of
f\circ{\gamma}
includes continuous differentiability as map
[0,\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}]\to F_{0}
with uniformly bounded derivative. Thus we can estimate for
t\to 0

t^{-1}\|f(\gamma(t))-f(\gamma(g(t)))\|_{F_{0}}\;\leq\;t^{-1}C|t-g(t)|\;=\;Ct^{-1}O(t^{2})=O(t)\;\to\;0.

Finally, continuity of
\Gamma:U\times F_{0}\to F_{0}
confirms the last step in
{\rm D}_{(0,0)}(f-s)(\nu)=0
.
∎

3.
Étale categories and groupoid completions

This section develops the general theory of étale categories in order to have a suitable language in which to discuss the structure and properties of the groupoids
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
that appear in Theorem
2.1.2
.
After a general introduction to étale categories in §
3.1
,
§
3.2
defines the notion of preuniformizer, generalizing the local uniformizers considered in
[
HWZ
]
. Lemma
3.2.5
gives conditions that imply the properness of a groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
and hence, by Corollary
3.2.6
, the Hausdorffness of
|{\mathcal{X}}|
.
This subsection also develops the notion of the groupoid completion of an étale category, which is used to construct
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
. Then §
3.3
discusses the properties
of group actions on étale categories such as the action of
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
in Theorem
2.1.2
.
The next subsection explains the basic properties of bundles and their sections in more detail, with special mention of the properties of strong bundles in the polyfold setting, that is in étale class (d). Globally structured multisections are defined in Definition
3.4.5
, and Proposition
3.4.11
shows that such sections are structurable in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.6]
.
Finally,
§
3.5
concentrates on the polyfold case, summarizing the notions required for ensuring the compactness of perturbed zero sets.

3.1.
Étale categories

Throughout this paper we will work with topological categories. Thus we consider small categories
{\mathcal{B}}
whose sets

B:={\rm Obj}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\bf B}:={\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{B}}}
of objects and morphisms are equipped with topologies such that the source and target maps
s,t:{\bf B}\to B
as well as unit
u:B\to{\bf B}
and composition

c:\{(m,m^{\prime})\in{\bf B}\times{\bf B}\,|\,t(m)=s(m^{\prime})\}\to{\bf B},\quad(m,m^{\prime})\mapsto m\circ m^{\prime},

are continuous. Further, any functor between topological categories is assumed continuous on object and morphism spaces.
When working with a groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})
we also assume that the inverse operation
i:{\bf X}\to{\bf X}
is continuous, i.e.
{\mathcal{X}}
is a topological groupoid.
For notational clarity we reserve the letter
{\mathcal{X}}
to denote étale groupoids throughout this paper.
In that case the relation

x_{1}\sim_{\mathcal{X}}x_{2}\quad:\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{2})\neq\emptyset

is an equivalence relation, and this defines the
realization

|{\mathcal{X}}|:=\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle X\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{X}}$}}
as topological space, equipped with the quotient topology.
Here we denote the set of morphisms between subsets
U_{1},U_{2}\subset X
, for example
U_{i}=\{x_{i}\}
, by

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(U_{1},U_{2})\,:=\;s^{-1}(U_{1})\cap t^{-1}(U_{2})\;\subset\;{\bf X}.

For more general categories
{\mathcal{B}}
which may not have inverses,
the realization
|{\mathcal{B}}|:=\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle B\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
is defined by the equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
on
B
that is generated by the morphisms.
Again, we equip
|{\mathcal{B}}|
with the quotient topology such that the quotient map is continuous,

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\,:\;B\;\to\;|{\mathcal{B}}|=\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle B\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}},\qquad x\;\mapsto\;|x|:=[x].

For any subset
V\subset B
we denote its image in the realization by
|V|:=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(V)\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
, and note that it carries two natural topologies which may not coincide
22
22

22

For example, consider
{\mathcal{B}}
with objects
B=[0,1]\times\{\pm 1\}
and non-identity morphisms

(x,-1)\mapsto(x,+1)
and
V=([0,1/2]\times\{-1\})\cup((1/2,1]\times\{1\})\subset B
.
Then
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(V)=|{\mathcal{B}}|
is homeomorphic to the interval
[0,1]
, while
\scriptstyle V\!
/
\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
is disconnected in the quotient topology.
: the relative topology from the inclusion into
|{\mathcal{B}}|
and the quotient topology from the identification
|V|\simeq\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle V\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
.
However, when
V\subset B
is open or saturated (that is
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(V))=V
) then the topologies agree by Lemma
3.1.7
.

In the following, we will always work with categories that are étale in the sense of the following Definition
3.1.2
. For that purpose we fix a class of metrizable topological spaces and a corresponding class of local homeomorphisms that is closed under compositions and taking local inverses. In particular, all our results will hold for any of the following classes of étale spaces and étale maps:

(a)

metrizable topological spaces and local homeomorphisms;

(b)

topological manifolds (assumed to be finite dimensional and second countable – possibly with specified boundary and corner structure, see below) and local homeomorphisms;

(c)

smooth manifolds (assumed to be finite dimensional and second countable – possibly with boundary and corners, see below) and local diffeomorphisms;

(d)

M-polyfolds and local sc-diffeomorphisms (see §
2.2
).

Unless otherwise specified, we allow manifolds and M-polyfolds to have boundary and corners, arising from local models in
[0,\infty)^{k}\times E
as in e.g.
[
FFGW
, §5.3]
, giving rise to a degeneracy index
d_{X}:X\to{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
The corresponding étale maps are required to restrict to local homeomorphisms resp. diffeomorphisms on each
boundary and corner stratum
– that is connected components of
\{x\in X\,|\,d_{X}(x)=\ell\}
for fixed
\ell\geq 1
.

Remark 3.1.1
.

(i)

A map
\phi:X\to Y
is called a
local homeomorphism (or diffeomorphism)
if for each
x\in X
there exist open neighbourhoods
23
23

23

We always assume that neighbourhoods are open sets; sometimes, for clarity, we mention this explicitly.

N_{x}\subset X
of
x
and
N_{y}\subset Y
of
y:=\phi(x)
so that the restriction
\phi|_{N_{x}}:N_{x}\to N_{y}
is a homeomorphism (or diffeomorphism) with respect to the induced topology (or smooth structure) on the open subsets
N_{x},N_{y}
.

(ii)

This convention implies that every étale map is open.
Indeed, given an open subset
U\subset X
, we check that its image
\phi(U)\subset Y
is open by finding for each image
y=\phi(x)
of some
x\in U
an open neighbourhood
N^{\prime}_{y}\subset Y
contained in
\phi(U)
.
We obtain
N^{\prime}_{y}:=\phi(N_{x}\cap U)
from a choice of open neighbourhoods
N_{x}\subset X
of
x
and
N_{y}\subset Y
of
y
so that
\phi|_{N_{x}}:N_{x}\to N_{y}
is a homeomorphism.
Here
N^{\prime}_{y}=\phi(N_{x}\cap U)
is open in
N_{y}
because it is the image under a homeomorphism of the open subset
N_{x}\cap U\subset N_{x}
. Finally, openness of
N^{\prime}_{y}\subset N_{y}
implies openness of
N^{\prime}_{y}\subset Y
since
N_{y}
is open.

(iii)

The topological categories associated to Kuranishi atlases or structures are almost never étale, as the local homeomorphism property of source and target maps rules out overlaps between Kuranishi charts whose domains have different dimension.
{\Diamond}

Once such a class is chosen, we will refer to
étale spaces
and
étale maps
as the spaces and maps in this class, and define the notion of étale category for any chosen class as follows.

Definition 3.1.2
.

A topological category
{\mathcal{B}}
is said to be
étale

w.r.t. a chosen class as above, if its object and morphism spaces
B,{\bf B}
are étale spaces and the structure maps
s,t,c,u
are étale maps.
If
{\mathcal{B}}
is a groupoid, then we require in addition that the inverse
i
is étale.

Remark 3.1.3
.

The requirement that composition be étale makes sense since each étale class is closed under fiber products in the following sense:
Three étale spaces
U,V,W
and two étale maps
t:U\to W,s:V\to W
yield a fiber product

U{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}V=\big\{(x,y)\in U\times V\ \big|\ t(x)=s(y)\big\}

that also is an étale space in this class. Further, the projections
U{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}V\to U
and
U{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}V\to V
are étale maps in the same class.

Indeed, a neighbourhood of
(x_{0},y_{0})\in U{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}V

consists of points
(x,y)
where
x\approx x_{0}
and
y=s\circ t^{-1}(x)
for a suitable local inverse
t^{-1}
to
t
.
A more detailed statement and proof including boundary and corner structures is
[
HWZ
, Prop.5.2.20]
for the polyfold case.

{\Diamond}

We will moreover use the following standard notions.

Definition 3.1.4
.

(i)

A functor
f:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
between étale categories is called
étale
if the induced maps on object and morphism spaces are étale.

(ii)

A category
{\mathcal{B}}
is said to be
nonsingular
if for any two objects
x,y\in B
there is at most one morphism in
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)
.

(iii)

A nonsingular category
{\mathcal{B}}
is called a
poset
if the object space
B
is equipped with a partial order
\preccurlyeq
such that
x\preccurlyeq y
iff
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(x,y)\neq\emptyset
.

(iv)

If for some
x\in B
the semigroup
24
24

24

A semigroup is a set
S
with an associative operation
S\times S\to S
that has an identity element but is not required to have inverses.

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
is a group, then we call
G_{x}:={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
the
isotropy group
at
x
.

(v)

A topological category
{\mathcal{B}}
is said to be
locally injective
if
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}:B\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is locally injective, i.e. every
x\in B
has a neighbourhood
N_{x}
such that
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}:N_{x}\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is injective.

(vi)

A groupoid

{\mathcal{X}}
is called
proper
if it satisfies the properness condition of Definition
2.1.1
(i), i.e. every
x\in X
has a neighbourhood
N(x)
such that the restricted target map

t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to X

is proper.
25
25

25

A map
f:X\to Y
is said to be
proper
if
f^{-1}(K)
is compact for every compact subset
K\subset Y
.

(vii)

An étale functor
f:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
between groupoids is said to be an
equivalence
if it induces isomorphisms
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(y,y^{\prime})\simeq{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(f(y),f(y^{\prime}))
between morphism sets and a homeomorphism
|f|:|{\mathcal{Y}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
of the realizations.

The various properness notions in the literature are compared in Lemma
3.2.5
, and Corollary
3.2.6
