shows that the realization of a proper étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
is Hausdorff.

If we consider an étale proper groupoid using the class of M-polyfolds and local sc-diffeomorphisms, then the above definition is not quite the same as the notion of ep-groupoid in Definition
2.1.1
, which requires only sc-smoothness of the maps
c,u,i
, rather than local sc-diffeomorphisms.
However, the next lemma shows that étale proper groupoids in this class are the same as ep-groupoids in the sense of polyfold theory
[
HWZ
, Ch.7]
.
26
26

26

For clarity, we will use the word ep-groupoid only if we are working in the
class of M-polyfolds.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be an étale category modelled on M-polyfolds in the sense of Definition
2.1.1
. Then the unit and composition maps are local sc-diffeomorphisms.
If in addition
{\mathcal{B}}
is a groupoid then the inverse map is also a sc-diffeomorphism.
Hence
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale in the sense of Definition
3.1.2
.

Proof.

It suffices to exhibit local inverses of the structure maps of
{\mathcal{B}}
. For the unit
u:B\to{\bf B},x\mapsto{\rm id}_{x}
we know that
s\circ u={\rm id}_{B}
, where
s:{\bf B}\to B
, is a local sc-diffeomorphism; hence
s
provides the local inverse for
u
.
For the composition
c:{\bf B}{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}{\bf B}\to{\bf B},(m,m^{\prime})\mapsto m\circ m^{\prime}
we can use the fact that
s\circ c=s\circ{\rm pr}_{1}
. Here
{\rm pr}_{1}:{\bf B}{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}{\bf B}\to{\bf B}
is projection to the first factor, which is a local sc-diffeomorphism by construction of the fiber product
{\bf B}{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}{\bf B}
. Since the source map
s
also is a local sc-diffeomorphism, this shows that the composition
c
is a local sc-diffeomorphism.
Finally, in the case of a groupoid the inverse
i:{\bf X}\to{\bf X},m\mapsto m^{-1}
is its own inverse map since inverses are unique in any groupoid. ∎

Remark 3.1.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
be a category.
For any subset
V\subset B
we obtain a
restricted category

{\mathcal{B}}|_{V}
by taking the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{B}}
with objects
V
.
If
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale and
V
is open, then Lemma
3.1.7
below identifies the realization
|{\mathcal{B}}|_{V}|=\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle V\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
(with the quotient topology) with the subset
|V|=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(V)\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
(with the relative topology) as topological spaces. Moreover, the restriction
{\mathcal{B}}|_{V}
to an open subset
V
is also étale, since
{\bf B}|_{V}:=s^{-1}(V)\cap t^{-1}(V)\subset{\bf B}
is an open subset, hence inherits étale structure, and all structure maps in
{\mathcal{B}}|_{V}
are restrictions of the étale structure maps in
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Furthermore, Lemma
3.2.5
considers an étale proper groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
and shows that the restriction
{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
to an open subset
V\subset X
is also an étale proper groupoid.

Although the above results apply when
V\subset B
is any open set, in applications such as
Theorem
2.1.2
,
V
is saturated, i.e. of the form
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(V))
.

{\Diamond}

We will often consider subsets
Z\subset B
that are not open but
saturated
. For groupoids as in
[
HWZ
, p.341]
,
this is equivalent to requiring that the set is closed under morphisms. (For example, the zero set
Z=f^{-1}(0)\subset X
of any sc-Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is saturated.)
In general categories, we call
Z\subset B
saturated if
Z=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z))
.
In that case, as noted above,
|Z|
carries both the relative topology given by the embedding
|Z|:=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
and the quotient topology given by the quotient map
Z\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}=|{\mathcal{Z}}|
to the realization of the full subcategory
{\mathcal{Z}}
of
{\mathcal{B}}
with objects
Z
.
Fortunately, the two topologies agree in étale settings (unlike the Kuranishi situation in
[
MW1
, Def.3.1.14]
).

Lemma 3.1.7
.

Suppose
Z\subset B
is an open or saturated subset of the objects in an étale category
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
.
Then the quotient topology on
|Z|=\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
agrees with the relative topology on
|Z|\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
.

Proof.

In both cases, we will identify
\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}\cong\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)
so that the quotient map
{\rm pr}_{Z}:Z\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
coincides with the restriction of
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
to
Z
.
If
Z
is open, then by
Lemma
3.1.8
below, every open subset of
Z
projects to an open subset of
|{\mathcal{B}}|
.
In particular,
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
is open. Thus the
openness of
O\subset\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)
in the relative topology is equivalent to the openness of
O\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
,
and (using the lemma again) to the openness of
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(O)\cap Z=\pi_{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}(O)
, and hence
to the openness of
O
in the quotient topology on
\scriptstyle Z\!
/
\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
.

In the case of a saturated subset
Z\subset B
,
openness of
O\subset\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)
in the relative topology means
O=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\cap V
for some open set
V\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
. By definition of the quotient topology on
|{\mathcal{B}}|
that means
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(V)\subset B
is open, and thus
Z\cap\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(V)\subset B
is an open subset in the relative topology of
Z\subset B
. Moreover, we have
{\rm pr}_{Z}^{-1}(O)=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\cap V)=Z\cap\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(V)
since
Z
is saturated.
This shows that
{\rm pr}_{Z}^{-1}(O)\subset Z
is open, and thus
O\subset\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
is open in the quotient topology.

Conversely, openness of
O\subset\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle\sim_{\mathcal{B}}$}}
in the quotient topology means
{\rm pr}_{Z}^{-1}(O)\subset Z
is open. Here
Z\subset B
is equipped with the relative topology, so
{\rm pr}_{Z}^{-1}(O)=Z\cap U
for some open subset
U\subset B
. Now
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U)\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
is open by Lemma
3.1.8
below.
Moreover, we have
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z\cap U)=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\cap\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U)
since
Z
is saturated.
This identifies
O=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}({\rm pr}_{Z}^{-1}(O))\subset\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)
as the intersection
O=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\cap\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U)
with an open set, thus as open set in the relative topology of
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
.
∎

Here and throughout, topological considerations in étale categories and étale proper groupoids are simplified by the following.

Lemma 3.1.8
.

(i)

The projection
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}:B\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is an open map in any étale category.

(ii)

For any étale proper groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
we have
{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(|U|)=|{\rm cl}_{X}(U)|
when
U\subset X
is saturated or
U\subset X
is precompact.

Proof.

(i) is a generalization of
[
HWZ
, Prop.7.1.8]
.
To prove it, let
U
be an open subset of
B
. Then
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U)
is open in
|{\mathcal{B}}|
iff
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U))
is open in
B
, i.e. iff every
y\in\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U)
has an open neighbourhood
U_{y}\subset\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U))
. For all such
y
there is a finite chain of morphisms

m_{1},\cdots,m_{k}
in
{\mathcal{B}}
with
s(m_{1})=y,\ t(m_{k})=y_{k}\in U
as follows

y=:y_{0}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\;y_{1}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\leftarrow}}\;y_{3}\;\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}\;y_{k}\in U.

(To achieve such a zigzag chain from an arbitrary chain, one can insert appropriate identity morphisms, and/or compose pairs of morphisms that go in the same direction.)
Since each
m_{i}
extends to an injective étale map from a neighbourhood of its source onto a
neighbourhood of its target,
the end point
y_{k}=y
has an open neighbourhood
N_{y}
in
B
consisting of points that are equivalent to some point in
x\in U
.
Hence
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U)
is open, because every point
y\in\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U))

has a neighbourhood
N_{y}\subset\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(U))
.

(ii) is proven by the same arguments as in
[
HWZ
, Lemma 12.4.4]
.
∎

We end this section by explaining the notation used in
[
HWZ
]
for bundles.
This is sufficient preparation for the discussion of group actions on bundles in §
3.3
.
A much fuller treatment of the basic definitions is given in §
3.4
.

Definition 3.1.9
.

A
bundle

{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
over an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}

is given by an étale space
W
with continuous surjections

P:W\to X
and
\mu:{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W\to W
such that

(i)

P
is a locally trivial vector bundle in the appropriate category.

(ii)

\mu
is étale and is a lift of the target map
t:{\bf X}\to X
in the sense that
P\circ\mu(\cdot,w)=t
for all
w\in W
. Further, it is compatible with the relevant linear and groupoidal structures.

As in
[
HWZ
, §8.3]
, these conditions ensure that

P
lifts to a functor
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
given by

{\mathcal{P}}(w)=P(w),\;{\mathcal{P}}(m,w)=m
, where
{\mathcal{W}}=(W,{\bf W})
is the étale groupoid with
objects
W
and

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle{\bf W}={\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W,\quad(s\times t)(m,w)=(w,\mu(m,w)),

\displaystyle{\rm id}_{w}=({\rm id}_{P(w)},w),\quad(m,w)\circ(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})=\bigl(m^{\prime}\circ m,w\bigr)\quad\mbox{ if }w^{\prime}=\mu(m,w).

A
section
of
(P,\mu)
is an étale map
f:X\to W
that satisfies
P\circ f={\rm id}_{X}
, and is compatible with morphisms in the sense that
f(t(m))=\mu(m,f(s(m)))
for all
m\in{\bf X}
.

One can check as in Lemma
3.4.3
that each section
f:X\to W
extends to a unique étale functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
.

3.2.
Preuniformizers, properness, and groupoid completions

An important property of ep-groupoids is the existence of local uniformizers
[
HWZ
, Def.7.1.20]
centered at any
x\in X
– roughly speaking, a neighbourhood
\simeq\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G_{x}$}}
of
|x|\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
given as the quotient of a natural action of the isotropy group
G_{x}:={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x)
on a neighbourhood
U\subset X
of
x
.
We will generalize this notion to étale categories – i.e. allowing any étale class from §
3.1
, and not requiring groupoid structure.
Throughout, we consider left actions of a group
G
on a space
U
, that is

(hg)*x=h*(g*x)
for
h,g\in G,x\in U
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

A
preuniformizer
for an étale category
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
consists of

(i)

an open subset
U\subset B
;

(ii)

an étale action
G\times U\to U,(g,y)\mapsto g*y
of a finite group
G
;

(iii)

an étale injection
{\Gamma}:G\times U\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(U,U)\subset{\bf B}
satisfying the following:

(a)

{\Gamma}
identifies the morphisms in its image with the group action in the sense that
27
27

27

The inversion of order in the last identity compared with
[
HWZ
]
results from our use of categorical notation for the composition
\circ
of morphisms, while group actions are left actions both here and in
[
HWZ
]
.

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle s\bigl({\Gamma}(g,y)\bigr)=y,\qquad t\bigl({\Gamma}(g,y)\bigr)=g*y,\qquad{\Gamma}(hg,y)={\Gamma}(g,y)\circ{\Gamma}(h,g*y);

(b)

{\Gamma}(G\times U)
contains all connected components of
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(U,U)
that have a morphism
m
with
s(m)=t(m)
;

(c)

the induced map
|{\Gamma}|:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is locally injective and has bounded fibers in the sense that

\sup_{p\in|U|}\#\{[x]\in\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\,|\,|x|=p\}<\infty
.

We will abbreviate this data by
(U,G,{\Gamma})
or even
(U,G)
, and also identify it with the induced faithful functor of the
translation groupoid
28
28

28

A category of the form
(U,G\times U)
is called a translation groupoid in
[
HWZ
, p.294 in §7.1]
.

(U,G\times U)
into the subcategory
(U,{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(U,U))
of
{\mathcal{B}}
. The induced map
|{\Gamma}|:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is called the
footprint map
.

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is an étale groupoid and
{\Gamma}:G\times U\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(U,U)
is surjective, then, as in
[
HWZ
, Def.7.1.20]

(U,G,{\Gamma})
is called a
local uniformizer
.
A
local uniformizer around

x\in X
is a local uniformizer
(U,G,{\Gamma})
such that
x\in U
and we have
t\circ{\Gamma}(\cdot,x)\equiv x
. In particular,
{\Gamma}(\cdot,x):G\to G_{x}={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x)
identifies
G
with the isotropy group of
x
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

(i)

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is an ep-groupoid, then local uniformizers for
{\mathcal{X}}
exist near any object
x\in X
by
[
HWZ
, Prop.7.1.19]
, where the isotropy group
G\simeq{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x)
is finite by
[
HWZ
, Prop.7.1.12]
. Moreover, by lifting a neighbourhood basis of
|x|\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
,
we may choose the domain
U
to be arbitrarily small.
This proof, although given in the polyfold case, also applies to the étale proper groupoids in the other étale categories considered here.

(ii)

Our notion of preuniformizer is significantly more general than that of a local uniformizer; in particular the footprint map
|{\Gamma}|:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is not in general injective – though it is assumed locally injective, and is locally surjective by Lemma
3.2.3
(ii).

However, our notion of local uniformizer is the same as in
[
HWZ
, Def.7.1.20]
; in particular, in this case
|{\Gamma}|
is a homeomorphism to its image by Lemma
3.2.3
(iii).

{\Diamond}

Here are some general properties of preuniformizers and thus local uniformizers.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

The following holds for any preuniformizer
(U,G,{\Gamma})
in an étale category
{\mathcal{B}}
.

(i)

For all
x\in U
, the semigroup
29
29

29

See the footnote in Definition
3.1.4
(iv).

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
is isomorphic via
{\Gamma}(\cdot,x)

to the subgroup
G_{x}=\{g\in G\,\big|\,t({\Gamma}(g,x))=x\}\subset G
.
In particular, each
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
is a group, and these groups are isomorphic as
x
varies in an orbit of
G
.

(ii)

The subset
|U|\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
is open and the induced map
|{\Gamma}|:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|U|
is a local homeomorphism
with respect to the quotient topology on
\scriptstyle U\!
/
\!\scriptstyle G
and the subspace topology on
|U|\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
.

(iii)

If
{\mathcal{B}}
is a groupoid, and
(U,G,{\Gamma})
is a local uniformizer, then

|{\Gamma}|:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|U|
is injective and hence a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Since
{\Gamma}
intertwines products in
G
with composition of morphisms, it induces an injective homomorphism
{\Gamma}_{x}:G_{x}\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)\subset G_{x}
.
Condition (iii)(b) in Definition
3.2.1
implies that
{\Gamma}_{x}:G_{x}\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
is surjective and hence an isomorphism, which implies that

G_{x}\simeq{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
for all
x\in U
.
The second claim in (i) then holds because it holds for the translation groupoid
(U,G\times U)
.

To prove (ii), notice first that
|U|
is open in
|{\mathcal{B}}|
because the subset
U\subset B
is open by condition (i) in Definition
3.2.1
,
and the étale property of
{\mathcal{B}}
implies that
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
is an open map
by Lemma
3.1.8
.
Further, the quotient map
{\sigma}:U\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}
is open.
Since the morphisms in the translation groupoid
(U,G\times U)
inject into those of
{\mathcal{B}}
, each
G
-orbit maps to a single equivalence class of
\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
. Hence there is an induced surjective map
|{\Gamma}|:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|U|
. To see that
|{\Gamma}|
is continuous,
consider
O^{\prime}:=|{\Gamma}|^{-1}(O)
where
O\subset|U|
is open. Then
{\sigma}^{-1}(O^{\prime})=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(O)\cap U
is an open set, since
{\sigma}:U\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}
is an open map, so that
O^{\prime}={\sigma}({\sigma}^{-1}(O^{\prime}))
is also an open set.

It remains to show that each
y\in U
has an open neighbourhood
N
such that
|{\Gamma}||_{{\sigma}(N)}
is a homeomorphism to its image.
To prove this, we adapt the arguments in
[
HWZ
, Prop.7.1.15,7.1.19]
.
Given
y\in U

choose an open neighbourhood
N\subset U
such that
|{\Gamma}|:{\sigma}(N)\to|U|
is injective. By shrinking
N
we may suppose that it is
G_{y}
-invariant and disjoint from its images under all
g\in G{\smallsetminus}G_{y}
. Then
{\sigma}:N\to{\sigma}(N)\subset\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}
factors through a homeomorphism
\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle N\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G_{y}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\sigma}(N)
.
We claim that the continuous map
|{\Gamma}||_{{\sigma}(N)}:{\sigma}(N)\to|N|
is a homeomorphism onto
|N|\subset|U|
(which is an open set since
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
is an open map). Since
|{\Gamma}||_{{\sigma}(N)}
is injective by construction we need only check that its inverse is continuous. Thus for each open set
O\subset{\sigma}(N)\subset\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}
we must check that
|{\Gamma}|(O)
is open.
But the open set
{\sigma}^{-1}(O)
maps onto
O
, so that
|{\Gamma}|(O)=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}({\sigma}^{-1}(O))
and hence is open because
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
is an open map. This completes the proof of (ii).

The hypothesis that
{\mathcal{B}}
is a groupoid implies that two points
x,y
in
U
are equivalent under
\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
if and only if there is a morphism
m\in{\bf B}
with
s(m)=x,t(m)=y
. Since this morphism lies in the full subcategory
{\mathcal{B}}|_{U}
which is isomorphic to the translation groupoid
(U,G\times U)
, the map
\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|U|
is injective. Hence (iii) follows from (ii).
∎

We now give an example of an étale category
{\mathcal{B}}
that is the union of two preuniformizers, both with trivial groups
G
. We show in (ii) that although
|{\mathcal{B}}|
is not usually Hausdorff, the category
{\mathcal{B}}
is proper in the sense of Definition
3.1.4
, which illustrates the fact that properness is not a useful notion in the context of étale categories that are not groupoids.
The setup is slightly more elaborate than is needed here to make our point, but it is a special case of the constructions discussed in §
4.2
.

Example 3.2.4
.

(i)

Let
{\widetilde{V}}\subset U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be nested open subsets,
and suppose that
G
is a finite group that acts freely on
{\widetilde{V}}
in such a way that the action extends freely to an open neighbourhood
U^{\prime}\subset U

of the closure
{\rm cl}({\widetilde{V}})
of
{\widetilde{V}}
in
U
.
Let
\rho:{\widetilde{V}}\to V:=\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\widetilde{V}}\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}
be the quotient map, and
consider the étale category
{\mathcal{B}}
with objects
V\sqcup U
and morphisms consisting of the identity morphisms together with
a component
30
30

30

To be consistent with the notation in
[
MW1
,
MW2
,
MW3
]
we will interpret these morphisms as going from
V
to
U
, though in some ways it might be more natural to think of them as going in the other direction.

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(V,U)\simeq{\widetilde{V}}
of morphisms from
V
to
U
that “quotients out by the action of
G
on
{\widetilde{V}}
.” More precisely, if we denote the elements of
{\widetilde{V}}
by
y
and suppress the inclusion map
{\widetilde{V}}\to U

we have

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(V,U)\simeq{\widetilde{V}},\quad s\times t\,:{\widetilde{V}}\to V\times U,\;\;y\mapsto\bigl(\rho(y),y\bigr).

While the projection map
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}|_{V}
is injective (where
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
is as in (
3.1.1
)),
the map
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}|_{U}
is not, because the points in
|{\widetilde{V}}|=|V|\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|

have
\#G
preimages in
U
.
Nevertheless, the map
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}|_{U}
is locally injective because
G
acts freely on
U^{\prime}
. Thus
{\mathcal{B}}
is covered by two preuniformizers with domains
V,U
respectively and trivial groups
G=\{{\rm id}\}
.

(ii)

We claim that if
{\mathcal{B}}
is as in (i) its realization
|{\mathcal{B}}|
is not Hausdorff unless
G=\{{\rm id}\}
or the frontier
{\rm Fr}_{U}({\widetilde{V}}):={\rm cl}({\widetilde{V}}){\smallsetminus}{\widetilde{V}}
is empty. To see this, suppose given
x\in{\rm Fr}_{U}({\widetilde{V}})\subset U
and
{\rm id}\neq g\in G
. Then
x,g(x)
are two distinct points in

U{\smallsetminus}V
, and have distinct images
|x|,|g(x)|
in
|{\mathcal{B}}|
. Now choose any neighbourhoods
N_{|x|},N_{|g(x)|}
of
|x|,|g(x)|
in
|{\mathcal{B}}|
and consider their inverse images
N_{x}
,
N_{g(x)}
in
U
. Since these sets are open and contain
x,g(x)
respectively, both
g(N_{x})
and
N_{g(x)}
are neighbourhoods of
g(x)
in
U
. Therefore
g(N_{x})\cap N_{g(x)}
is a neighbourhood of
g(x)
, and hence must have nonempty intersection with
{\widetilde{V}}
. But if
y\in g(N_{x})\cap N_{g(x)}\cap{\widetilde{V}}
, then

|y|\in N_{|x|}\cap N_{|g(x)|}

because
|N_{x}|=N_{|x|}
and
|N_{g(x}|=N_{|g(x)|}
by construction. Therefore

N_{|x|},N_{|g(x)|}
are never disjoint.

On the other hand, the map
s\times t\,:{\widetilde{V}}\to V\times U
is proper because the map
\rho:{\widetilde{V}}\to V
is surjective and proper. Further, every point
x\in B=V\sqcup U
does have a neighbourhood
N(x)
such that the map
t:s^{-1}({\rm cl}_{B}N(x))\to B
is proper.
Indeed, if
x\in U
and we choose
N(x)\subset U
, the only morphisms in
s^{-1}({\rm cl}_{B}N(x))
are identity maps, and the existence of
N(x)
is clear. Further, since

\rho({\widetilde{V}})=V
then for any
x\in V
we may take
N(x)\subset V
to be compact, so that
s^{-1}({\rm cl}_{B}N(x))=({\rm cl}_{V}N(x))\sqcup\rho^{-1}({\rm cl}_{V}N(x))
; thus
t:s^{-1}({\rm cl}_{B}N(x))\to V\sqcup U
is proper.

{\Diamond}

We next discuss various conditions on a topological groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}:=(X,{\mathcal{X}})

that guarantee that its realization is Hausdorff.
We will assume that the object space
X
is first countable, but we do not assume that
{\mathcal{X}}
is étale.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a topological groupoid such that
X
is first countable.

(i)

If the space
X
is locally compact and

s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
is proper, then
{\mathcal{X}}
is proper
in the sense of Definition
3.1.4
.

(ii)

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is proper in the sense of Definition
3.1.4
, then
the map
s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
is proper and has closed graph.

(iii)

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is étale and has local uniformizer
(U,G)
, then for every closed subset
C
of
U

such that
|C|
is closed in
|{\mathcal{X}}|
the map
t:s^{-1}(C)\to X
is proper.

(iv)

If the map
\pi:X\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
is open
31
31

31

For étale groupoids this is guaranteed by Lemma
3.1.8
(i).
and
s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
has closed graph, then
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is Hausdorff.

Proof.

To prove (i) suppose that
s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
is proper. Then, the local compactness assumption on
X
provides for any
x\in X
a neighbourhood
N(x)\subset X
whose closure
{\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))
is compact. Now, given a compact subset
K\subset X
, its
preimage under
t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to X
is

s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\cap t^{-1}(K)=(s\times t)^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\times K\bigr)\subset{\bf X},

which is also compact by properness of
s\times t
and compactness of the Cartesian product
{\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\times K\subset X\times X
.

To prove the first claim in (ii), suppose that every
x\in X
has a neighbourhood
N(x)\subset X
such that
t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to X
is proper, and denote by
({\rm pr}_{i})_{i=1,2}
the projection of
X\times X
onto its
i
th factor. Then the preimage in
{\bf X}
of a compact subset
K\subset X\times X
under
s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
is compact because

s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\cap t^{-1}\bigl({\rm pr}_{2}(K)\bigr)
is compact for every
x
by choice of
N(x)
, and the compactness of
{\rm pr}_{1}(K)
implies that it has a finite covering by sets of the form
N(x)
.

To prove the second claim in (ii),
it suffices to observe that if
s\times t
does not have closed graph, there is a convergent sequence
(x_{k},y_{k})\to(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})
in
X\times X
, with
(x_{k},y_{k})\in{\rm im\,}(s\times t)
, but

(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})\notin{\rm im\,}(s\times t)
. Then the compact set
K=\{(x_{k},y_{k})|k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\cup\{(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})\}
does not have compact preimage.

The statement in (iii) generalizes
[
HWZ
, Prop.7.5.10]
, which characterizes the
“proper” subsets of an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
(subsets
C
for which the map
t:s^{-1}(C)\to X
is proper).
Here we do not assume
{\mathcal{X}}
to be proper, and so need to give a direct proof.
Let
K\subset X
be compact. Since
U\cap\pi^{-1}(|C|)=\bigcup_{g\in G}gC
,
it suffices to prove the claim when
C=U\cap\pi^{-1}(|C|)
. Further, because
X
is first countable, it suffices to prove that every sequence
\phi_{k}\in s^{-1}(C)\cap t^{-1}(K)
has a convergent subsequence. Since
K
is compact, we can assume that
y_{k}:=t(\phi_{k})\to y_{\infty}\in K
.
Consider the sequence
x_{k}:=s(\phi_{k})\in C\cap\pi^{-1}(\pi(K))
. Since

|C|\cap|K|
is the intersection of a compact set with a closed set and hence compact, and since
\pi:U\to|U|
quotients out by the action of the finite group
G
, the set
C\cap\pi^{-1}(\pi(K))=C\cap\pi^{-1}(|C|\cap|K|)
is also compact. Hence, without loss of generality we may assume that
x_{k}
also converges, with limit
x_{\infty}
.
Since
\pi:C\to|C|
is continuous and
|x_{k}|=|y_{k}|
by construction, we have
|x_{\infty}|=\lim|x_{k}|=\lim|y_{k}|=|y_{\infty}|
, where here we make essential use of the fact that
|U|
is Hausdorff, which holds because the map
\psi:\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G$}}\to|U|
is a homeomorphism by Lemma
3.2.3
(iii). Therefore there is an element
m_{\infty}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})
, and
it is now easy to find a convergent subsequence of
(\phi_{k})
. Indeed,
because the maps
s,t
are étale, there is a convergent sequence
\phi_{k}^{\prime}\in{\bf X}
with limit
m_{\infty}
and with
t(\phi^{\prime}_{k})=y_{k}
and
s(\phi_{k}^{\prime})\in U
for all
k
. Since
|s(\phi_{k}^{\prime})|=|y_{k}|=|x_{k}|
for all
k
, there are elements
g_{k}\in G
such that

x_{k}=g_{k}*s(\phi_{k}^{\prime})=s\bigl({\Gamma}(g_{k},x)\circ\phi_{k}^{\prime}\bigr),

which implies by the groupoid property of
{\mathcal{X}}
that there are elements
h_{k}\in G_{x_{k}}
such that

\phi_{k}={\Gamma}(h_{k},x)\circ{\Gamma}(g_{k},x)\circ\phi^{\prime}_{k}
for all
k
. Since there are only finitely many possibilities for
h_{k},g_{k}
, the given sequence
\phi_{k}
must have a convergent subsequence.

To prove (iv)
suppose that
x,y
are two points in
X

such that every neighbourhood of
|x|
intersects every neighbourhood of
|y|
.
Since the map
\pi:X\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
is open
we may take neighbourhoods of the form
\pi(N_{x}),\pi(N_{y})
where
N_{x},N_{y}
are open neighbourhoods of
x,y
respectively, and hence find sequences

x_{k}\to x
and
y_{k}\to y
such that
x_{k}\sim y_{k}
. Because
{\mathcal{X}}
is a groupoid,
x_{k}\sim y_{k}
exactly if there is a morphism
x_{k}\to y_{k}
, i.e. exactly if
(x_{k},y_{k})\in s\times t({\bf X})\subset X\times X
. Since
(x_{k},y_{k})\to(x,y)
, the closed graph condition implies that
(x,y)\in s\times t\,({\bf X})
. Hence
|x|=|y|
, i.e.
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is Hausdorff. A similar argument applies if
\pi
is closed, since every point of
X
has a countable basis of closed neighbourhoods.
∎

Corollary 3.2.6
.

(i)

The realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|
of any étale proper groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
is Hausdorff.

(ii)

Suppose the étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
has Hausdorff realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|
, and is covered by a finite number of local uniformizers
(U_{I},G_{I})_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}
in the sense that
|{\mathcal{X}}|=\bigcup_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}|U_{I}|
.
Let
U:=\bigcup_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}U_{I}\subset X
, and define
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
(as in Remark
3.1.6
) to be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{X}}
with objects
U
.
Then the categories
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
are both proper.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is étale, the projection
\pi:X\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
is open by Lemma
3.1.8
, and the map
s\times t:{\bf X}\to X\times X
has closed graph by Lemma
3.2.5
(ii). Therefore (i) follows from Lemma
3.2.5
(iv).

Towards (ii), we first show that the realization
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is metrizable when we assume it to be Hausdorff and covered
|{\mathcal{X}}|=\bigcup_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}|U_{I}|
by a finite number of local uniformizers
(U_{I},G_{I})_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}
.
Note first that each
U_{I}
is metrizable since it is a subset of
X
, which is metrizable by
definition of the étale spaces (a)-(d). By the Nagata–Smirnov metrization theorem (see
[
Mu
, Thm.40.3]
, this is equivalent to saying that
U_{I}
is regular and has a basis for its topology that is a countable union of locally finite collections of open sets. Both these properties are inherited by the quotient
|U_{I}|=U_{I}/G_{I}
; regularity of the quotient follows from Exercise 7(b) on p 199 of
[
Mu
]
, while the second condition holds because the projection
U_{I}\to|U_{I}|
is open and globally finite-to-one so that the image in
|U_{I}|
of a locally finite collection of open sets in
U_{I}
has the same properties. Hence the quotient
|U_{I}|
is metrizable.
Thus, because
|U_{I}|
is open in
|{\mathcal{X}}|

by Lemma
3.2.3
(ii), the space
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is locally metrizable.
Further, because metrizability is equivalent to paracompactness, Hausdorffness
and local metrizability by Smirnov’s metrizability theorem (see
[
Mu
, Thm.42.1]
),
each
|U_{I}|
is paracompact. It follows that
|{\mathcal{X}}|
, which is the finite union of the open sets
|U_{I}|
, is also paracompact. Since
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is Hausdorff by hypothesis,
Smirnov’s metrizability theorem implies that
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is metrizable.

To prove properness of
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
in (ii), we must check that every
x\in U
has a neighbourhood
N(x)
so that
t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to X
is proper.
By assumption, we have
x\in U_{I}
for some local uniformizer
(U_{I},G_{I})
, and applying
Lemma
3.2.5
(iii) to this uniformizer guarantees properness of
t:s^{-1}(C)\to X
for any closed subset
C
subset of
U_{I}
such that
|C|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is closed. Thus it suffices to find a closed neighbourhood

C\subset U_{I}
of
x
such that
|C|
is closed in
|{\mathcal{X}}|
.
To this end, consider the closed set
|{\mathcal{X}}|{\smallsetminus}|U_{I}|
. Since
|x|\notin|{\mathcal{X}}|{\smallsetminus}|U_{I}|
and
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is metrizable and hence normal (by
[
Mu
, Thm.32.3]
), the point
|x|
(which is a closed set since
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is Hausdorff) has an open neighbourhood
W\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
whose closure
{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(W)
is disjoint from
|{\mathcal{X}}|{\smallsetminus}|U_{I}|
and hence is contained in
|U_{I}|
.
Therefore
C:=\pi^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(W)\bigr)
is contained in
U_{I}
. Moreover, because

{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(W)
is
a closed neighbourhood of
|x|
in the open set
|{\mathcal{X}}|
its pullback
C
to
U_{I}

is a closed neighbourhood of
x
in
U_{I}
.

It is now straightforward to check that the category
{\mathcal{X}}
itself is proper. Indeed, by assumption, for every
y\in X
there is some
x\in U_{I}
such that
|x|=|y|
. Hence, as in the proof of Lemma
3.1.8
(i) there is a local étale bijection
\phi:N^{\prime}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}N^{\prime}(x)
from a neighbourhood
N^{\prime}(y)
of
y
onto a
neighbourhood
N^{\prime}(x)\subset U
of
x
. Now choose a neighbourhood
N(x)
of
X
such that

{\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\subset N^{\prime}(x)
and
t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to X
is proper. Then
the étale map

s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(x))\bigr)\to s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(y))\bigr),\quad m\mapsto\phi\circ m

has an étale inverse. It follows that
t:s^{-1}\bigl({\rm cl}_{X}(N(y))\bigr)\to X
is proper.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Finally, we discuss groupoid completions of étale categories
{\mathcal{B}}
.
For that purpose recall from Definition
3.1.4
that the sets
B_{x}:={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,x)
are generally semigroups as they may lack inverses.

Definition 3.2.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
be a category. Then a pair
(\widehat{\mathcal{B}},{\iota})
consisting of a groupoid
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
together with an injective functor
{\iota}:{\mathcal{B}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{B}}}
is called a
groupoid completion
of the category
{\mathcal{B}}
if the following holds:

(a)

{\iota}:B\to\widehat{B}
is bijective and induces a bijection
|{\mathcal{B}}|\to|\widehat{{\mathcal{B}}}|
,

(b)

for all
x\in B
, the functor
{\iota}
induces an isomorphism
B_{x}\to\widehat{B}_{x}:={\rm Mor}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}(x,x)
.

If
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale, then a pair
(\widehat{\mathcal{B}},{\iota})
as above is an
étale groupoid completion
if in addition

(c)

the category
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
has an étale structure so that
\iota
is étale.

Remark 3.2.8
.

If
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale and has étale groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
, then by conditions (a) and (c) we can identify the object spaces of both categories. Then by (a) and the fact that the realization of a category has the quotient topology, the two realization maps
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}:B\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
and

\pi_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}:\widehat{B}\to|\widehat{\mathcal{B}}|
can also be identified, and have the same topological properties. For example, if one is locally injective, so is the other.

{\Diamond}

The following lemma establishes some useful properties of groupoid completions.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

(i)

If
{\mathcal{B}}
has a groupoid completion, then each
B_{x}
is a group, and these groups are isomorphic as
x
ranges over an equivalence class with respect to
\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
.

(ii)

If
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale and there is an injective étale functor
f:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
to an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
that induces an isomorphism
f:B_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}G_{f(x)}:={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x)
for all
x\in B
and an injection
|f|:|{\mathcal{B}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
that is a homeomorphism to its image, then
{\mathcal{B}}
has an étale groupoid completion, namely
({\mathcal{X}}|_{f(B)},f)
, where
{\mathcal{X}}|_{f(B)}
is the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{X}}
with objects
f(B)
.

(iii)

A nonsingular category
{\mathcal{B}}
has a unique groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
up to functorial isomorphism. It is necessarily nonsingular, and is étale and/or locally injective if
{\mathcal{B}}
is.

(iv)

If the nonsingular category
{\mathcal{B}}
is a poset such that each equivalence class
[x]:=\{y\in B\ |\ |x|=|y|\}
in
B
has a unique minimum with morphisms to all other elements.
Then every functor
f:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
whose target is a groupoid extends to a functor

\widehat{f}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
. Further if
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{X}}
and
f:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
are étale, then so is the functor
\widehat{f}
.

Proof.

To prove (i), note first that the isomorphisms
B_{x}\to\widehat{B}_{x}
in (b) provide inverses for
B_{x}
.
Next, in any groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
the morphism
m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
induces an isomorphism

\phi_{m}:G_{x}\to G_{y},\quad{\alpha}\mapsto{\beta}:=m^{-1}\circ{\alpha}\circ m:y\to x\to x\to y.

Since the above identity may be written
m\circ{\beta}={\alpha}\circ m
, it follows that if
{\mathcal{B}}
has a groupoid completion
the equation
m\circ{\beta}={\alpha}\circ m
in
{\bf B}
must have a unique solution
{\beta}\in B_{y}
for each
{\alpha}\in B_{x},m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)
,
and moreover that the assignment

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle\phi_{m}:\quad{\alpha}\mapsto{\beta}=:\phi_{m}({\alpha}),\;\;\mbox{ where }m\circ{\beta}={\alpha}\circ m

is a group isomorphism
B_{x}\to B_{y}
. This proves (i).

To prove (ii), notice that because
f
is étale,
f(B)
is an open subset of
X
. Further,
if
{\mathcal{X}}
is an étale groupoid and
U\subset X
is open then
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
is also an étale groupoid. Hence
f:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{f(B)}
satisfies the conditions in Definition
3.2.7
.

To prove (iii), notice that by the definition of nonsingularity (see Definition
3.1.2
) we have
|{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)|\leq 1
for all
x,y\in B
. Therefore condition (b) implies that any groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is also nonsingular. By
condition (a) we must therefore have

{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}:=\bigl\{(x,y)\in B\times B\ \big|\ x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y\bigr\},

with structure maps
s,t
given by projection onto the first and second factors respectively. Then
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
with this set of morphisms is a groupoid. Next note that
{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}
can be given the structure of an étale space in such a way that all structure maps are étale. But this follows because, as in the proof of Lemma
3.1.8
, each added morphism
y_{0}\to y_{k}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is formed from a zigzag chain

y_{0}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\;y_{1}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\leftarrow}}\;y_{3}\;\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}\;y_{k}
of morphisms in
{\mathcal{B}}
. Finally, the claim about local injectivity holds by Remark
3.2.8
.

In the situation of (iv), denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
the unique groupoid completion of
{\mathcal{B}}
provided by (iii).
For any pair
x,y\in B
with
x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y
, denote by
x_{\min}\in B
the root of the equivalence class
[x]
so that there are unique morphisms
m_{x}:x_{\min}\to x,\ m_{y}:x_{\min}\to y
in
{\mathcal{B}}
. If
m_{xy}\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}

is the unique morphism in
{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}(x,y)
, define
\widehat{f}(m_{xy})=f(m_{x})^{-1}\circ f(m_{y})
. Then
\widehat{f}
takes identity maps to identity maps and is compatible with inverses. To see it is compatible with composition, notice that if
x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y\sim_{\mathcal{B}}z
, then

m_{xz}=m_{xy}\circ m_{yz}=\bigl((m_{x})^{-1}\circ m_{y}\bigr)\circ\bigl((m_{y})^{-1}\circ m_{z}\bigr)=(m_{x})^{-1}\circ m_{z}.

It follows that

\widehat{f}(m_{xz})=f(m_{x})^{-1}\circ f(m_{z})=f(m_{x})^{-1}\circ f(m_{y})\circ f(m_{y})^{-1}\circ f(m_{z})=\widehat{f}(m_{xy})\circ\widehat{f}(m_{yz}).

Finally, if
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{X}},f
are étale, then the induced map
\widehat{f}:{\bf B}\to\widehat{\bf B}
is a composite of étale maps and hence itself étale.
∎

Remark 3.2.10
.

This notion of groupoid completion is rather weak. For example, if
{\mathcal{B}}
is a nonsingular category and
\phi:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is any functor whose target
{\mathcal{X}}
is a groupoid, it is not necessarily true that
\phi
extends to the groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
. Suppose for example that
B=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
and that
there are morphisms
m_{ij}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x_{i},x_{j})
for

(i,j)=(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4)
.
Then
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
has a unique morphism between any pair of objects. However, in order for
\phi
to extend to
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
we would need
\phi(m_{13})\circ\phi(m_{23})^{-1}=\phi(m_{14})\circ\phi(m_{24})^{-1}
, a relation that may or may not hold.

{\Diamond}

The following example shows that groupoid completion generally does not preserve properness, and thus realizations of étale categories are often not Hausdorff.

Example 3.2.11
.

Consider the étale category
{\mathcal{B}}
constructed in Example
3.2.4
with objects
B=V\sqcup U
and
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(V,U)\simeq{\widetilde{V}}\subset U
.
This category is nonsingular and
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}:B\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
is locally injective, hence
{\mathcal{B}}
has a groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
by Lemma
3.2.9
(iii).
Besides the morphisms in
{\mathcal{B}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
has morphisms from
{\widetilde{V}}\subset U
to
V
given by inverting the elements of
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(V,U)

and also morphisms with both source and target in
U
given by the action of
G
on
{\widetilde{V}}\subset U
:

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}(U,U)

\displaystyle=U\sqcup\bigl({\widetilde{V}}\times(G{\smallsetminus}\{{\rm id}\})\bigr),

\displaystyle s\times t(x,{\gamma})

\displaystyle=({\gamma}^{-1}*x,x),\quad x\in{\widetilde{V}},{\gamma}\in G{\smallsetminus}\{{\rm id}\}.

Thus
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is étale.
However, it follows from Example
3.2.4
(ii) that
|\widehat{\mathcal{B}}|=|{\mathcal{B}}|
is not Hausdorff if
{\rm Fr}_{U}({\widetilde{V}})\neq\emptyset
and
G\neq{{\rm id}}
. In this case,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is not proper, since properness implies Hausdorffness by Lemma
3.2.5
(ii) and (iv) and Lemma
3.1.8
.

{\Diamond}

3.3.
Group actions on étale categories

We begin with a natural notion of the action of a finite group on a category.
Here we denote by
{\rm Aut}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
the group of invertible functors
{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
on a given category
{\mathcal{B}}
.
If
{\mathcal{B}}
is topological, then we require functors in
{\rm Aut}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
to act continuously on object and morphism spaces.
If
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale, then we require functors in
{\rm Aut}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
to be étale.

Definition 3.3.1
.

An
action of a finite group
G
on a (possibly topological or étale) category
{\mathcal{B}}
is a homomorphism
F:G\to{\rm Aut}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}),g\mapsto F_{g}
, that is satisfying
F_{hg}=F_{g}\circ F_{h}
.

If
G
acts on two (possibly topological or étale) categories
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
via the homomorphisms
F,F^{\prime}
, then a (continuous or étale) functor
\psi:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
is said to be

G
-equivariant
if
F_{g}\circ\psi=\psi\circ F^{\prime}_{g}
for all
g\in G
.

Remark 3.3.2
.

Above we write functor composition in categorical notation so that, for example,
(F_{g}\circ F_{h})(x)=F_{h}(F_{g}(x))
for
x\in B
. On the other hand, the action
x\mapsto g*x
of a group
G
on a space
X
satisfies the identity
(hg)*x=h*(g*x)
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group.

(i)

Any action of
G
on a topological category
{\mathcal{B}}
induces a continuous action of
G
on the realization
|{\mathcal{B}}|
of
{\mathcal{B}}
.

(ii)

Let
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
be a (possibly topological resp. étale) category. Then

G
acts on
{\mathcal{B}}
if and only if
G
acts
on the spaces
B,{\bf B}
by (continuous resp. étale) maps

F_{g}:x\mapsto g*x,F_{g}:m\mapsto g*m
, in such a way that
for all
x\in B,\;g,h\in G,\;m,m^{\prime}\in{\bf B}
we have

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle g*{\rm id}_{x}={\rm id}_{g*x},\quad

\displaystyle g*(m\circ m^{\prime})=(g*m)\circ(g*m^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(s\times t)(g*m)=\bigl(g*s(m),g*t(m)\bigr).

(iii)

Suppose
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})
is a groupoid and
{\alpha}:G\times X\to{\bf X}
is a map with

(3.3.2)

\displaystyle t({\alpha}(g,x))=x,\quad{\alpha}({\rm id},x)={\rm id}_{x},\quad{\alpha}(hg,x)={\alpha}(g,s({\alpha}(h,x)))\circ{\alpha}(h,x)

for all
g,h\in G,\;x\in X
.
Then this induces an action of
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
as in (ii) with

(3.3.3)

g*x:=s({\alpha}(g^{-1},x)),\qquad g*m:={\alpha}(g^{-1},s(m))\,\circ\,m\,\circ\,{\alpha}(g,g*t(m))

for
x\in X
and
m\in{\bf X}
.
If
{\mathcal{X}}
is a topological resp. étale groupoid and
{\alpha}
is continuous resp. étale, then this construction yields an action of
G
on the topological resp. étale category
{\mathcal{X}}
. Further, the induced action on
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is trivial.

(iii
′
)

Suppose
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})
is a groupoid and
\overline{{\alpha}}:G\times X\to{\bf X}
is a map with

(3.3.4)

\displaystyle s(\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x))=x,\quad\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)={\rm id}_{x},\quad\overline{{\alpha}}(hg,x)=\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x)\circ\overline{{\alpha}}(h,t(\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x)))

for all
g,h\in G,\;x\in X
.
Then this induces an action of
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
as in (ii) with

(3.3.5)

g*x:=t(\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x)),\qquad g*m:=\overline{{\alpha}}(g^{-1},g*s(m))\,\circ\,m\,\circ\,\overline{{\alpha}}(g,t(m))

for
x\in X
and
m\in{\bf X}
.
If
{\mathcal{X}}
is a topological resp. étale groupoid and
{\alpha}
is continuous resp. étale, then this construction yields an action of
G
on the topological resp. étale category
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Further, the induced action on
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is trivial.

(iv)

Let
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
be a category that is nonsingular and locally injective.
Suppose that
G
is a finite group that acts on the object set
B
and preserves the equivalence relation
x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y\Longrightarrow g*x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}g*y
for all
x,y,g
.
Then this action extends uniquely to
G
actions on the category
{\mathcal{B}}
and its groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
.

Moreover, if
{\mathcal{B}}
is a topological resp. étale category and the action of
G
on
B
is contiunuous resp. étale, then this extension is a
G
-action on the topological resp. étale category and its topological resp. étale groupoid completion.

Proof.

In (i), by hypothesis, for each
g\in G
there is a functor
F(g):{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
, and we denote
the induced maps on objects and morphisms by
F_{g}(x)=g*x,F_{g}(m)=g*m
. Because
F(g)
is a functor, each
m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)
yields
g*m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(F_{g}(x),F_{g}(y))={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(g*x,g*y)
.
To see that the action
(g,x)\mapsto g*x
of
G
on
B
preserves the equivalence relation on
B
,
note that
x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y
iff there is a chain of morphisms from
x
to
y
as follows

x:=x_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\leftarrow}}x_{2}\cdots x_{k-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}x_{k}:=y.

Because

m\in{\rm Mor}(x,y)\Longrightarrow g*m\in{\rm Mor}(g*x,g*y)
, applying
g\,*
to this chain gives a similar chain of
morphisms from
g*x
to
g*y
. Therefore
x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y
iff
g*x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}g*y
, so that
the action of
G
preserves the equivalence relation on
B
and hence descends to an action
|x|\mapsto g*|x|
on
|{\mathcal{B}}|
. This action commutes with the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}:B\to|{\mathcal{B}}|
, that is

\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(g*x)=g*(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(x))
. It follows that the action of
G
on
|{\mathcal{B}}|
is continuous; indeed for every open subset
V\subset|{\mathcal{B}}|
and every
g\in G
,
we have
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(g^{-1}*V)=g^{-1}*(\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(V))
.
This proves (i).

To prove (ii), first note that each functor
F(g)\in{\rm Aut}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
consists of two maps
F_{g}:B\to B
,
F_{g}:{\bf B}\to{\bf B}
whose continuity or étaleness and three algebraic properties in (
3.3.1
) follow from
F(g):{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
being a (continuous resp. étale) functor. The fact that both are
G
-actions follows from
F
being a homomorphism:

(hg)*x=F_{hg}(x)=(F_{g}\circ F_{h})(x)=F_{h}(F_{g}(x))=h*(g*x)

and analogously on morphisms.

Conversely, assume the actions on object and morphism spaces satisfy (
3.3.1
) (and possibly are continuous resp. étale). Then we must check that each
g\in G
induces an invertible functor
F(g)=(F_{g},F_{g})
on
{\mathcal{B}}=(B,{\bf B})
and that
F(hg)=F(g)\circ F(h)
holds for all
g,h\in G
.
Then (
3.3.1
) ensures that each
F(g)
is a functor. It inherits continuity resp. étaleness directly from the two actions.
The homomorphism property is equivalent to the maps on objects and morphisms both satsifying
F_{hg}=F_{g}\circ F_{h}
for all
g,h\in G
, which holds since
G\times X\to X,(g,x)\mapsto F_{g}(x)
and
G\times{\bf X}\to{\bf X},(g,m)\mapsto F_{g}(m)
are
G
-actions by hypothesis.
This hypothesis also guarantees that
F_{{\rm id}}:X\to X
and
F_{{\rm id}}:{\bf X}\to{\bf X}
are the identity maps and hence
F({\rm id})
is the identity functor
{\rm id}_{\mathcal{B}}
. Now the homomorphism property guarantees that each
F(g)
is invertible since
F(g)\circ F(g^{-1})=F(g^{-1}g)=F({\rm id})={\rm id}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
F(g^{-1})\circ F(g)=F(gg^{-1})=F({\rm id})={\rm id}_{\mathcal{B}}
, which completes the proof of (ii).

To prove (iii) we must check that (
3.3.3
) defines
G
-actions which satisfy (
3.3.1
).
The action property on objects follows from the source identity included in
{\alpha}(g^{-1}h^{-1},x)={\alpha}(h^{-1},s({\alpha}(g^{-1},x)))\circ{\alpha}(g^{-1},x)
to obtain

(hg)*x=s\bigl(\alpha((hg)^{-1},x)\bigr)=s\bigl({\alpha}(h^{-1},s({\alpha}(g^{-1},x)))\bigr)=h*(g*x).

This guarantees an action
*:G\times X\to X
, and then by definition
{\alpha}(g,x)\in{\rm Mor}(g^{-1}*x,x)
.
With that we can rewrite the above identity as

{\alpha}(g^{-1}h^{-1},x)={\alpha}(h^{-1},g*x)\circ{\alpha}(g^{-1},x)

and use this to check the action property on morphisms: Denoting
x:=s(m)
and
y:=t(m)

\displaystyle(hg)*m

\displaystyle={\alpha}((hg)^{-1},x)\,\circ\,m\,\circ\,{\alpha}(hg,(hg)*y)

\displaystyle={\alpha}(h^{-1},g*x)\circ{\alpha}(g^{-1},x)\circ\,m\,\circ\ {\alpha}(g,h^{-1}*((hg)*y))\circ{\alpha}(h,(hg)*y)

\displaystyle={\alpha}(h^{-1},g*x)\circ(g*m)\circ{\alpha}(h,h*(g*y))\ =h*(g*m).

To check that these actions satisfy (
3.3.1
), first use the composition property in the form

{\rm id}_{y}={\alpha}(gg^{-1},y)={\alpha}(g^{-1},g^{-1}*y)\circ{\alpha}(g,y)
applied to
y=g*x
to obtain

g*{\rm id}_{x}={\alpha}(g^{-1},x)\circ{\rm id}_{x}\circ{\alpha}(g,g*x)={\alpha}(g^{-1},x)\circ{\alpha}(g,g*x)={\rm id}_{g*x}.

Next, the conditions on
(s\times t)(g*m)
may be checked by inspecting (
3.3.3
):

s(g*m)=s({\alpha}(g^{-1},s(m)))=g*m,\qquad t(g*m)=t({\alpha}(g,g*t(m)))=g*t(m)).

Third, compatibility with composition of morphisms
m,m^{\prime}
with
t(m)=s(m^{\prime})=x
holds by

\displaystyle g*(m\circ m^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\alpha}(g^{-1},s(m))\,\circ\,m\,\circ m^{\prime}\,\,\circ\,{\alpha}(g,g*t(m^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\Bigl({\alpha}(g^{-1},s(m))\,\circ\,m\,\circ{\alpha}(g,g*t(m))\Bigr)\circ\Bigl({\alpha}(g^{-1},s(m^{\prime}))\,\circ\,m^{\prime}\,\,\circ\,{\alpha}(g,g*t(m^{\prime}))\Bigr)

\displaystyle=(g*m)\circ(g*m^{\prime}),

where the second equality holds because
{\rm id}_{x}={\alpha}(g^{-1}g,x)={\alpha}(g,g*x)\circ{\alpha}(g^{-1},x)
by (
3.3.2
).

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is a topological resp. étale groupoid and
{\alpha}
is continuous resp. étale, then the maps in (
3.3.3
) are continuous resp. étale since they arise by compisition with the continuous resp. étale structure maps of
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Hence this yields an action of
G
on the topological resp. étale category
{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense of Definition
3.3.1
.
Finally, the induced action on
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is trivial because
x\sim g*x
via the morphism
{\alpha}(g^{-1},g*x)
. This proves (iii).

We prove
(iii
′
) by noting that it is equivalent to (iii). Indeed, an
{\alpha}
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense of (iii) is equivalent to an
\overline{{\alpha}}
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}^{op}
in the sense of (iii
′
), where
\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x)={\alpha}(g^{-1},x)^{op}
and vice versa. Now,
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}^{op}
are isomorphic by the
functor that is the identity on objects and
m\mapsto(m^{-1})^{op}
on morphisms. Using this isomorphism, an
{\alpha}
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}
induces an
\overline{{\alpha}}
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}^{op}
by
\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x)={\alpha}(g,x)^{-1}
.

To prove (iv),
note first that because the morphisms in
{\mathcal{B}}
are uniquely determined by their source and target, the action of
G
on
B
lifts to a unique action on the morphism space
{\bf B}
that satisfies the conditions in (ii). Moreover, as in the proof of in Lemma
3.2.9
(iii),
this action is
étale because locally the map
m\mapsto g*m
may be written as the composite
m\mapsto t^{-1}\circ\bigl(g*(s(m))\bigr)
, where
t^{-1}
is a suitable local inverse to
t
.
Therefore
the homomorphism
F:G\to{\rm Aut}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
exists by (i). Moreover, as in (i), because the
G
-action preserves identity maps it necessarily commutes with the inverse map. Hence
F
extends to a homomorphism
F:G\to{\rm Aut}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}},\widehat{\mathcal{B}})
.
∎

The description of a
G
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}
by a function
{\alpha}:G\times X\to{\bf X}
as in (
3.3.2
), or equivalently by
\overline{{\alpha}}:G\times X\to{\bf X}
as in (
3.3.4
), means that the
G
-action is induced by appropriate morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Furthermore, we will see below that such actions naturally lift to bundles.
For the latter purpose, it is much more natural to use a description with
\overline{{\alpha}}
satisfying
s(\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x))=x
because, as in Definition
3.1.9
, the structure map
\mu:{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W\to W
of a bundle
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
uses the source of a morphism rather than its target. This motivates the next definitions.

Definition 3.3.4
.

(i)

An
inner action
of
G
on a (possibly topological or étale) groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
is
an action induced by a subset of the morphisms of
{\mathcal{X}}
as follows:
There is a (continuous resp. étale) function
{\alpha}:G\times X\to{\bf X}
that satisfies (
3.3.4
),
so that the actions on
X,{\bf X}
are given by (
3.3.5
).
In this case we also say that
G

acts on
{\mathcal{X}}
by inner automorphisms
.

(ii)

If
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is a bundle over a (possibly topological or étale) groupoid,
then we say that an action of
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}

lifts to

{\mathcal{W}}
if
G
acts on
{\mathcal{W}}
in such a way that
{\mathcal{P}}
is
G
-equivariant.

Remark 3.3.5
.

(i)

If
G
acts on a topological or étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
by inner automorphisms, then the induced action of
G
on
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is trivial by Lemma
3.3.3
(iii).

(ii)

Given a local uniformizer
(U,G,{\Gamma})
of an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
as in Definition
3.2.1
, the étale injection
{\Gamma}:G\times U\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(U,U)\subset{\mathcal{X}}
induces a local action of
G
by inner automorphisms on the restricted groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}=(U,{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(U,U))
.
Indeed,
\overline{{\alpha}}:={\Gamma}:G\times U\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(U,U)
satsfies (
3.3.4
) by the conditions on
{\Gamma}
in (
3.2.1
):

s(\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x))=s({\Gamma}(g,x))=x
follows directly.
Compatibility with composition follows from the composition and target property of
{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\overline{{\alpha}}(hg,x)

\displaystyle\,=\,{\Gamma}(hg,x)\,=\,{\Gamma}(g,y)\circ{\Gamma}(h,g*x)

\displaystyle\,=\,{\Gamma}(g,x)\circ{\Gamma}(h,t({\Gamma}(g,x))\,=\,\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x)\circ\overline{{\alpha}}(h,t(\overline{{\alpha}}(g,x))).

Moreover, this implies

\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)\circ\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},t(\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)))=\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id}\,{\rm id},x)=\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x),

where we have

t(\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x))=t({\Gamma}({\rm id},x))={\rm id}*x=x.

So this implies
\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)\circ\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)=\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)
, and then composition with
\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)^{-1}
implies
\overline{{\alpha}}({\rm id},x)={\rm id}_{x}
.

So when discussing
G
-actions in local uniformizers, we may write
{\Gamma}
instead of
\overline{{\alpha}}
. In
[
HWZ
, §7.1]
this is called the natural representation of
G=G_{x}
.

{\Diamond}

Lemma 3.3.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be a bundle with structure map
\mu
as in Definition
3.1.9
over an étale groupoid.
Then every inner action of a finite group
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
has a natural lift to an inner action on
{\mathcal{W}}
.
In fact, let
\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}:G\times X\to{\bf X}
be the étale map determining the
G
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}
as in Lemma
3.3.3
(iii
′
). Then a natural
G
-action on
{\mathcal{W}}
is given as in Definition
3.3.4
(i) by

\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}:G\times W\to{\bf W}={\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W,\qquad\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w):=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w\bigr).

The induced actions on objects and morphisms as in Lemma
3.3.3
(ii) are

g*w=\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w\bigr),\qquad g*(m,w)=(g*m,g*w).

Further, the functor
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
and all sections
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
are
G
-equivariant.

Proof.

To establish the inner action on
{\mathcal{W}}
via Lemma
3.3.3
(iii
′
), note that
\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}
is a composition of étale maps, hence étale. So it remains to check the three properties in (
3.3.4
).
The source identity follows from the definition of
\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}
and the bundle construction (
3.1.2
)

s(\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w))=s\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w\bigr)=w.

The identity property requires in addition the identity property of
\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}

\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}({\rm id},w)=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}({\rm id},P(w)),w\bigr)=\bigl({\rm id}_{P(w)},w\bigr)={\rm id}_{w}.

The composition property follows from the composition property of
\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}
, the composition in (
3.4.1
), and the bundle property
P\mu(m,w)=t(m)
applied to
m=\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w))

\displaystyle\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(hg,w)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(hg,P(w))\,,\,w\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w))\circ\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(h,t(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w))))\,,\,w\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w))\,,\,w\bigr)\circ\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(h,P\mu(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w))\,,\,t(\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w)\circ\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(h,\mu(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w))

\displaystyle=\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w)\circ\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(h,t(\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w))).

Now (
3.3.5
) and (
3.4.1
) provide the induced actions on obects and morphisms:

\displaystyle g*w

\displaystyle=t\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,w)\bigr)=\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w\bigr),

\displaystyle g*(m,w)

\displaystyle=\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g^{-1},g*s(m,w))\,\circ\,(m,w)\,\circ\,\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}(g,t(m,w))

\displaystyle=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g^{-1},P(g*w)),g*w\bigr)\,\circ\,(m,w)\,\circ\,\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,t(m)),\mu(m,w)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g^{-1},g*s(m))\circ m\circ\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,t(m)),g*w\bigr)

\displaystyle=(g*m,g*w)

using the fact that

P(g*w)=P\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w\bigr)=t(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)))=g*P(w)=g*s(m)
.

Next,
G
-equivariance of
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense of Definition
3.3.1
follows similarly by checking it on objects and morphisms:

\displaystyle P(g*w)

\displaystyle\,=\;P\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w)),w\bigr)\;=\;t\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(w))\bigr)\;=\;g*P(w),

\displaystyle P(g*(m,w))

\displaystyle\,=\;P((g*m,g*w))\;=\;g*m\;=\;g*P((m,w)).

Finally, any section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
in the sense of Definition
3.4.2

is given by a map
f:X\to W
satisfying
P\circ f={\rm id}
and
f(t(m))=\mu(m,f(s(m)))
.
It lifts to a functor by Lemma
3.4.3
, given on morphisms by
f(m)=\bigl(m,f(s(m))\bigr)
.
These properties are used to check
G
-equivariance on objects and morphisms:

\displaystyle g*f(x)

\displaystyle\,=\;\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,P(f(x))),f(x)\bigr)\;=\;\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,x),f(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle\,=\;\mu\bigl(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,x),f(s(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,x)))\bigr)\;=\;f(t(\overline{{\alpha}}_{X}(g,x)))\;=\;f(g*x),

\displaystyle g*f(m)

\displaystyle\,=\;g*\bigl(m,f(s(m))\bigr)\;=\;\bigl(g*m,g*f(s(m))\bigr)

\displaystyle\,=\;\bigl(g*m,f(g*s(m))\bigr)\;=\;\bigl(g*m,f(s(g*m))\bigr)\;=\;f(g*m),

where the last steps used equivariance of
f
on objects and
s(g*m)=s(\overline{{\alpha}}(g^{-1},g*s(m))=g*s(m)
from (
3.3.5
) and (
3.3.4
).
∎

Remark 3.3.7
.

(i)

The above constructions are functorial as follows. Let
\Psi:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
be a bundle map over
an étale functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
between étale groupoids as in Definition
3.4.2
and Lemma
3.4.3
, and suppose given inner actions
\overline{{\alpha}}_{X},\overline{{\alpha}}_{X^{\prime}}
of
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
.
We say that the functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
is
equivariant with respect to these inner actions

(or
(G,{\alpha},{\alpha}^{\prime})
-equivariant) if

\psi({\overline{{\alpha}}}_{X}(g,x))=\overline{{\alpha}}_{X^{\prime}}(g,\psi(x)):\ G\times X\to{\bf X}^{\prime}.

It is straightforward to check that in this case
\Psi
is
G
-equivariant with respect to the lifted inner actions on the bundles; in other words

\Psi\circ\overline{{\alpha}}_{W}=\overline{{\alpha}}_{W^{\prime}}\circ({\rm id}_{G}\times\Psi)
.

(ii)

Note that the condition in (i) is stronger than the equivariance condition for an arbitrary action since we now assume that
\psi
takes the morphism
{\overline{{\alpha}}}_{X}(g,x)
that implements the action
x\mapsto g*x
of
G
on
X
to that which implements the action
\psi(x)\mapsto g*\psi(x)

of
G
on
X^{\prime}
. This condition does imply that
\psi
is
G
-equivariant
in the sense of Definition
3.3.1
, but, unless the categories
{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
are effective, the two conditions may not be the same.

To avoid confusion, unless specific mention is made to the contrary, whenever a functor is said to be
G
-equivariant this should be understood in the weaker sense of Definition
3.3.1
.

{\Diamond}

For the final construction of this section we return to general (possibly topological or étale) categories.

Lemma 3.3.8
.

Suppose that the finite group
G
acts on the category
{\mathcal{B}}
.

(i)

This induces a category
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
with object space
B
, morphism space
{\bf B}\times G
and structure maps given for all
x\in B,m\in{\bf B},g\in G
by

\displaystyle(s\times t)\,(m,g)=\bigl(g^{-1}*s(m),\,t(m)\bigr),\quad{\rm id}^{{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}}_{x}=({\rm id}^{\mathcal{B}}_{x},{\rm id}),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)=\bigl((h*m)\circ m^{\prime},\,hg\bigr).

(ii)

If
{\mathcal{B}}
is étale and
G
acts by étale maps, then the category
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is étale,
and the identification
{\rm Obj}_{\mathcal{B}}=B={\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}}
induces a homeomorphism
|{\mathcal{B}}|/G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}|
, where
G
acts on
|{\mathcal{B}}|
as in Lemma
3.3.3
(i).

Moreover, if
{\mathcal{B}}
is a (possibly étale) groupoid, then
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is an (étale) groupoid with inverse given by

(m,g)^{-1}=\bigl(g^{-1}*m^{-1},g^{-1}\bigr).

(iii)

If
G
also acts on another category
{\mathcal{C}}
, then any
G
-equivariant functor
\phi:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
naturally extends to a functor
\phi^{\times G}:{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\times G}
, given by
\phi^{\times G}(m,g)=(\phi(m),g)
on morphisms.

(iv)

Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be a nonsingular, locally injective, étale category, and consider the
associated
G
action on
its groupoid completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
constructed in Lemma
3.3.3
(iv).
Then
the category
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is an étale groupoid completion of
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
.

(v)

If
G
acts on a groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
by inner automorphisms, then the identity functor
{\iota}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
extends to a functor
{\iota}^{\times G}:{\mathcal{X}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}
given on morphisms by

(3.3.6)

\displaystyle(m,g)\mapsto{\alpha}(g,s(m))\circ m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(g^{-1}*s(m),t(m)),

where
{\alpha}
is as in Lemma
3.3.3
(iii).

Proof.

We first note that the formulas in (i) restrict to the formulas for
{\mathcal{B}}
when
g={\rm id}
.
We next assume that the source and target map are as given, and then check that the composition formula is consistent with the source and target maps, and that it is associative.
To this end, note that
(m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)
is defined only if
t(m)=t(m,g)=s(m^{\prime},h)=h^{-1}*s(m^{\prime})
, which implies

h*t(m)=s(m^{\prime})
. Since
h*t(m)=t(h*m)
by assumption (i), the composite
(h*m)\circ m^{\prime}
is defined.
Further

\displaystyle s\bigl((m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)\bigr)=s(m,g)=g^{-1}*s(m)=(hg)^{-1}h*s(m)=s\bigl((h*m)\circ m^{\prime}\bigr),

\displaystyle t\bigl((m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)\bigr)=t(m^{\prime},h)=t(m^{\prime})=t\bigl((h*m)\circ m^{\prime}\bigr).

Therefore, the composition formula makes sense. To prove associativity
one uses the identity
g*(m\circ m^{\prime})=(g*m)\circ(g*m^{\prime})

to check that

\displaystyle\bigl((m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)\bigr)\circ(m^{\prime\prime},k)

\displaystyle=\bigl((h*m)\circ m^{\prime},\,hg\bigr)\circ(m^{\prime\prime},k)

\displaystyle=\bigl(k*((h*m)\circ m^{\prime})\circ m^{\prime\prime},\,khg\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(k*h*m\circ(k*m^{\prime})\circ m^{\prime\prime},\,khg\bigr)

\displaystyle=(m,g)\circ\bigl((m^{\prime},h)\circ(m^{\prime\prime},k)\bigr).

This proves that
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is a well defined category.

To check (ii), notice that because

G
is a finite group that acts by étale maps by assumption, the structure maps in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
are étale,
which means that
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is étale, as claimed. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{B}}
is a groupoid,
one checks the formula for the inverse by noting
that

(s\times t)\bigl(g^{-1}*m^{-1},g^{-1}\bigr)=(t\times s)(m,g),

and then checking that
the composition formula gives

(g^{-1}*m^{-1},g^{-1}\bigr)\circ(m,g)=\bigl((g*(g^{-1}*m^{-1}))\circ m,{\rm id}\bigr)=\bigl(m^{-1}\circ m,{\rm id}\bigr)=({\rm id}_{t(m)},{\rm id}).

Next, we prove the claim about the realizations of these categories.
Note that if
x\sim_{\mathcal{B}}y
then
x\sim_{{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}}g*y
since we can simply adjoin to the above chain of morphisms in
{\mathcal{B}}
an extra morphism
({\rm id}_{y},g):y\to g*y
.
Therefore there is a well defined map
|{\mathcal{B}}|/G\to|{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}|
, which is necessarily surjective. To see that it is injective,
notice that by using the identities
(m,g)=(m,{\rm id})\circ({\rm id}_{t(m)},g)=({\rm id}_{s(m)},g)\circ(g*m,{\rm id})
, one can convert any chain of morphisms

x:=x_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(m_{1},g_{1})}}{{\rightarrow}}x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(m_{2},g_{2})}}{{\leftarrow}}x_{2}\cdots x_{k-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(m_{k},g_{k})}}{{\rightarrow}}x_{k}:=y

in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
to a chain of morphisms in
{\mathcal{B}}
followed by one morphism of the form
({\rm id}_{z},h)
. It follows that

x\sim_{{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}}y
if and only if there is
h\in G
such that
x\sim_{{\mathcal{B}}}h^{-1}*y
. Thus
h*|x|=|y|
, which proves injectivity.

To prove (iii), define
\phi^{\times G}:{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{B}}\times G}\to{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{C}}\times G}:\ (m,g)\mapsto(\phi(m),g).

This is compatible with source and target maps because

\displaystyle(s\times t)(\phi(m),g)

\displaystyle=\bigl(g^{-1}*s(\phi(m)),t(\phi(m)\bigr)=\bigl(s(g^{-1}*\phi(m)),t(\phi(m))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(s(\phi(g^{-1}*m)),t(\phi(m))\bigr)=\phi\bigl(s(g^{-1}*m),t(m)\bigr),

where in the second equality we use the fact that the source map in
{\mathcal{C}}
is
G
-equivariant (see (
3.3.1
)), and in the third used the fact that the functor
\phi
is
G
-equivariant.
To see that this is compatible with composition, we note:

\displaystyle\phi^{\times G}\bigl((m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\phi^{\times G}\bigl((h*m)\circ m^{\prime},\,hg\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\phi((h*m)\circ m^{\prime}),\,hg\bigr)=\bigl(\phi(h*m)\circ\phi(m^{\prime}),\,hg\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl((h*\phi(m))\circ\phi(m^{\prime}),\,hg\bigr)=(\phi(m),g)\circ(\phi(m^{\prime}),h)

\displaystyle=\phi^{\times G}(m,g)\circ\phi^{\times G}(m^{\prime},h),

where the fourth equality uses the
G
equivariance of
\phi
.
One can check similarly that if
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}}
are groupoids,
\phi^{\times G}
is compatible with inverses. This completes the proof of (iii).

Now consider (iv).
To check that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is an étale groupoid completion of
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
we need to see that the conditions in Definition
3.2.7
hold.
Now (a) and (c) hold because the inclusion
{\iota}:{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
is an étale functor by part (iii) above that induces an homeomorphism on the realization by part (ii) above. Further, (b) holds because
{\mathcal{B}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
are nonsingular so that the
isotropy group
of
{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
and

\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\times G}
at
x\in B
both equal
\{g\in G\ |\ g*x=x\}
. This proves (iv).

Towards (v), note first that because
(s\times t)(m,g)=(g^{-1}*s(m),t(m))
by the definition in (i)
and
(s\times t)({\alpha}(g,x))=(g^{-1}*x,x)

by (
3.3.2
),
the morphisms

(3.3.7)

\displaystyle(m,g)\quad\mbox{ and }\;\;\;{\iota}^{\times G}(m,g)={\alpha}(g,s(m))\circ m

do have the same the source and target.
To check compatibility of
{\iota}^{\times G}
with composition, note that

(m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)

is defined when
t(m,g)=t(m)=s(m^{\prime},h)=h^{-1}*s(m^{\prime})
.
If we write

x:=s(m),\quad h*y:=h*t(m)=s(m^{\prime}),

then because

{\alpha}(hg,h*x)={\alpha}(g,x)\circ{\alpha}(h,h*x)
by (
3.3.2
) we have

\displaystyle{\iota}^{\times G}\bigl((m,g)\circ(m^{\prime},h)\bigr)

\displaystyle={\iota}^{\times G}\bigl((h*m)\circ m^{\prime},hg\bigr)

\displaystyle={\alpha}(hg,s(h*m))\circ(h*m)\circ m^{\prime}\;\;\mbox{ by }\eqref{eq:gpident4}

\displaystyle={\alpha}(hg,h*x)\circ(h*m)\circ m^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigl({\alpha}(g,x)\circ{\alpha}(h,h*x)\bigr)\circ\bigl({\alpha}(h^{-1},x)\circ m\circ{\alpha}(h,h*y)\bigr)\circ m^{\prime},\;\;\mbox{ by }\eqref{eq:gpident3}

\displaystyle={\alpha}(g,x)\circ m\circ{\alpha}(h,h*y)\circ m^{\prime}\;\;\mbox{ by }\eqref{eq:gpident2}

\displaystyle={\iota}^{\times G}(m,g)\circ{\iota}^{\times G}(m^{\prime},h)\;\;\mbox{ by }\eqref{eq:defG}.

Since
{\iota}^{\times G}
preserves identity maps, it is a functor as claimed.
∎

Remark 3.3.9
.

To make sense of the rather complicated formulas in (v) above, notice that the category
{\mathcal{X}}^{\times G}
is formed by adding morphisms to
{\mathcal{X}}
that act essentially by left multiplication by
g\in G
. More precisely, the morphism
{\iota}^{\times G}(m,g)
of
{\mathcal{X}}
first multiplies by
g
and then acts by
m
.
Because by assumption
{\mathcal{X}}
supports an inner action of
G
, there is an action
m\mapsto g*m
of
G
on
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}
as in (
3.3.3
). But this should be thought of as a conjugation action since it acts on both source and target by multiplication by
g
. In fact, it follows readily from (
3.3.3
) that

g*m={\iota}^{\times G}(m,g^{-1})\circ{\iota}^{\times G}({\rm id}_{g*t(m)},g).

In particular, we do not equip the category
{\mathcal{X}}^{\times G}
with a
G
action and do not claim that the functor
{\iota}^{\times G}
is in any sense
G
-equivariant.

{\Diamond}

3.4.
Bundles, sections and structured multisections

We now give a much more thorough treatment of the notion of bundle, as preparation for the discussion of compactness controlling data for polyfold bundles in §
3.5
.
We will generalize
[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.1]
to a notion of bundle over any étale groupoid.
For that purpose we need to add notions of admissible fibers, linear structures, local trivializations, etc to the étale classes (a)-(d) at the beginning of §
3.1
. However, the only instance used in this paper is strong bundles over étale groupoids in the class (d) of M-polyfolds, whose properties were summarized in Definition
2.1.1
and are reviewed in more detail here.
Both this section and the next one can be omitted on first reading.

Definition 3.4.1
.

A
bundle

{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
over an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}

is given by an étale space
W
with continuous maps
32
32

32

Both maps are surjections due to their algebraic properties below:
P(0_{x})=x
and
\mu(1_{P(w)},w)=w
.

P:W\to X
and
\mu:{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W\to W
and the following additional properties and structures:

(i)

Each fiber
P^{-1}(x)
for
x\in X
carries the structure of a vector space, in particular contains a zero vector
0_{x}\in W
with
P(0_{x})=x
. Moreover,
P
is equipped with an equivalence class of compatible local trivializations:

For each
x\in X
there is an injection
\Phi:P^{-1}(V)\hookrightarrow O\times F
covering a chart
\phi:V\overset{\simeq}{\to}O
of an open neighbourhood
V\subset X
of
x
, which preserves fibers
\phi\circ P|_{P^{-1}(V)}={\rm pr}_{O}\circ\Phi
and restricts to bounded linear isomorphisms on fibers, where we make the following requirements in the different étale classes:

(a)

\phi
is the identity map on a metric space
V=O
,
F
is a finite rank vector space,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism to
O\times F
;

(b,c)

\phi
is a chart of the (topological/smooth) manifold
X
,
F
is a finite rank vector space,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism to
O\times F
;

(d)

\phi
is an M-polyfold chart as in §
2.2
,
F
is a sc-Banach space,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism to a strong bundle retract
\bigcup_{u\in O}{\rm im\,}{\Gamma}(u)\subset O\times F
as in §
2.2
and
[
HWZ
, Def.2.6.2]
.

Two such local trivializations
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
are compatible if

\Phi^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}:\Phi(P^{-1}(V^{\prime}\cap V))\to\Phi^{\prime}(P^{-1}(V^{\prime}\cap V))

is a (a),(b) homeomorphism, (c) diffeomorphism, (d) sc-diffeomorphism with respect to each of the two sc-structures
O\times F\to O^{\prime}\times F^{\prime}
and
O\times F_{1}\to O^{\prime}\times F^{\prime}_{1}
.

Two coverings of
W
by atlases of compatible local trivializations are equivalent if all local trivializations in their union are compatible.

(ii)

\mu
is a lift of the target map
t:{\bf X}\to X
in the sense that
P\circ\mu(\cdot,w)=t
for all
w\in W
, it is étale and, in class (d), sc-smooth for each of the two sc-structures on
O\times F
.

(iii)

\mu
is compatible with the linear and groupoidal structures in the sense that

-

\mu(m,\cdot):P^{-1}(s(m))\to P^{-1}(t(m))
is linear for each morphism
m\in{\bf X}
;

-

\mu(1_{x},w)=w
for all
x\in X
and
w\in P^{-1}(x)
;

-

\mu(m\circ m^{\prime},w)=\mu(m^{\prime},\mu(m,w))
for all
m,m^{\prime}\in{\bf X}
with
t(m)=s(m^{\prime})
,
w\in P^{-1}(s(m))
.
33
33

33

Comparing with
[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.1]
note that we write composition of morphisms in categorical notation.

As in
[
HWZ
, §8.3]
, these conditions ensure that

P
lifts to a functor
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
given by

{\mathcal{P}}(w)=P(w),\;{\mathcal{P}}(m,w)=m
, where
{\mathcal{W}}=(W,{\bf W})
is the étale groupoid with
objects
W
and

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle{\bf W}={\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W,\quad(s\times t)(m,w)=(w,\mu(m,w)),

\displaystyle{\rm id}_{w}=({\rm id}_{P(w)},w),\quad(m,w)\circ(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})=\bigl(m^{\prime}\circ m,w\bigr)\quad\mbox{ if }w^{\prime}=\mu(m,w).

The following definition generalizes the notions of sections and bundle maps from the polyfold context
[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.2-4]
to general étale bundles.

Definition 3.4.2
.

Let
(P,\mu)
be a bundle over an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
as in Definition
3.4.1
.

(i)

A
section
of
(P,\mu)
is a map
f:X\to W
that satisfies
P\circ f={\rm id}_{X}
, is compatible with morphisms in the sense that
f(t(m))=\mu(m,f(s(m)))
for all
m\in{\bf X}
, and is étale in the sense that
f
is (a,b) continuous, (c) smooth, (d) sc-smooth.

(ii)

An
étale bundle map
from
(P,\mu)
to another bundle
(P^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
covering an étale functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
to another étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},{\bf X}^{\prime})
is a map
{\Psi:W\to W^{\prime}}
that restricts to linear maps between fibers
\Psi|_{P^{-1}(x)}:P^{-1}(x)\to{P^{\prime}}^{-1}(\psi(x))
and is compatible with morphisms in the sense that
\Psi(\mu(m,w))=\mu^{\prime}(\psi(m),\Psi(w))
for all
(m,w)\in{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W
.
Depending on the étale class, we moreover require that
\Psi
is (a,b) continuous, (c) smooth, (d) sc-smooth with respect to both sc-structures.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

The conditions in Definition
3.4.2
guarantee extensions to functors:

(i)

Any section
f:X\to W
lifts to an étale functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
in the same étale class as
f
. The lifted map on morphisms
{\bf X}\to{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W
is given by
m\mapsto f(m):=\bigl(m,f(s(m))\bigr)
.

(ii)

Any étale bundle map
\Psi:W\to W^{\prime}
lifts to a functor
\Psi:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
that covers
\psi
in the sense that
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\circ\Psi=\psi\circ{\mathcal{P}}
. The lifted map on morphisms
\Psi:{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W\to{\bf X}^{\prime}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P^{\prime}}W^{\prime}
is given by
(m,w)\mapsto(\psi(m),\Psi(w))
. It has the same étaleness properties as
\Psi
; in particular in étale class (d)
\Psi
is a strong bundle map in the sense of §
2.2
.

Proof.

We prove (i) first note that
f
takes identity morphisms to identity morphisms since
f({\rm id}_{x})=({\rm id}_{x},f(x))
by construction and
({\rm id}_{x},f(x))={\rm id}_{f(x)}
by (
3.4.1
).
Further, it follows from the definition of the category
{\mathcal{W}}
in (
3.4.1
) that

s\times t\bigl(f(m)\bigr)=s\times t\bigl(m,f(s(m))\bigr)=\bigl(f(s(m)),\ \mu(s(m),f(s(m))\bigr),

Therefore, if
t(m)=s(m^{\prime})
we have

f(s(m^{\prime}))=f(t(m))=\mu\bigl(s(m),f(s(m))\bigr)=t(f(s(m)))

where the second equality holds by the compatibility condition. Therefore
the composite
f(m)\circ f(m^{\prime})
is defined and by (
3.4.1
) we have

\displaystyle f(m)\circ f(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigl(m,f(s(m))\bigr)\circ\bigl(m^{\prime},f(s(m^{\prime}))\bigr)=\bigl(m\circ m^{\prime},f(s(m))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(m\circ m^{\prime},f(s(m\circ m^{\prime}))\bigr)=f(m\circ m^{\prime}).

This proves (i).
The proof of (ii) is outlined after
[
HWZ
, Def.8.3.2]
.
We check compatitibility of
\Psi:{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W\to{\bf X}^{\prime}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P^{\prime}}W^{\prime}
with source and target maps:

\displaystyle s(\Psi(m,w))=s(\psi(m),\Psi(w))=\Psi(w)=\Psi(s(m,w)),

\displaystyle t(\Psi(m,w))=t(\psi(m),\Psi(w))=\mu^{\prime}(\psi(m),\Psi(w))=\Psi(\mu(m,w))=\Psi(t(m,w)).

Therefore, the composite
\Psi(m,w)\circ\Psi(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is defined whenever
(m,w)\circ(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is defined.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\Psi(m,w)\circ\Psi(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\psi(m),\Psi(w))\circ(\psi(m^{\prime}),\Psi(w^{\prime}))=\bigl(\psi(m)\circ\psi(m^{\prime}),\Psi(w)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\psi(m\circ m^{\prime}),\Psi(w)\bigr)=\Psi(m\circ m^{\prime},w)=\Psi\bigl((m,w)\circ(m^{\prime},w^{\prime})\bigr).

Finall,
\Psi
preserves identity maps because

\Psi({\rm id}_{w})=\Psi\bigl(({\rm id}_{P(w)},w)\bigr)=({\rm id}_{P^{\prime}(\psi(w))},\Psi(w))={\rm id}_{\Psi(w)}.

Hence
\Psi:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
is a functor, as claimed. It satisfies
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\circ\Psi=\psi\circ{\mathcal{P}}
on objects by the given properties of
\Psi:W\to W^{\prime}
and on morphisms by construction.
