Moreover, the functor
\Psi
inherits its étaleness properties directly from the étale bundle map
\Psi
and the underlying étale functor
\psi
.
In particular, in the polyfold case (d), the notion of strong bundle map is satisfied since
\Psi
covers the sc-smooth map
\psi
on the base, is linear on fibers and restricts to a sc-smooth map
W[1]\to W^{\prime}[1]
by Definition
3.4.2
(ii).
∎

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be a bundle as in Definition
3.4.1
given by a pair
(P,\mu)
, and let
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
be a section as in Definition
3.4.2
.

(i)

Every open subset
U\subset X
induces a
restricted bundle

{\mathcal{P}}|_{U}:{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
given by restricting
P
to
W|_{U}:=P^{-1}(U)
and
\mu
to
{\bf X}|_{U}\,{\vphantom{\times}}{}_{s}{\times}_{P}W|_{U}
, where
{\bf X}|_{U}:=s^{-1}(U)\cap t^{-1}(U)
.
The resulting bundle category
{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}
is the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{W}}
with objects
W|_{U}
, and realization
|{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}|=|P|^{-1}(|U|)
.

The section
f
then induces a
restricted section

f|_{U}:{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}\to{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}
given by restricting
f|_{U}:U\to W|_{U}
.

(ii)

Every étale functor
\psi:{\mathcal{Y}}=(Y,{\bf Y})\to{\mathcal{X}}
induces a
pullback bundle

\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
given by

(3.4.2)

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{Y}}:

\displaystyle\;\psi^{*}W:=Y{\vphantom{\times}}_{\psi}{\times}_{P}W\to Y,\quad(y,w)\mapsto y,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}:

\displaystyle\;{\bf Y}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P_{\mathcal{Y}}}\psi^{*}W\to\psi^{*}W,\quad(m,(y,w))\mapsto\bigl(t(m),\mu(\psi(m),w)\bigr).

In particular, if
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is a strong polyfold bundle (i.e. a bundle in the étale category (d), so is the pullback
\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
.

(iii)

The pullback bundle in (ii) has a
pullback bundle functor

\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
that is the functor induced by an étale bundle map, given on objects and morphisms by

(3.4.3)

\displaystyle\psi^{*}W=\{(y,w)\,|\,y\in Y,w\in W_{\psi(y)}\}\to W,\quad(y,w)\mapsto w,

\displaystyle{\bf Y}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P_{\mathcal{Y}}}\psi^{*}W\to{\bf X}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}W,\quad(m,(y,w))\mapsto(\psi(m),w).

It is a
local bundle isomorphism
in the sense that for open subsets
U\subset Y
on which
\psi|_{U}
has an étale inverse, there is an étale bundle map
\Psi|_{U}^{-1}:{\mathcal{W}}|_{\psi(U)}\to\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}
inverse to
\Psi
.

(iv)

Given a pullback bundle as in (ii), the section
f:X\to W
induces a
pullback section

\psi^{*}f:\ Y\to\psi^{*}W
given by
\psi^{*}f(y)=\bigl(y,f(\psi(y))\bigr)
. Correspondingly, there is a pullback section functor

\psi^{*}f:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}
satisfying
\Psi\circ\psi^{*}f=f\circ\psi
.

(v)

Assume in addition that
\psi
is an equivalence in the sense of Definition
3.1.4
(vii). Then
\Psi
in (iii) is an étale bundle equivalence in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.10.4.1]
, that is an étale bundle map and local bundle isomorphism (as above) such that
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is an equivalence of étale groupoids in the sense of Definition
3.1.4
(vii).
In the étale category (d), it is a strong bundle equivalence in the sense that it also restricts to an equivalence

\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}[1]\to{\mathcal{W}}[1]
.
Moreover, in (iv) an equivalence
\psi
induces a homeomorphism
|\psi|:|(\psi^{*}f)^{-1}(0)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|f^{-1}(0)|
.

Proof.

We saw in Remark
3.1.6
that when
U
is open
the restricted category
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
is also étale. Similarly,
{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}
is étale, and has realization

|{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}|=|P|^{-1}(|U|)
. All the other properties
listed in Definition
3.4.1
(i) are local in the base space
X
in the sense that they involve properties of the restriction of
{\mathcal{P}}
to a neighbourhood of a point
x\in X
, and hence they automatically hold for the map
{\mathcal{W}}|_{U}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
. Similarly, because the morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}|_{U}
are defined
to consist of those morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}
with both source and target in
U
,
the properties in Definition
3.4.1
(ii), (iii) automatically hold for the restriction. This proves (i).

To construct the pullback bundle in (ii),
\psi^{*}W=Y{\vphantom{\times}}_{\psi}{\times}_{P}W
is topologized as a subset of
Y\times W
. Then continuity of
P_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
follows from the corresponding properties of
P,\mu
. Similarly, the algebraic properties of
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
in Definition
3.4.1
(ii),(iii) follow from the properties of
\mu
and functoriality of
\psi
.

The fibers
(\psi^{*}W)_{y}=\{(y,w)\,|\,\psi(y)=P(w)\}=\{y\}\times W_{\psi(y)}
are given vector space structures by identification with the fibers of
W
. These identifications fit together into the globally defined and continuous map
\Psi
in (
3.4.3
). So
\Psi
is a bounded isomorphism on fibers by construction. We will use this map to specify the étale structure of the fiber products by finding compatible trivializations as in Definition
3.4.1
(i) – before we can establish the étale property of
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
in (ii).

Given any
y\in Y
, we can find an open neighbourhood
V_{\mathcal{Y}}\subset Y
so that the restriction
\psi|_{V_{\mathcal{Y}}}:V_{\mathcal{Y}}\overset{\simeq}{\to}V\subset X
is a sc-diffeomorphism to its image. We can moreover choose
V_{\mathcal{Y}}
sufficiently small for
V
to support a local trivialization
\Phi:P^{-1}(V)\hookrightarrow O\times F
of
W
that covers an étale chart
\phi:V\overset{\simeq}{\to}O
around
\psi(y)\in X
.
In this neighbourhood,
\Psi|_{\psi^{*}W|_{V_{\mathcal{Y}}}}
is a homeomorphism since its inverse
w\mapsto(\psi^{-1}(P(w)),w)
is continuous. (This also provides the local inverse in (iv).)
The composition
\Phi\circ\Psi:\psi^{*}W|_{V_{\mathcal{Y}}}\to O\times F
then provides a local trivialization of
\psi^{*}W
, and any two such local trivializations are compatible since
\Psi
cancels out so that the transition maps are the same as those for
W
.

To check the étale property of
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
in Definition
3.4.1
(ii) we moreover need to lift the local bundle chart
\Phi\circ\Psi:\psi^{*}W|_{V_{\mathcal{Y}}}\to O\times F
to a chart for the morphism bundle

{\bf Y}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P_{\mathcal{Y}}}\psi^{*}W|_{V_{\mathcal{Y}}}=\bigl\{(m,(y,w))\,|\,m\in s^{-1}(y),y\in V_{\mathcal{Y}},w\in W_{\psi(y)}\bigr\}.

Near a fixed morphism
(m_{0},(y_{0},w_{0}))
, this is done by shrinking
V_{\mathcal{Y}}
further and fixing an inverse of the source map
s_{0}^{-1}:V_{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\bf Y}
centered at
s_{0}^{-1}(y_{0})=m_{0}
. Then a chart for the pullback morphism bundle is obtained by precomposing the local trivialization of
W|_{V}
with the continuous map
\Pi:{\bf Y}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P_{\mathcal{Y}}}\psi^{*}W,(m,(y,w))\mapsto w
, whose local inverse is
w\mapsto(s_{0}^{-1}(y_{w}),y_{w},w)
with
y_{w}:=\psi^{-1}(P(w))
.
In these local charts,
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
takes the form

\displaystyle(\Phi\circ\Psi)\circ\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}\circ(\Phi\circ\Pi)^{-1}:\;\Phi(W_{V})

\displaystyle\to{\bf Y}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P_{\mathcal{Y}}}\psi^{*}W\to\psi^{*}W\to O\times F,

\displaystyle\Phi(w)\mapsto\bigl(s_{0}^{-1}(y_{w}),y_{w},w\bigr)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(t(s_{0}^{-1}(y_{w})),\mu(\psi(s_{0}^{-1}(y_{w})),w)\bigr)\mapsto\Phi\bigl(\mu(s_{{\mathcal{X}},0}^{-1}(P(w)),w)\bigr)

with
s_{{\mathcal{X}},0}^{-1}=\psi\circ s_{0}^{-1}\circ\psi^{-1}:V\to s_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(V)
the local inverse to the source map of
{\mathcal{X}}
obtained by pullback of
s_{0}^{-1}
with
\psi
.
This coincides with
\mu
in the local trivialization
\Phi
(and its lift to morphisms via
s_{{\mathcal{X}},0}^{-1}
), hence
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
inherits all required étaleness properties from the assumed étaleness properties of
\mu
.

To prove (iii), note that
\Psi
on objects is the bundle map (
3.4.3
) which describes the identification of fibers in the construction of the pullback bundle and is used in (ii) to transfer local bundle charts from
W
to
\psi^{*}W
.
So the map
\Psi:\psi^{*}W\to W
in the corresponding local charts of
\psi^{*}W
and
W
is simply represented by the identity map – thus
\Psi
is linear, a bounded isomorphism on fibers, and étale, in particular in class (d) it is sc-smooth in both sc-structures. The same holds for its locally defined inverse – noting that the restriction
\Psi:(\psi^{*}W)|_{U}\to W|_{\psi(U)}
has an étale inverse if and only if the map
\psi|_{U}:U\to\psi(U)
does.
In summary,
\Psi:\psi^{*}W\to W
is a local (strong in class (d) bundle isomorphism.

The same holds on morphisms since – as with all bundle constructions – the map on morphisms is obtained from the map on objects by pullback with the source map. This transfers the étaleness properties to morphism level.
In detail, we apply Lemma
3.4.3
after checking compatibility with morphisms in the sense of Definition
3.4.2
(ii): We have
\Psi(\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}(m,(y,w)))=\mu(y,w))=\mu(\psi(m),\Psi(y,w))
for all
(m,(y,w))\in{\bf Y}{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P_{\mathcal{Y}}}\psi^{*}W
.
Then the lifted map on morphisms is

(m,(y,w))\mapsto(\psi(m),\Psi(y,w))=(\psi(m),w)
as in (
3.4.3
).

This proves (iii) and begins the proof of the first part of (v), where we assume that
\psi
is an equivalence in the sense of Definition
3.1.4
(vii), i.e. it identifies morphisms
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(y,y^{\prime})\simeq{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime}))
and induces a homeomorphism
|\psi|:|{\mathcal{Y}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
.

To check that
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
(and in the polyfold étale class (d), also its restriction
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}[1]\to{\mathcal{W}}[1]
) is an equivalence of étale groupoids we first need to verify that for any given
(y,w),(y^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in\psi^{*}W
(so in class (d), in particular for pairs

(y,w),(y^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in\psi^{*}W[1]
) the map
(m,(y,w))\mapsto(\psi(m),w)
restricts to a bijection from

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}_{\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}}((y,w),(y^{\prime},w^{\prime}))=

\displaystyle\qquad\bigl\{(m,(y,w))\,\big|\,m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{Y}},s(m)=y,t(m)=y^{\prime},\mu(\psi(m),w)=w^{\prime}\bigr\}

to
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\Psi(y,w),\Psi(y^{\prime},w^{\prime}))={\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(w,w^{\prime})
, where

{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(w,w^{\prime})=\bigl\{(n,w)\,\big|\,n\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}},s(n)=\psi(y),t(n)=\psi(y^{\prime}),\mu(n,w)=w^{\prime}\bigr\}.

Since
y,y^{\prime}
and
w,w^{\prime}
are fixed, this is equivalent to
\psi
mapping

\bigl\{m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,y^{\prime})\,|\,\mu(\psi(m),w)=w^{\prime}\bigr\}

bijectively to

\bigl\{n\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime}))\,|\,\mu(n,w)=w^{\prime}\bigr\}.

Here we know that
\psi:{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,y^{\prime})\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime}))
is a bijection, hence its restriction

\bigl\{m\ \,|\,\mu(\psi(m),w)=w^{\prime}\bigr\}\to\bigl\{n\,|\,\mu(n,w)=w^{\prime}\bigr\}

is injective. To check that it is surjective, consider
n\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}(\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime}))
with
\mu(n,w)=w^{\prime}
. From the given bijectivity we know
n=\psi(m)
for some
m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,y^{\prime})
, and can deduce
\mu(\psi(m),w)=\mu(n,w)=w^{\prime}
, which confirms surjectivity of the restricted map.

The second requirement for
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
to be an equivalence of étale groupoids is that the induced map
|\Psi|:|\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}|\to|{\mathcal{W}}|
is a homeomorphism.
This map is continuous maps since
\Psi
is continuous and a functor. To check that
|\Psi|
is bijective, note that the fibers of
|\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}|
resp.
|{\mathcal{W}}|
over points represented by
y\in Y
and
\psi(y)\in X
are the quotients of
\{y\}\times W_{x}
resp.
W_{x}
by the morphisms for fixed
y=y^{\prime}
that we identified above. So the fact that
\Psi
restricts to isomorphisms of fibers
\{y\}\times W_{x}\to W_{x},(y,w)\mapsto w
implies that
|\Psi|
is a bijection on fibers. It moreover covers the homeomorphism
|\psi|:|{\mathcal{Y}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
and hence is globally bijective. Finally,
|\Psi|
is an open map and hence a homeomorphism because the local inverses of
\Psi
in (iii) descend to local inverses of
|\Psi|
– using again the fact that we identified the morphisms above.

In the polyfold class (d), we additionally need
|\Psi|\bigl(|\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}[1]|\bigr)=|{\mathcal{W}}[1]|
to ensure that the restriction of
|\Psi|
induces a homeomorphism
|\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}[1]|\to|{\mathcal{W}}[1]|
.
This is guaranteed by the fact that
\Psi
is a strong bundle map, i.e. preserves the sc-filtration that defines the shift “
[1]
” and has local inverses that are strong bundle maps as well. This proves the first assertion in (v).

Going back to (iv), the map
\psi^{*}f:Y\to\psi^{*}W,y\mapsto\bigl(y,f(\psi(y))\bigr)
satisfies

P_{\mathcal{Y}}\circ(\psi^{*}f)={\rm id}_{Y}
, and is étale because the map
y\mapsto f(\psi(y))
is a composite of étale maps and hence étale. To complete the proof of (iv) it remains to check that
\psi^{*}f
is compatible with morphisms, i.e. that for all morphisms
m^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{Y}}
we have

(\psi^{*}f)(t(m^{\prime}))=\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}\bigl(m^{\prime},(\psi^{*}f)(s(m^{\prime}))\bigr)\in\psi^{*}W.

Since
f
is compatible with morphisms we know

f(t(m))=\mu_{\mathcal{X}}\bigl(m,f(s(m))\bigr)
for all morphisms
m
in
{\mathcal{X}}
, where for clarity we write
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
for the operator
\mu
in the bundle
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
.
Therefore, using the definition of
\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}
in (
3.4.2
)
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Y}}\bigl(m^{\prime},(\psi^{*}f)(s(m^{\prime}))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(t(m^{\prime}),\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(\psi(m^{\prime}),f(\psi(s(m^{\prime})))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(t(m^{\prime}),\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(\psi(m^{\prime}),f(s(\psi(m^{\prime})))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(t(m^{\prime}),\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(m,f(s(m))\bigr)\quad\mbox{ where }m:=\psi(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigl(t(m^{\prime}),f(t(\psi(m^{\prime})))\bigr)\quad\mbox{ since $f$ is a section}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{*}f)(t(m^{\prime})),

where the last equality uses the fact that
t(\psi(m^{\prime}))=\psi(t(m^{\prime}))
and the definition
\psi^{*}(f)(y)=(y,f(\psi(y))
. That proves (iv).

Finally, we prove the second part of (v). By definition, an equivalence
\psi
induces a homeomorphism
|\psi|:|{\mathcal{Y}}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
. So to establish the homeomorphism
|\psi|:|(\psi^{*}f)^{-1}(0)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|f^{-1}(0)|
it remains to show that the image of
|(\psi^{*}f)^{-1}(0)|
under
|\psi|
is
|f^{-1}(0)|
. This is guaranteed by the fact that the pullback construction gives
y\in(\psi^{*}f)^{-1}(0)\Leftrightarrow\psi(y)\in f^{-1}(0)
. This completes the proof.
∎

To end this subsection, we discuss multisections and their structurability. As we will see, these are generally needed
to achieve transversely cut out perturbed solution sets
in the presence of isotropy.

Definition 3.4.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be an étale bundle. A
multisection
of
{\mathcal{P}}
is a functor

(3.4.4)

\displaystyle\Lambda:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}:={\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,\infty),

that can in local charts
W|_{V}
be represented as

\Lambda(w)=\sum_{\{i\,|\,w={\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(P(w))\}}\sigma_{i}

in terms of finitely many étale sections
34
34

34

The
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
here are simply maps
V\to W
with no functorial properties, rather than sections of bundles as in Definition
3.4.2
.

{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}:V\to W|_{V}
and weights
\sigma_{i}>0
.

A
global section structure
for a multisection functor
{\Lambda}
of a bundle
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is a finite family of sections
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i}:X\to W)_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
such that

(3.4.5)

\displaystyle{\Lambda}(w)=\tfrac{1}{|{\mathcal{I}}|}\#\{i\in{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(P(w))=w\}.

A multisection functor
{\Lambda}
is called
globally structured

if it is equipped with a global section structure
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
and a global natural correspondence

{\kappa}\,:\;{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to\{b:{\mathcal{I}}\to{\mathcal{I}}\;\text{bijection}\}

satisfying

(i)

{\kappa}
is locally constant;

(ii)

for all
m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

(3.4.6)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}_{{\kappa}(m)(i)}(t(m))=\mu(m,{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(s(m)))\in W_{t(m)}.

Definition 3.4.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be an étale proper category. A collection of open neighbourhoods
(U(x))_{x\in X}
of the points
x\in X
is called a
good system of neighbourhoods
if

-

for every
x\in X
the target map
t:s^{-1}({\rm cl}_{X}(U(x))\to X
is proper;

-

each
U(x)
is equipped with the natural action of the isotropy group
G_{x}
at
x
;

-

for any pair
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and each connected component
{\Sigma}
of
{\rm Mor}(U(x),U(x^{\prime}))
the maps
s:{\Sigma}\to U(x)
and
t:{\Sigma}\to U(x^{\prime})
are étale bijections onto their images.

This system is said to be
connected
if each set
U(x)
is connected.

Lemma 3.4.7
.

If
(U(x))_{x\in X}
is a good system of neighbourhoods, and
U(x^{\prime})\subset U(x)
is any

G_{x}
-invariant open subset of
U(x)
, then

(U^{\prime}(x))_{x\in X}
is a good system of neighbourhoods. In particular,
any étale category with a good system of neighbourhoods also has a good connected system
of neighbourhoods. Further, the intersection
(U(x)\cap U^{\prime}(x))_{x\in X}
of any two good systems
of neighbourhoods is also a good system of neighbourhoods.

Proof.

The first claim is immediate. To prove the second claim, given any good system of neighbourhoods
(U(x))_{x\in X}
, define
U^{\prime}(x)
to be the connected component of
U(x)
containing
x
. Then
U^{\prime}(x)
is
G_{x}
-invariant, and the other conditions are immediate.
∎

Lemma 3.4.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be an étale proper category with a
good system of neighbourhoods and let
{\Lambda}
be a multisection with a global structure provided by
the sections
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
and correspondence
{\kappa}
.
For
x\in X
, let
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}
be the quotient of
{\mathcal{I}}
by the equivalence relation
i\sim_{x}j
if
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(y)={\mathfrak{s}}_{j}(y)
for all
y
near
x
, and denote the quotient map by
{\rm pr}_{x}:{\mathcal{I}}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{x}
.
Then we may arrange that the following conditions hold.

(a)

Each
U(x)
is connected and
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}={\mathcal{I}}_{y}
for all
y\in U(x)
.
Moreover
i\sim_{x}j
exactly if there is
z\in U(x)
such that
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(z)={\mathfrak{s}}_{j}(z)
.

(b)

For all
x\in X
and all composable pairs of morphisms
m,m^{\prime}
with
s(m)\in U(x)
, we have

(3.4.7)

\displaystyle{\kappa}(m\circ m^{\prime})(i)\sim_{x}{\kappa}(m^{\prime})\circ{\kappa}(m)(i),\quad\forall i\in{\mathcal{I}}.

(c)

The action of the isotropy group
G_{x}
on
{\mathcal{I}}
given by
{\kappa}
descends to an action on
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}
via the identity

g*{\underline{i}}={\rm pr}_{x}\bigl({\kappa}(m_{g})(r_{x}(i))\bigr)\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x},\quad

where
m_{g}\in{\rm Mor}(x,x)=G_{x}
is the morphism
g\in G_{x}
, and
r_{x}:{\mathcal{I}}_{x}\to{\mathcal{I}}
is any right inverse to
{\rm pr}_{x}
. In particular,
{\rm pr}_{x}
is
G_{x}
-equivariant,

Proof.

First notice that
if
(U(x))_{x\in X}
is a good system of neighbourhoods, and
U(x^{\prime})\subset U(x)
is any

G_{x}
-invariant open subset of
U(x)
, then

(U^{\prime}(x))_{x\in X}
is a good system of neighbourhoods. Therefore we may assume that each
U(x)
is connected by replacing it by its connected component containing
x
. Further, it
follows immediately from the definition that if
i\sim_{x}j
then
i\sim_{y}j
for all
y
in some open neighbourhood of
x
.
Therefore, because
{\mathcal{I}}
is a finite set, the two equivalence relations
\sim_{x},\sim_{y}
must agree on some
G_{x}
-invariant neighbourhood of
x
. Therefore we can shrink the
U(x)
so that the first claim in (i) holds. The second claim in (i) holds because
{\mathcal{I}}
is finite, and
i\not\sim_{x}j
if and only if there is
z
near
x
such that
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(z)\neq{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}(z)
.

Towards (b), notice first that the equivalence in (
3.4.7
) holds when
s(m)=x
because

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}_{{\kappa}(m\circ m^{\prime})(i)}(t(m\circ m^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mu\bigr(m\circ m^{\prime},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(s(m\circ m^{\prime}))\bigl)

\displaystyle=\mu\bigr(m^{\prime},\mu(m,{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(s(m))\bigl)

\displaystyle=\mu\bigr(m^{\prime},{\mathfrak{s}}_{{\kappa}(m)i}(t(m))\bigl)\quad\mbox{by }\ \eqref{eq:global1}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{s}}_{{\kappa}(m^{\prime})\circ{\kappa}(m)(i)}(t(m^{\prime})).

Since
{\kappa}
is locally constant by definition and the relation
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}
is locally constant by (a),
this identity continues to hold when we vary the composable pair
m,m^{\prime}
so that
s(m)
is close to
x
.
This proves (b).

Note finally that (c) holds because (
3.4.6
) and part (a) above together imply that
the
G_{x}
action on
{\mathcal{I}}
preserves the relation
\equiv_{x}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.9
.

(i) If we assume given
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
and
{\kappa}
satisfying the conclusions of
Lemma
3.4.8
, and then
define
{\Lambda}
by (
3.4.5
), the fact that
{\Lambda}
is a functor follows from
(
3.4.6
).

(ii) In general it is not true that the correspondence
{\kappa}
in Definition
3.4.5

is compatible with composition, that is there could be a composable pair

m,m^{\prime}
of morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}
such that the two bijections

{\kappa}(m\circ m^{\prime}),{\kappa}(m)\circ{\kappa}(m^{\prime}):{\mathcal{I}}\to{\mathcal{I}}
are different. However by
Lemma
3.4.8
(b) this does hold locally modulo the equivalence relation
\sim_{x}
.

{\Diamond}

We now rephrase Lemma
3.4.8
in the language used in
[
HWZ
]
. As above, we denote by
r_{x}:{\mathcal{I}}_{x}\to{\mathcal{I}}
any map satisfying
\pi_{x}(r_{x}({\underline{i}}))={\underline{i}},\ \forall\ {\underline{i}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x}
.

Lemma 3.4.10
.

In the situation of Lemma
3.4.8
, the following holds.

(a)

\bigl(U(x),({\mathfrak{s}}_{r_{x}({\underline{i}})})_{{\underline{i}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x}}\bigr)
is a symmetric section structure for
{\Lambda}
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.2.5]
.

(b)

For each pair
x,x^{\prime}\in X
the map

\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}:{\rm Mor}(U(x),U(x^{\prime}))\to{\rm Biject}({\mathcal{I}}_{x},{\mathcal{I}}_{x^{\prime}}),\quad m\mapsto{\rm pr}_{x^{\prime}}\circ{\kappa}(m)\circ r_{x}

is a correspondence in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.4]
between the local structures

\bigl(U(x),({\mathfrak{s}}_{r_{x}({\underline{i}})})_{{\underline{i}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x}}\bigr)
and
\bigl(U(x^{\prime}),({\mathfrak{s}}_{r_{x^{\prime}}({\underline{j}})})_{{\underline{j}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x^{\prime}}}\bigr)
.

Proof.

To prove (a), we need to check the two conditions in
[
HWZ
, Def.13.2.5]
. The second condition follows immediately from (
3.4.5
) and the fact that
i\sim_{x}j
implies that
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(y)={\mathfrak{s}}_{j}(y)
for all
y\in U(x)
so that
{\mathfrak{s}}_{r_{x}({\underline{i}})}(y)
is independent of the choice of lift
r_{x}:{\mathcal{I}}_{x}\to{\mathcal{I}}
. The first condition is written in our notation as

{\mathfrak{s}}_{g*{\underline{i}}}(g*y)=\mu(m_{g,y},{\mathfrak{s}}_{r_{x}(i)}(y)),\quad\forall\ y\in U(x),\ {\underline{i}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x},\ g\in G_{x},

where
m_{g,y}\in{\rm Mor}(U(x),U(x))
is the morphism
y\mapsto g*y
. This is an immediate consequence of (
3.4.6
).

To prove (b), we must check the three conditions in
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.4]
. The first condition, i.e. local constancy of
\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}
, holds because the map
{\kappa}
in (
3.4.6
) is locally constant.
35
35

35

In view of the notation
\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}
used in
[
HWZ
]
for these local correspondences, it would have been more natural to write
\tau
instead of
{\kappa}
for the global correspondence in Definition
3.4.5
. However, this might cause confusion with the use of
\tau
to denote the functor
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
in Theorem
2.1.2
.

The third condition holds because it is a restatement of (
3.4.6
). The second condition
states that
\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}
is compatible with the actions of
G_{x},G_{x^{\prime}}
on
U(x),U(x^{\prime})
.
This can be written as

\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}(g*m*g^{\prime})=g^{\prime}*\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}(m)*g:{\mathcal{I}}_{x}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{x^{\prime}},

for all
m\in{\rm Mor}(U(x),U(x^{\prime})),\ g\in G_{x},\ g^{\prime}\in G_{x^{\prime}},

where on the left hand aside
g*m*g^{\prime}
is the morphism that first multiplies by
g
then does
m
and then multiplies by
g^{\prime}
,
and on the right denote by
g
(resp.
g^{\prime}
) the map
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{x}:{\underline{i}}\mapsto g*{\underline{i}}

(resp.
{\mathcal{I}}_{x^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{x^{\prime}}:{\underline{j}}\mapsto g^{\prime}*{\underline{j}}
). Again, this identity is a straightforward consequence of the
compatibility of
{\kappa}
with composition stated in (
3.4.7
).
∎

Here is the main result about multisections.

Proposition 3.4.11
.

If
{\mathcal{X}}
has a good system of neighbourhoods
(U(x))_{x\in X}
, then
every globally structured multisection
({\Lambda},({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}},{\kappa})
is structurable in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.6]
. Moreover
{\Lambda}
determines a unique structured multisection
[{\Lambda},{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{S}},\tau]
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.8]
.

Proof.

The first claim holds provided that the maps
\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}
defined in Lemma
3.4.10
(b) above satisfy the additional identities

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle\qquad\tau_{x,x}(m_{g,y})({\underline{i}})=g*{\underline{i}},\quad\forall g\in G_{x},y\in U(x),{\underline{i}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{x},

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)}

\displaystyle\qquad\tau_{x^{\prime},x}(m)=\bigr(\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}(m^{-1})\bigl)^{-1},\quad\forall m\in{\rm Mor}(U(x^{\prime}),U(x)),

where the morphism
m_{g,y}:y\to g*y
in (i) implements the action of
g
.
36
36

36

These identities are simplified forms of those in
[
HWZ
]
because we consider
{\kappa}
and the associated correspondences
\tau_{x,x^{\prime}}
to take values in the set of bijections
{\mathcal{I}}\to{\mathcal{I}}
, rather than in the set of correspondences
{\mathcal{I}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathcal{I}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{I}}
as
in
[
HWZ
]
.

Both identities follow immediately from our definitions. This proves the first claim.

By
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.6]
a
structured multisection
is an equivalence class of tuples
({\Lambda},{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{S}},\tau)
where
{\Lambda}
is a multisection,
{\mathcal{U}}
is a good system of neighbourhoods,
{\mathcal{S}}
is a collection of local sections that determine
{\Lambda}
via local versions of (
3.4.5
), and
\tau=(\tau_{x,x^{\prime}})
is a family of correspondences as in Lemma
3.4.10
.
Since any two good systems of neighbourhoods
{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
are equivalent under restriction,
and the collection
{\mathcal{S}}
is uniquely determined up to equivalence
by restricting the fixed sections
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
,
it follows straightforwardly from the definitions in
[
HWZ
]
that the equivalence class

[{\Lambda},{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{S}},\tau]
of this tuple is unique.
∎

3.5.
Compactness control

We specify to the polyfold étale class (d) for this subsection.
Since polyfolds are typically infinite dimensional, we cannot expect to find precompact neighbourhoods of
|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
. Instead, compactness of the perturbed solution sets will be achieved by the following notions from
[
HWZ
, §12.2, §15.3]
.

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
be a sc-Fredholm section functor of a strong bundle
{{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}}
as in Definition
2.1.1
.

(i)

An
auxiliary norm
on
{\mathcal{W}}
is a continuous map
N:W[1]\to[0,\infty)
compatible with morphisms (i.e.
N(s(m))=N(t(m))
for
m\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{W}}
), which restricts to a complete norm on each fiber
W[1]\cap P^{-1}(x)
, and such that
N(h_{k})\to 0,P(h_{k})\to x\in X
implies convergence
h_{k}\to 0_{x}
in the topology of
W[1]
specified in §
2.2
.

(ii)

A
saturated open neighbourhood of the solution category
is an open subset
{\mathcal{U}}\subset X
that contains
f^{-1}(0):=\{x\in X\,|\,f(x)=0_{x}\}
.

(iii)

A pair
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
of data as above
controls the compactness of
\mathbf{f}
if the closure of

\bigl|\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\,|\,f(x)\in W[1],N(f(x))\leq 1\}\bigr|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is compact.

Remark 3.5.2
.

Compactness of
|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
implies the existence of compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
by
[
HWZ
, Thm.12.4.2]
(since we assume paracompactness of
|{\mathcal{X}}|
in Definition
2.1.1
).
This will allow to preserve compactness when perturbing the solution set.

To compare perturbed solution sets arising from different choices of compactness control data it is useful to note the following:
Given compactness control data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
for
f
, any pair
(N^{\prime},{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})
of a smaller saturated open set
f^{-1}(0)\subset{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
and larger auxiliary norm
N^{\prime}\geq N
also controls compactness of
f
. This is since
\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\,|\,N^{\prime}(f(x))\leq 1\}
is contained in
\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\,|\,N(f(x))\leq 1\}
, and hence the closure of
\bigl|\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\,|\,f(x)\in W[1],N^{\prime}(f(x))\leq 1\}\bigr|
is a closed subset of the compact set
{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}\bigl(\bigl|\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\,|\,f(x)\in W[1],N(f(x))\leq 1\}\bigr|\bigr)
, and thus compact.

In particular, if
V\subset X
is a saturated open subset that contains
f^{-1}(0)
, then we can choose compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})
for
f
with
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset V
by applying the above observation to any choice of compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{U}}\cap V
.

{\Diamond}

The pullback constructions in Lemma
3.4.4
are compatible with the sc-Fredholm notion and compactness control as follows.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Suppose that
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is a sc-Fredholm section functor of a strong bundle
{{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}}
and that
\psi:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
is an equivalence of ep-groupoids in the sense of Definition
3.1.2
(vii), where
{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
denotes the restriction to an open (but not necessarily saturated)
subset
V\subset X
as in Lemma
3.4.4
(i). Then
\psi^{*}f:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}
is sc-Fredholm.

If, moreover, the zero set
|f^{-1}(0)|
is compact and contained in
|V|
, then we can find compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
for
f
as in Definition
3.5.1
with
|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})|\subset|V|
.
And given such data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
, the pullback data
\bigl(N_{\mathcal{Y}}:=N\circ\Psi,\ {\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\psi^{-1}({\mathcal{U}})\bigr)
controls the compactness of
\psi^{*}f
, where
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is the pullback bundle functor from Lemma
3.4.4
(iii).

Proof.

The sc-Fredholm property
[
HWZ
, Def.12.1.1]
of the functor
\psi^{*}f
just requires the sc-Fredholm property
[
HWZ
, Def.3.1.16]
for the section of the object bundle
\psi^{*}f:Y\to\psi^{*}W,y\mapsto\bigl(y,f(\psi(y))\bigr)
. The three properties encoded in the sc-Fredholmness of
f
— (1) sc-smoothness, (2) regularization
f(x)\in W_{m,m+1}\Rightarrow x\in X_{m+1}
, (3) local coordinates for base and bundle in which the section has a contraction form — are all local properties on the base. So to check that these properties transfer to
\psi^{*}f
it suffices to consider domains on which
\psi
restricts to a sc-diffeomorphism. On such domains they follow – as in
[
HWZ
, Thm.10.4.5]
– from (1) the chain rule in scale calculus, (2) the fact that sc-maps preserve the sc-indices, (3) composing local coordinates for the base with
\psi
.

Now assume that
|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|V|
is compact.
To find compactness controlling data with
|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})|\subset|V|
recall that
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is metric and
{\rm im\,}|\psi|=|V|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is an open subset that contains
|f^{-1}(0)|
. So, since
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is a normal topological space, we can find an open neighbourhood
N_{S}\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
of the closed subset
S=|f^{-1}(0)|
whose closure does not intersect the closed subset
|{\mathcal{X}}|{\smallsetminus}|V|
. Its preimage
V^{\prime}:=\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(N_{S})\subset{\rm Obj}_{\mathcal{X}}
is a saturated open neighbourhood of
f^{-1}(0)
, so as in Remark
3.5.2
we can choose compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
for
f
with
{\mathcal{U}}\subset V^{\prime}
. This construction gives the desired closure control – using continuity of
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
–

|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})|\subset|{\rm cl}_{X}\bigl(V^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(N_{S})\bigr)|\subset|{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(N_{S})|\subset|V|.

Next, let
N:{\mathcal{W}}[1]\to[0,\infty)
be an auxiliary norm. Then we claim that
N_{\mathcal{Y}}:=N\circ\Psi
defines an auxiliary norm
\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}[1]\to[0,\infty)
as in Definition
3.5.1
.
Indeed, it is continuous since
N
and
\Psi
are continuous, compatible with morphisms since
\Psi
is a functor, and restricts to a complete norm on each fiber since pullback bundle functor
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
from Lemma
3.4.4
(iii) restricts to a strong sc-isomorphism on each fiber.
The convergence property above (and
[
HWZ
, Def.12.2.1 (2)]
) is preserved under pullback:
Given
(y_{n},w_{n})\in{\rm Obj}_{\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}}[1]=Y{\vphantom{\times}}_{\psi}{\times}_{P}W[1]
with
P_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{n},w_{n})=y_{n}\to y\in Y
and

N_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{n},w_{n})=N(w_{n})\to 0
we have
w_{n}\in{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}}[1]
with
P(w_{n})=\psi(y_{n})\to\psi(y)\in X
since
\psi
is continuous. Then convergence of the auxiliary norm
N(w_{n})\to 0
implies
w_{n}\to 0_{\psi(y)}
in
{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}}[1]
, which implies the required convergence
(y_{n},w_{n})\to(y,0_{\psi(y)})=0_{y}
in
{\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{Y}}}[1]
.

Finally, to check that
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\psi^{-1}({\mathcal{U}})\subset Y
together with
N_{\mathcal{Y}}
controls compactness of
\psi^{*}f
as in Definition
3.5.1
, first note that
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is open since
\psi
is continuous, saturated since
\psi
is a functor and
{\mathcal{U}}
is saturated in
{\mathcal{X}}
, and a neighbourhood of
(\psi^{*}f)^{-1}(0)
since
|\psi|
identifies the zero sets and
|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
. Now it remains to show that

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\{y\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\subset Y\,\big|\,N_{\mathcal{Y}}(\psi^{*}f(y))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\bigl\{y\in Y\,\big|\,\psi(y)\in{\mathcal{U}},N(f(\psi(y)))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\psi^{-1}\bigl\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\subset X\,\big|\,N(f(x))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|

\displaystyle=|\psi|^{-1}\bigl|\bigl\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\subset X\,\big|\,N(f(x))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|

has compact closure in
|{\mathcal{Y}}|
. Since
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
controls compactness of
f
, we know that
B_{f}:=\bigl|\bigl\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\subset X\,\big|\,N(f(x))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|
has compact closure in
|{\mathcal{X}}|
. Moreover,
|\psi|:|{\mathcal{Y}}|\to|V|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is a homeomorphism by Definition
3.1.4
(vii).
So
|\psi|
intertwines closures and maps compact sets to compact sets as long as we can guarantee that the closure of
B_{f}
is contained in
|V|
. This is why we chose
{\mathcal{U}}\subset X
with
|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})|\subset|V|
, as this guarantees

{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(B_{f})\subset{\rm cl}_{|{\mathcal{X}}|}(|{\mathcal{U}}|)=\bigl|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})\bigr|\subset|V|
,
where we used Lemma
3.1.8
(ii) to identify closures.
Thus
(N_{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
controls compactness of
\psi^{*}f
.
∎

To activate the compactness control when perturbing a sc-Fredholm section
f:X\to W
over an M-polyfold
X
, we can now restrict perturbations to sc
+
-sections
s:X\to W[1]
that are bounded by
N(s(x))\leq 1
(or in fact any other constant by
[
HWZ
, Thm.12.4.3]
), so that
the perturbed zero set

\bigl|(f-s)^{-1}(0)\bigr|=\bigl|\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\,|\,f(x)=s(x)\}\bigr|\subset\bigl|\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\,|\,f(x)\in W[1],N(f(x))\leq 1\}\bigr|

is automatically compact.

Next, the presence of isotropy in any étale proper groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
generally requires the use of multivalued perturbations to achieve transversely cut out perturbed solution sets.
In the case of a sc-Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
over an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
, we additionally need sc
+
-perturbations, leading to the formalism of
[
HWZ
, §13.2]
: A
sc
+
-multisection
is a functor

(3.5.1)

\displaystyle\Lambda:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}:={\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,\infty),

which can in local charts
W|_{V}
be represented as

\Lambda(w)=\sum_{\{i\,|\,w={\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(P(w))\}}\sigma_{i}

in terms of finitely many sc
+
-sections
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}:V\to W[1]|_{V}
and weights
\sigma_{i}>0
.
The composition of such a multisection
\Lambda:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
with
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
yields a functor
\Lambda\circ f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
, whose support

S_{\Lambda\circ f}:=\{x\in X\,|\,\Lambda(f(x))>0\}\subset X

represents the perturbed solution set.
Indeed, we have
V\cap S_{\Lambda\circ f}=\bigcup_{i}\{x\in V\,|\,f(x)={\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(x)\}
locally.
Moreover, each “branch”
(f-{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})^{-1}(0)
has a natural weight
\sigma_{i}
, and these add up to a weighting function
\Lambda\circ f|_{S_{\Lambda\circ f}}
.
Transversality of these multivalued perturbations as in
[
HWZ
, §15.2]
requires transversality of each local section
f-{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
, and requirements of compactness control and transversality are gathered in the following notion of regularity.

Definition 3.5.4
.

Let
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
be a sc-Fredholm section functor of a strong bundle
{{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}}
as in Definition
2.1.1
, and suppose
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
controls the compactness of
f
as in Definition
3.5.1
.
Then a sc
+
-multisection
\Lambda:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
as in (
3.5.1
)
is called
\mathbf{(}N,{\mathcal{U}})
-regular
(as a perturbation of
f
) if it satisfies
37
37

37

This is a special case of
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
-admissibility
[
HWZ
, Def.15.3.3]
used in
[
HWZ
, Cor.15.3.10]
.
:

(1)

N(\Lambda)<1
, which is shorthand for:
N(w)<1
for all
w\in W
with
\Lambda(w)>0
.

(2)

\text{dom-supp}(\Lambda)\subset{\mathcal{U}}
, which is shorthand for:

the closure of the set
\{x\in X\,|\,\exists w\in P^{-1}(x){\smallsetminus}\{0\}:\Lambda(w)>0\}
is contained in
{\mathcal{U}}
.

(3)

{\rm T}_{(f,\Lambda)}
is surjective on
{\rm supp\,}(\Lambda\circ f)
, which is shorthand for:

each linearization
{\rm D}(f-{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})(x)
at a solution
f(x)={\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(x)
of a local section
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}

representing
\Lambda
is surjective; see
[
HWZ
, Def.15.2.1]
.

If
{\mathcal{X}}
has nonempty boundary, then we require in addition:

(3a)

nothing;

(3b)

the kernel of each linearization at a solution
x\in\partial X
is in good position
[
HWZ
, Def.3.1.24]
to the partial quadrant in a chart around
x
;

(3c)

the kernel of each linearization at a solution
x\in\partial X
is in general position
[
HWZ
, Def.5.3.9]
to the partial quadrant in a chart around
x
.

The results in this paper hold for all three versions (3a–c) of boundary transversality when e.g. regularity is transferred under pullback.
Perturbation constructions for a single moduli space can usually achieve the strongest condition (3c), and so do ours. The less strict condition (3b) only becomes relevant for the coherent perturbation of a collection of moduli spaces, whose Cartesian products form each others’ boundaries, as discussed in
[
FH
]
.
Either condition (3b) or (3c) induces a tame smooth structure on the perturbed solution set in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.9.3.6]
: The composition of a sc-Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
and a regular sc
+
-multisection
\Lambda:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a branched ep
+
-subgroupoid
\Lambda\circ f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
.

4.
General construction principles

The purpose of this section is to develop general constructions of étale categories, their groupoid completions, and group actions in the context that will be relevant for the proof of Theorem
2.1.2
but is valid for any of the étale classes discussed in §
3.1
. We also discuss the structure of multisections. Section
4.1
states the main results and explains their relevance to Theorem
2.1.2

and Corollary
2.1.3
, while subsequent subsections give the proofs.
The key fact that makes our later constructions possible is the simple structure of the category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
. This is the groupoid completion of a poset in which each equivalence class has a unique element with morphisms to all the other equivalent elements; see Proposition
4.1.2
(i). This permits the construction of the stabilization functor
\tau
in Proposition
4.1.5
and Theorem
5.4.2
.

4.1.
The structure of the categories
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}

The étale proper groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}
in
Theorem
2.1.2
is generated by étale data of the following type, called for short
étale data of type
V
.

(a)

G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
is a finite collection of finite groups.
Then for
I\subset\{1,\dots,N\}=:A

we denote
G_{I}:=\prod_{i\in I}G_{i}
, for short writing
G:=G_{A}:=\prod_{i\in A}G_{i}
.
For
I\subset J
38
38

38

Here, as elsewhere, the notation
I\subset J
includes the possibility that
I=J
.
we consider
G_{I}\subset G_{J}
as natural subgroup and write
G_{J}\to G_{I},g\mapsto g|_{I}
for the natural projection.

(b)

V_{J}
is a (possibly empty) étale space equipped with an étale action of
G_{J}
for each
J\subset A
.

(c)

{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\subset V_{J}
for each
I\subset J
is a
G_{J}
-invariant open subset
on which
G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}
acts freely,
and
\rho_{IJ}:{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\to V_{I}
is a composite
{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\to\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}$}}\to V_{I}
of the quotient map with an injective étale map with open image
V_{IJ}:=\rho_{IJ}({\widetilde{V}}_{IJ})\subset V_{I}
with the following properties:

(identity)
For all
I\subset A
we have
{\widetilde{V}}_{II}=V_{II}=V_{I}
and
\rho_{II}={\rm id}
.

(separation)

For
H\subset I,J
we have
{\rm cl}(V_{HI})\cap{\rm cl}(V_{HJ})=\emptyset
unless
I\subset J
or
J\subset I
.

(separation)
For
H,I\subset J
we have
{\rm cl}({\widetilde{V}}_{IJ})\cap{\rm cl}({\widetilde{V}}_{HJ})=\emptyset
unless
I\subset H
or
H\subset I
.

(composition)

For
H\subset I\subset J
we have
{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\cap{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}=\rho_{IJ}^{-1}({\widetilde{V}}_{HI}\cap V_{IJ})
and

\rho_{HJ}|_{{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\cap{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}}=\rho_{HI}\circ\rho_{IJ}.

(equivariance)

\rho_{IJ}:{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\to V_{I}
is equivariant with respect to the projection
G_{J}\to G_{I}
.

(closed graph)
for all
I\subset J
,
{\rm graph}(\rho_{IJ})\subset V_{J}\times V_{I}
is closed.

Before explaining how to build the étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
in Theorem
2.1.2
from data of this kind, we introduce the following notation.

Here and throughout,
we often write
\rho_{I}:=\rho_{I\bullet}:{\widetilde{V}}_{I\bullet}\to V_{I}
, where
\bullet
stands for any
J\supset I
.
For any
I\subset J
, we also write
\rho_{J}:{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\to V_{J}
for the inclusion map.
We define:

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle I\vee J:=\begin{cases}\max\{I,J\}=J\,,&I\subset J;\\
\max\{I,J\}=I\,,&J\subset I;\\
\emptyset\,,&\text{else};\end{cases}\quad I\wedge J:=\begin{cases}\min\{I,J\}=I\,,&I\subset J;\\
\min\{I,J\}=J\,,&J\subset I;\\
\emptyset\,,&\text{else};\end{cases}.

Further, we say that the subsets
I_{1},\dots,I_{k}
are
nested
if they may be rearranged into an increasing chain

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle I_{\ell_{1}}\subset I_{\ell_{2}}\subset\cdots\subset I_{\ell_{k}}.

In particular, we write
I\sim J
if
I,J
are nested. Note that
the sets
I\wedge J
and
I\vee J
are empty unless
I\sim J
.
More generally, for nested sets
I_{1},\dots,I_{k}
we define

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle I_{1}\vee\cdots\vee I_{k}=\max\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\},\quad I_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge I_{k}=\min\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}.

As above, if
I_{1},\dots,I_{k}
are not nested both
I_{1}\vee\cdots\vee I_{k}
and
I_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge I_{k}
are defined to be empty.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Étale data of type
V

as in (a)–(c) above
determines an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}=(X_{\mathcal{V}},{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}})
with

\displaystyle X_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset A}V_{I},

\displaystyle\qquad s(I,J,y,g)

\displaystyle:=(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y)),

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\sim J}{\widetilde{V}}_{(I\wedge J)(I\vee J)}\times G_{I\wedge J},

\displaystyle\qquad t(I,J,y,g)

\displaystyle:=(J,\rho_{J}(y)),

\displaystyle{\rm id}_{(I,x)}

\displaystyle:=(I,I,x,{\rm id}),

\displaystyle\qquad(I,J,y,g)^{-1}

\displaystyle:=(J,I,g^{-1}*y,g^{-1}),

where we use the notation
(I,x):=x\in V_{I}
for objects and
(I,J,y,g):=(y,g)\in{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\times G_{I\wedge J}
for morphisms.
If
t(I,J,x,g)=s(J,K,y,h)
, then
I,J,K
are necessarily nested, and we define the composition by

(4.1.4)

\displaystyle(I,J,x,g)\circ(J,K,y,h):=\bigl(I,K,\rho_{I\vee K}(v),k(hg)|_{I\wedge K}\bigr),

where
(v,k)\in V_{I\vee J\vee K}\times G_{I\wedge K}

is
given as follows
39
39

39

For an explanation of these formulas see Remark
4.2.5
.
:

•

if
I\subset K\subset J
then
(v,k)=(y,{\rm id})
;

•

if
K\subset I\subset J
then
(v,k)=\bigl((g|_{I{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*y,{\rm id}\bigr)
;

•

if
I\subset J\subset K
then
(v,k)=(y,{\rm id})
;

•

if
J\subset I\subset K
then
(v,k)=(y,k)
where
k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
is uniquely determined by
\rho_{I}(y)=h*k*x
;

•

if
K\subset J\subset I
then
(v,k)=\bigl((g|_{J{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*h*x,{\rm id}\bigr)
;

•

if
J\subset K\subset I
then
(v,k)=(k*h*x,k)
where
k\in G_{K{\smallsetminus}J}
is uniquely determined by
y=h*k*\rho_{K}(x)
.

Further, this construction has the following properties:

(i)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an étale proper groupoid whose realization
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|=\bigcup_{J\subset A}|V_{J}|
is the union of the realizations of the finite set of local uniformizers
(V_{J},G_{J})
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is equipped with an inner action of
G
that is determined via Lemma
3.3.3
(iii)
by the action of
G
on
X_{\mathcal{V}}=\sqcup_{I}V_{I}
given by the map

(4.1.5)

\displaystyle{\alpha}:G\times X_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}},\quad(g,(I,x))\mapsto(I,I,x,g|_{I}),

which in particular induces the action
g*(I,x)=(I,g|_{I}*x)
on objects.

The resulting groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
will generally have nontrivial isotropy arising from the
G_{J}
-action on
V_{J}
. However, we will be able to extract a nonsingular groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
from this data by
restricting consideration to the free part of the actions. The next result explains how to construct

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
as the groupoid completion (see Definition
3.2.7
) of a nonsingular étale category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
that has very simple structure.

Proposition 4.1.2
.

Étale data of type
V
defines a nonsingular, locally injective, étale category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}=(Q,{{{\bf Q}}})
with

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle:={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset A}}V_{I},

\displaystyle\qquad{{{\bf Q}}}

\displaystyle:={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset J}{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}},

\displaystyle s\times t\,:\;{{{\bf Q}}}

\displaystyle\to Q\times Q,

\displaystyle\quad(I,J,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl((I,\rho_{IJ}(y)),(J,y)\bigr),

\displaystyle{\rm id}

\displaystyle\,:\;Q\to{{{\bf Q}}},

\displaystyle\quad(I,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(I,I,x);

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\,:\;{{{\bf Q}}}{\vphantom{\times}}_{t}{\times}_{s}{{{\bf Q}}}\to{{{\bf Q}}},

\displaystyle\quad\bigl((H,I,y),(I,J,z)\bigr)

\displaystyle\mapsto(H,I,y)\circ(I,J,z):=(H,J,z).

Further
{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
has the following properties.

(i)

It is a
poset
; in other words
its object space can be equipped with a partial order relation
\preccurlyeq
such that

(4.1.6)

\displaystyle(I,x)\preccurlyeq(J,y)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\bigl((I,x),(J,y)\bigr)\neq\emptyset.

Further, each equivalence class in
Q
has a unique minimum with morphisms to all other elements. In the equivalence class containing
(J,y)
, that minimum is
(H_{y},\rho_{H_{y}J}(y))
, with
H_{y}:=\bigcap\{H:y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\}
.

(ii)

Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}:=\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
be the étale groupoid completion of
{{\mathcal{Q}}}

given by Lemma
3.2.9
(iii).
Then there
are morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
between
V_{I}
and
V_{J}
only if
I,J
are nested, and for
I\subset J
the
morphism spaces are given as follows :

(4.1.7)

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}}(V_{I},V_{J})

\displaystyle:={\textstyle\bigcup_{\emptyset\neq H\subset I}}\;\bigl({\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\cap{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\bigr)\times G_{I{\smallsetminus}H},

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(y,g)\in V_{J}\times G\ \big|\ y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ},g\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}\bigr\},

where we denote
H_{y}:=\min\{H\,|\,y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\}
. The structure maps
40
40

40

Since
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
is nonsingular, the source and target map determine the inverse and composition maps uniquely. An explicit formula for the composition can be found in (
4.1.4
).

of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
are given by the same formulas as those of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
in Proposition
4.1.1
.

The proof is given in §
4.2
.
Note that
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|
is not in general Hausdorff. However the next result shows that the category

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
supports a
G
-action and that the quotient
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|/G\simeq|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is Hausdorff.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}},{{\mathcal{Q}}},{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}(=\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}})
be constructed from étale data as in Propositions
4.1.1
and
4.1.2
.
Then the inclusion
{\iota}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
factors as

{{\mathcal{Q}}}\;\to\;{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\;\;\to\;{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}.

Further
G
acts on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
, and the inclusion
{\iota}
extends
to functors
{\iota}^{\times G}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}\to({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
that induce identifications
of the realizations

|{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}|\;\simeq\;|({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})^{\times G}|\;\simeq\;|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|/G\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\;|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|.

We next explain the construction of the bundle
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E
and the functor
\tau
in Theorem
2.1.2
.
This bundle is simply the product of the category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
with the category
E
, whose object space is a finite dimensional vector space

E
on which
G
acts and whose morphisms consist only of identity morphisms. This category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E
has a
G
action as follows.

Lemma 4.1.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
be as in Proposition
4.1.3
(so that
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
has groupoid completion
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}:={\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
),
and let
E
be a finite dimensional vector space on which
G
acts.
Consider the following product category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E
:

\displaystyle Q\times E={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I}}V_{I}\times E,

\displaystyle\quad{{{\bf Q}}}\times{\bf E}={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset J}}{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\times E,

\displaystyle(s\times t)\,(I,J,y,e)

\displaystyle=\bigl((I,\rho_{IJ}(y),e),(J,y,e)\bigr),

with groupoid completion
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}\times E
.
Then:

(i)

The category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E
is étale, and
G
acts on both
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E
and its groupoid completion
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E=({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})\times E
by the product action on objects and morphisms.

(ii)

There is a bundle
{\rm pr}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with fiber
E
that supports a
G
-action and is
such that the functorial inclusion
{\iota}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
defined in Proposition
4.1.1
(ii) lifts to a
G
-equivariant functor

{\iota}_{E}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
that induces a homeomorphism

|({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\times G}|\;\simeq\;|({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)|/G\simeq|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}|/G.

The proof is given in Lemma
4.3.1
below. This lemma also describes precise conditions under which the quotient
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}|/G
is not Hausdorff. The point is this:
The bundle
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
in (iii) above is constructed from the spaces
V_{J}\times E
by adjoining morphisms given by the
diagonal
group action of
G
on
V_{J}\times E
. On the other hand,
the partial group actions that appear as morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E
are given by
G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}
-actions on
{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\times E
that are trivial on the
E
factor. We will see in
Lemma
4.3.1
(ii) below that the space
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}|/G
is not Hausdorff
when these actions are different and the covering of
|{{\mathcal{Q}}}|
given by the sets
|V_{I}|
is sufficiently complicated..

The
G
-equivariant functor

\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}

whose existence is claimed in Theorem
2.1.2
(v) will be constructed in §
5.4

from a family of local stabilizations of the original Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
.
It has the following formal structure.

Proposition 4.1.5
.

In the situation of Lemma
4.1.4
, suppose given
a bundle
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
over
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

with induced inner
G
-action as defined in Lemma
3.3.6
.
Suppose further that there is a functor
{\tau}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
such that

-

the induced map on object spaces is
G
-equivariant

-

its restriction to each fiber
(I,y)\times E
is linear with kernel that includes the subspace
E_{A{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
, where
H_{y}=\min\{H\,|\,y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HI}\}
as in (
4.1.7
).

-

{\tau}\circ{\mathcal{P}}={\iota}\circ{\rm pr}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
,

where
{\iota}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the functorial inclusion from Proposition
4.1.3
.
Then:

(i)

{\tau}
extends uniquely to a functor
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

(ii)

Further
\tau
factors as the composite
\tau=\tau_{E}\circ{\iota}_{E}
in the following
commutative diagram of functors

\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\scriptstyle{\;\hskip 8.19447pt{\iota}_{E}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\tau_{E}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{P}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\iota}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\;=}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}},}

where
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
and the
G
-equivariant functor
{\iota}_{E}
are as in Lemma
4.1.4
(ii).
Further,
\tau_{E}
is
G
-equivariant, in the sense that it takes the
G
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
defined in Lemma
4.1.4
(ii) to that on
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

For the proof, see Proposition
4.3.3
.

Finally we discuss the construction of multisections of
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
. For basic definitions, see Definition
3.4.5
.

Proposition 4.1.6
.

Let the bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, group
G
, and functor
\breve{\tau}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with extension
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}{\Gamma}}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
be as
in Proposition
4.1.5
.

(i)

Each
e\in E
induces a multisection functor
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}

given by

(4.1.12)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}(w)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{|G|}\ \#\{h\in G\,|\,\tau(P_{\mathcal{V}}(w),h*e)=w\},

where
\#
denotes the number of elements in a finite set.

(ii)

This multisection
{\Lambda}
is globally structured in the sense of Definition
3.4.5
by the sections
({\mathfrak{s}}_{g})_{g\in G}
and correspondence
{\kappa}:{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\to\{b:G\to G,{\rm bijection}\}
given by

(4.1.13)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}_{g}(I,x)

\displaystyle=\tau(I,x,g*e),\ \quad{\kappa}(I,J,y,g)(h)=g*h.

(iii)

The triple
({\Lambda},({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}},{\kappa})
is structurable in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.6]
, and determines a unique structured multisection
[{\Lambda},{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{S}},\tau]
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.8]
, where
(U(x)_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
is
the system of good neighbourhoods in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
defined in Lemma
4.2.9
.

The proof,
together with a few other results about multisections, may be found at the end of
§
4.3
.

4.2.
The category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
and its relation to
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

We now construct the categories
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, and prove
Propositions
4.1.1
,
4.1.2
, and
4.1.3
. We begin by
constructing the categories
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
, establishing part (i) and most of (ii) of Proposition
4.1.2
.
We then construct two étale categories that are related to
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
.
The first new category is
{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}
and is obtained simply by multiplying the morphism space of
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
by
G
as in Lemma
3.3.8
, while the second one is the groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

in Propositions
4.1.1
and
4.1.3
that
incorporates the
G
-action in a more subtle way. As we explained in §
4.1
the groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

has
the same objects
\bigsqcup_{I}V_{I}
as
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
, but for each
J
contains the full translation groupoid defined by the action of
G_{J}
on
V_{J}
rather than the ‘partial’ actions of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}
on

{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\subset V_{J}
that lie in
{\rm Mor}_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
, and it takes quite a bit of work to establish the
composition rule (
4.1.4
) in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Finally Lemma
4.2.7
exhibits
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
as the groupoid completion of a category
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
that only has morphisms from
V_{I}
to
V_{J}
when
I\subset J
, and also shows that each functor
\phi:{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
(where
{\mathcal{Z}}
is an arbitrary groupoid) has a unique extension to a functor
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
. This result is an essential ingredient in the construction of the functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
in Theorem
2.1.2
(ii).

Lemma 4.2.1
.

Given étale data of tyye
V

let
{{\mathcal{Q}}}=(Q,{{{\bf Q}}})
be
the étale
category with

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I}}V_{I},\qquad{{{\bf Q}}}={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset J}{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}},

\displaystyle{\rm id}:

\displaystyle\;\;Q\to{{{\mathcal{Q}}}},\quad\;(I,y)\mapsto(I,I,y),

\displaystyle(s\times t):

\displaystyle\;\;\ {{{\bf Q}}}\to Q\times Q,\quad(I,J,y)\mapsto\bigl((I,\rho_{IJ}(y)),(J,y)\bigr),

\displaystyle(I,J,y)

\displaystyle\circ(J,K,z)=(I,K,z),\quad\mbox{ if }I\subset J\subset K,

Then the following holds.

(i)

{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is a nonsingular étale category such that
\pi_{{\mathcal{Q}}}:Q\to|{{\mathcal{Q}}}|
is locally injective. Moreover,
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is a poset, i.e. the relation
\preccurlyeq
on its object space given by

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle(I,x)\preccurlyeq(J,y)\Longleftrightarrow{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\bigl((I,x),(J,y)\bigr)\neq\emptyset

is a partial order. Further, each equivalence class in
Q
has a unique minimum with morphisms to all other elements. In the equivalence class containing
(J,y)
that minimum is
(H_{y},\rho_{H_{y}J}(y))
, with
H_{y}:=\bigcap\{H:y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\}
.

(ii)

Let
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
be the étale groupoid completion of
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
(which exists by Lemma
3.2.9
(iii), (iv)).
Then there
are morphisms in
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
between
V_{I}
and
V_{J}
only if
I,J
are nested, and for
I\subset J
the
morphism spaces are given as follows:

(4.2.2)

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}(V_{I},V_{J})

\displaystyle={\textstyle\bigcup_{\emptyset\neq H\subset I}}\;\bigl({\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\cap{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\bigr)\times G_{I{\smallsetminus}H},

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(I,J,y,g)\in V_{J}\times G\ \big|\ y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ},g\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}\bigr\},

where for
I\subset J
we define

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle(s\times t)\,(I,J,y,g_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}})

\displaystyle=\bigl(\,(I,\,g_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}^{-1}*\rho_{IJ}(y)),\;(J,y)\bigr).

Further (again for
I\subset J
)

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}(V_{J},V_{I})=\bigl\{(I,J,y,g)^{-1}:=(J,I,g^{-1}*y,g^{-1})\ |\ (I,J,y,g)\in{\rm Mor}_{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}(V_{I},V_{J})\bigr\}.

Proof.

It follows from the identity
\rho_{IK}=\rho_{IJ}\circ\rho_{JK}
for
I\subset J\subset K

that
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is a well defined, nonsingular category. It is étale because for each
I\subset J
the map
\rho_{IJ}
quotients out by a free action of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}
.
Moreover,
\preccurlyeq
is a partial order; in particular it is antisymmetric because the identity property implies
that for any pair of distinct elements
(I,x),(J,y)
at most one of the properties
(I,x)\preccurlyeq(J,y)
and

(J,y)\preccurlyeq(I,x)
holds.

Next observe that as in the proof of Lemma
3.1.8
, two distinct elements
(I,x),(J,y)
lie in the same equivalence class of
\sim_{{\mathcal{Q}}}
if and only if there is a chain from
(I,x)
to
(J,y)
of the following type:

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle(I,x)=:(I_{0},x_{0})\preccurlyeq(I_{1},x_{1})\succcurlyeq(I_{2},x_{2})\preccurlyeq\cdots\succcurlyeq(I_{k},x_{k}):=(J,y).

Now, in a triple of the form

(I,x)\preccurlyeq(K,z)\succcurlyeq(J,y)
we must have
z\in{\widetilde{V}}_{IK}\cap{\widetilde{V}}_{JK}
which implies that
I,J,K
are nested.
Similarly, the existence of a triple of the form
(I,x)\succcurlyeq(K,z)\preccurlyeq(J,y)
implies that
z\in V_{KI}\cap V_{KJ}
, so that again
I,J,K
are nested. Hence the sets
(I_{i})_{0\leq i\leq k}
in any chain such as (
4.2.5
) must be nested.
In particular, every
y\in V_{J}
lies in a set of the form
{\widetilde{V}}_{H_{y}J}
for a unique minimal set
H_{y}
, that is contained in all other sets
I
such that
(J,y)\sim_{{\mathcal{Q}}}(I,y^{\prime})
.
Further, because
H_{y}
is minimal, there is a unique element
z:=\rho_{H_{y}J}(y)
in
V_{H_{y}}
in the equivalence class
[(J,y)]
. Hence the equivalence class of
(J,Y)
has a unique minimum with morphisms to all other elements.

To see that
\pi_{{\mathcal{Q}}}:Q\to|{{\mathcal{Q}}}|
is locally injective, notice that
because the set
{\widetilde{V}}_{H_{y}J}
is open, the second separation property implies that
y
has a neighbourhood
N_{y}\subset{\widetilde{V}}_{H_{y}J}\subset V_{J}
that intersects
{\rm cl}({\widetilde{V}}_{FJ})
only if
H_{y}\subset F
. It follows that
H_{y}=H_{y^{\prime}}
for all
y^{\prime}\in N_{y}
. In other words, each equivalence
class
[(J,y^{\prime})]
for
y\in N_{y}
contains a unique element of the form
(H_{y},z^{\prime})
, where
z^{\prime}:=\rho_{H_{y}J}(y^{\prime})
.
Since
\rho_{H_{y}J}:{\widetilde{V}}_{H_{y}J}\to V_{H_{y}}
quotients out by a free action of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
, we may
shrink
N_{y}
so that the map
\rho_{H_{y}J}|_{N_{y}}
is injective. It follows that
\pi_{{\mathcal{Q}}}:N_{y}\to|{{\mathcal{Q}}}|
is injective. This completes the proof of (i).

To prove (ii), note first that because the completion
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
is nonsingular, each morphism is determined by its source and target.
In particular,
the formulas (
4.2.2
) and (
4.2.3
) do describe morphisms from
V_{I}
to
V_{J}
in
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
in the case
I\subset J
. When
I\subset J
, we find using (
4.2.4
) that

\displaystyle(s\times t)\bigl((I,J,y,g)^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(s\times t)(J,I,g^{-1}*y,g^{-1})

\displaystyle=\bigl((J,y),(I,\rho_{IJ}(g^{-1}*y)\bigr)=(t\times s)(I,J,y,g)

since
\rho_{IJ}
commutes with the action of
G_{I}
. Therefore the description of
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}(V_{J},V_{I})
in
(
4.2.4
) is consistent with the source and target maps, as is the formula for the inverse.

It remains to show that if
I\subset J
, every element in
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
has the form given in (
4.2.2
).
The remarks above imply that
if
I\subset J
and
(I,x)\sim_{{\mathcal{Q}}}(J,y)
, we can replace the chain (
4.2.5
) by one of the form

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle(I,x)\succcurlyeq\bigl(H_{y},z\bigr)\preccurlyeq(J,y),\quad z:=\rho_{H_{y}I}(x)=\rho_{H_{y}J}(y)=\rho_{H_{y}I}\bigl(\rho_{IJ}(y)\bigr).

Thus the elements
x
and
\rho_{IJ}(y)
of
V_{I}
have the same image under
\rho_{H_{y}I}
, so that they must both lie in the same
G_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
orbit.
Hence there is a unique element
g_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
such that

x=g_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}^{-1}*\rho_{IJ}(y)
. Thus this morphism in
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
from
(I,x)
to
(J,y)
has the same source and target as the tuple

(I,J,y,g_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}),\quad\mbox{ where }\;\;x=g_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}^{-1}*\rho_{IJ}(y),

and hence may be identified with it since
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
is nonsingular. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.2
.

(i)

Both
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
and
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
have realizations that are unions of the realizations of finitely many preuniformizers
\bigl((V_{I},{\rm id})\bigr)_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}
; see Definition
3.2.1
. But just as in Example
3.2.4
these are not uniformizers.

(ii)

Above, given
y\in V_{J}
, we defined
H_{y}:=\min\{H:y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\}
. We remark here that
the set
H_{y}
does not depend on the choice of
J
but only on the equivalence class
[(J,y)]
of the element
(J,y)
. Indeed by (
4.2.6
) we could equally well have defined
H_{y}
to be the minimum of the sets

I
that appear in this equivalence class.

(iii)

As in Example
3.2.4
the space
|{{\mathcal{Q}}}|=|\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}|=|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|
is not in general Hausdorff.

{\Diamond}

Lemma 4.2.3
.

The étale categories
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
and
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
constructed in
Lemma
4.2.1
support an action of
G
.

Proof.

We define the action of
G
on the object space
Q:=\bigsqcup_{I}V_{I}
by setting

g*(I,x)=(I,g_{I}*x),\quad x\in V_{I},\ g\in G,

where
g_{I}
denotes the projection of
g:=(g_{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}\in G
to
g_{I}:=(g_{i})_{i\in I}\in G_{I}
. This action preserves the equivalence relation
\sim_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
on
Q
because we have assumed that
\rho_{IJ}
is equivariant with respect to the projection
G_{J}\to G_{I}
.
Therefore,
since the nonsingular category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is locally injective,
the existence of the
G
action on
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
and hence on
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
follows from Lemma
3.3.3
(iv).
∎

We now prove Propositions
4.1.1
and
4.1.3
which we restate for the convenience of the reader.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Every collection of étale data of type
V
defines
an étale groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}=(X_{\mathcal{V}},{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}})
with

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle X_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset A}V_{I},

\displaystyle\qquad s(I,J,y,g)

\displaystyle:=(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y)),

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\sim J}{\widetilde{V}}_{(I\wedge J)(I\vee J)}\times G_{I\wedge J},

\displaystyle\qquad t(I,J,y,g)

\displaystyle:=(J,\rho_{J}(y)),

\displaystyle{\rm id}_{(I,x)}

\displaystyle:=(I,I,x),

\displaystyle\qquad(I,J,y,g)^{-1}

\displaystyle:=(J,I,g^{-1}*y,g^{-1}).

If
t(I,J,x,g)=s(J,K,y,h)
, then
I,J,K
are necessarily nested, and we define the composition by

(4.2.8)

\displaystyle(I,J,x,g)\circ(J,K,y,h):=\bigl(I,K,\rho_{I\vee K}(v),k(hg)|_{I\wedge K}\bigr),

where
(v,k)\in V_{I\vee J\vee K}\times G_{I\wedge K}

is
given as follows:

•

if
I\subset K\subset J
then
(v,k)=(y,{\rm id})
;

•

if
K\subset I\subset J
then
(v,k)=\bigl((g|_{I{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*y,{\rm id}\bigr)
;

•

if
I\subset J\subset K
then
(v,k)=(y,{\rm id})
;

•

if
J\subset I\subset K
then
(v,k)=(y,k)
where
k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
is uniquely determined by

(4.2.9)

\displaystyle\rho_{I}(y)=h*k*x;

•

if
K\subset J\subset I
then
(v,k)=\bigl((g|_{J{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*h*x,{\rm id}\bigr)
;

•

if
J\subset K\subset I
then
(v,k)=(k*h*x,k)
where
k\in G_{K{\smallsetminus}J}
is uniquely determined by

(4.2.10)

\displaystyle y=h*k*\rho_{K}(x).

Further,

(i)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an étale proper groupoid whose realization
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|=\bigcup_{J\subset A}|V_{J}|
is the union of the realizations of the finite set of local uniformizers
(V_{J},G_{J})
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
supports an inner action of
G
that is determined as in (
3.3.3
) by the map

(4.2.11)

\displaystyle{\alpha}:G\times X_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}},\quad(g,(I,x))\mapsto(I,I,x,g|_{I}).

(iii)

The inclusion
{\iota}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
factors as

{{\mathcal{Q}}}\;\to\;{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}={\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\;\;\to\;{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}},

and extends to functors
{\iota}^{\times G}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}\to{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
that induce the identification

|{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}|\;\simeq\;|{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}|\;\simeq\;|Q|/G\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\;|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|

of the realizations.

Remark 4.2.5
.

The composition rule in (
4.2.8
) above is most complicated (i.e.
k\neq{\rm id}
) when
J
is minimal among
\{I,J,K\}
, since that is the case in which the compatibility in
V_{J}
required for the composite to be defined gives the least information.
For example, if
J\subset I\subset K
, the composite
(I,J,x,{\rm id})\circ(J,K,y,{\rm id})
is defined if

\rho_{J}(x)=\rho_{J}(y)
(where
x\in{\widetilde{V}}_{JI}\subset V_{I},y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{JK}\subset V_{K}
). Therefore by the composition property in Lemma
4.2.1
, the element
\rho_{I}(y)\in{\widetilde{V}}_{JI}
must be defined and lie in the same
G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
-orbit as
x
, which implies by the freeness of the action of
G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
on
{\widetilde{V}}_{JI}
that there
is a unique
k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
such that
\rho_{I}(y)=k*x
as in (
4.2.9
).
Note also that if
g,h\neq{\rm id}
, then
g,h\in G_{J}
and so both commute with
k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
.
Further, as
x,y
vary,
k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
is a locally constant function, though it need not be constant on the components of the morphism space
unless the principal
G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
-bundle
{\widetilde{V}}_{JI}\to V_{JI}
is trivial.

{\Diamond}

Proof of Proposition
4.2.4
.

Step 1:

If composition is defined then
I,J,K
are nested.

The composition
(I,J,x,g)\circ(J,K,y,h)
is defined only if

t(I,J,x,g)=\rho_{J}(x)=s(J,K,y,h)=\rho_{J}(h^{-1}*y).

Further we know that
I,J
are nested and
J,K
are nested. Hence
I,J,K
are nested except possibly if
J
is either maximal or minimal.
But if
J
is maximal, then the condition
t(I,J,x,g)=s(J,K,y,h)
implies that
{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\cap{\widetilde{V}}_{KJ}\neq\emptyset
so that

I,J,K
are nested by the separation axiom in §
4.1
(c). Similarly, if
J
is minimal, we have
V_{JI}\cap V_{JK}\neq\emptyset

which again implies that
I,J,K
are nested.

Step 2:

The
rule (
4.2.8
) is consistent with source and target maps.

We must check
that

\displaystyle{\rm(A)}:\quad s(I,J,x,g)=s\bigl(I,K,\rho_{I\vee K}(v),k(hg)|_{I\wedge K}\bigr),

\displaystyle{\rm(B)}:\;\;\quad(K,\rho_{K}(y))=t(J,K,y,h)=t\bigl(I,K,\rho_{I\vee K}(v),k(hg)|_{I\wedge K}\bigr)=(K,\rho_{K}(v)),

when

t(I,J,x,g)=\rho_{J}(x)=s(J,K,y,h)=\rho_{J}(h^{-1}*y),

i.e. when

(4.2.12)

\displaystyle h*\rho_{J}(x)=\rho_{J}(y),\;\;\mbox{ where }h\in G_{J\wedge K}.

We first prove (B). First notice that
(B) holds whenever
y=v
, and hence when
K
is maximal or
I\subset K\subset J
.
If
K\subset I\subset J
then
v=(g|_{I{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*y
and
h\in G_{K}
. Therefore
\rho_{K}(v)=\rho_{K}(y)
since
\rho_{K}
quotients out by the action of
G_{I{\smallsetminus}K}
.
If
I
is maximal and
K
is minimal, then

v=(g|_{J{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*h*x\in V_{I}
and
h\in G_{K}
. Therefore

\rho_{K}(v)=\rho_{K}(h*x)=h*\rho_{K}(x)=\rho_{K}(y)

because
\rho_{K}
is
G_{K}
-equivariant and constant on the orbits of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}K}
.
Finally if
I
is maximal and
J
is minimal, the compatibility condition (
4.2.12
) it implies that
y\in V_{K}
lies in the same
G_{K{\smallsetminus}J}
-orbit as
\rho_{K}(h*x)
. Hence there is
a unique element
k\in G_{K{\smallsetminus}J}
such that
y=k*h*\rho_{K}(x)
.
By definition we take
v=k*h*x
in this case. Hence again
\rho_{K}(v)=y=\rho_{K}(y)
, as required.
Thus (B) holds in all cases.

Next observe that (A) will hold if

(4.2.13)

\displaystyle g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x)=(k(hg)|_{I\wedge K})^{-1}*\rho_{I}\rho_{I\vee K}(v)=(g|_{I\wedge K})^{-1}*(h|_{I\wedge K})^{-1}*k^{-1}*\rho_{I}(v).

We first consider the three cases with
v=y
.

\bullet

If
I\subset J\subset K
then
x\in V_{J}
,
g\in G_{I},h\in G_{J}
,
k={\rm id}
and
v=y\in V_{K}
, so that
we must check

g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x)=g^{-1}*(h|_{I})^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y).

But because
\rho_{I}
is equivariant with respect to the projection
G_{J}\to G_{I}
we have

(h|_{I})^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y)=\rho_{I}(h^{-1}*y)
, so that this follows from the compatibility condition (
4.2.12
).

\bullet

If
I\subset K\subset J
, then
v=y\in V_{J}
,
h\in G_{K}
and
k={\rm id}
,
and the same argument holds.

\bullet

If
J\subset I\subset K
, then
x\in V_{I}
,
v=y\in V_{K}
and by definition of
k
we have
\rho_{I}(y)=h*k*x
where
h\in G_{J},k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
commute. Since
h=h|_{I\wedge K}
and
g=g|_{I\wedge K}
, this equation is equivalent to (
4.2.13
).

\bullet

If
K\subset I\subset J
then
h\in G_{K}
, and
g\in G_{I}
decomposes as the product
g=g|_{K}\,g|_{I{\smallsetminus}K}
, where these factors commute. Further
k={\rm id}
and
v=(g_{I{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*y\in V_{J}
, while the compatibility equation (
4.2.12
) reads
y=h*x
.
In this case, (
4.2.13
) can be written as

g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x)=(g|_{K})^{-1}*h^{-1}*(g|_{I{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y)

which follows from
y=h*x
because
h\in G_{K}
commutes with
g|_{I{\smallsetminus}K}
.

\bullet

If
K\subset J\subset I
, then
v=(g|_{J{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*h*x\in V_{I}
and
k={\rm id}
. Since
h\in G_{K}
,
h
commutes with

g|_{J{\smallsetminus}K}
. Hence

(g|_{K})^{-1}*h^{-1}*v=(g|_{K})^{-1}*(g|_{J{\smallsetminus}K})^{-1}*x=g^{-1}*x

as is required by (
4.2.13
).

\bullet

Finally, if
J\subset K\subset I
then
v=k*h*x\in V_{I}
where
k\in G_{K{\smallsetminus}J}
is the unique element such that
y=h*k*\rho_{K}(x)
. By definition,
g,h\in G_{J}
so that
g=g|_{I\wedge K}
and
h=h|_{I\wedge K}
.
Therefore (
4.2.13
) is equivalent to
x=h^{-1}*k^{-1}*v
, which holds by the definition of
v
.

This completes the proof of Step 2.

Step 3:

The formula for the inverse
is consistent with that for composition.

To check this, we note that

\displaystyle(I,J,x,g)\circ\bigl((I,J,x,g)\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(I,J,x,g)\circ(J,I,g^{-1}*x,g^{-1})

\displaystyle=(I,I,\rho_{I}(v),k)\;\;\mbox{ with }(v,k)\mbox{ as in }\eqref{eq:MMGa30}

\displaystyle=(I,I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x),{\rm id})

because if
K=I\subset J
we have
k={\rm id},\ \rho_{I}(v)=\rho_{I}(g^{-1}*x)=g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x)
; while if
J\subset I=K
we have

x,y\in V_{I}
,
v=y
and
k\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
so that
y=g^{-1}*k*x
, which implies

k=id
by (
4.2.12
).

Step 4:

There is a well defined inclusion
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
; in particular, the structure maps in
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
are given by (
4.2.7
), (
4.2.8
).

Lemma
4.2.1
shows that
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
has a unique nonsingular groupoid completion
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
whose structure maps are determined by its source and target maps. This lemma also shows that
these maps
s,t
are given by the formulas in (
4.2.7
), as is the formula for the inverse.
Further, the composition rule in
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is given by the case
I\subset J\subset K
of (
4.2.8
).
Hence to check that composition in
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
is given by (
4.2.8
), in view of Step 2 it remains to verify that this rule preserves the subset
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
of
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
. To this end, recall from the
proof of Lemma
4.2.1
that
each equivalence class
[(J,y)]
in
{\rm Obj}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
contains a unique element of the form
(H_{y},z)
where
H_{y}
is the minimal set
I
such that
(J,y)\sim(I,x)
for some
x\in V_{I}
. Further,
the description of
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
in
(
4.2.2
) and (
4.2.4
) together with the identity
(I,J,z,\ell)^{-1}=(J,I,\ell^{-1}*z,\ell^{-1})
implies that

(4.2.14)

\displaystyle(I,J,z,\ell)\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\ell\in G_{I\wedge J{\smallsetminus}H_{z}}.

Therefore, to see that
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
is closed under the composition rule (
4.2.8
), it
remains to check that if
(I,J,x,g),(J,K,y,h)
both satisfy the condition in (
4.2.14
) and

t(I,J,x,g)=s(J,K,y,h)
, then the element

(I,K,z,\ell)
given by (
4.2.8
)
also satisfies this condition.
But in this case, there is equality
H_{x}=H_{y}=H_{z}
and
H_{x}\subset I\wedge J\wedge K
.
Further
\ell=k(hg)|_{I\wedge K}
where
g\in G_{I\wedge J{\smallsetminus}H_{z}},h\in G_{J\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{z}}
, and
k\in G_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
by construction. Therefore
\ell\in G_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{z}}
as required.
Hence the composition defined in (
4.2.8
) does restrict to
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
. This completes the proof.

Step 5:

The composition rule in (
4.2.8
) is associative.

Although associativity is proved in
[
Mc1
, Prop.2.3]
,
41
41

41

As will become more evident in §
5.2
