below, the category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the subcategory of the category
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{K}}
considered in
[
Mc1
, Prop.2.3]
that corresponds to a reduction
{\mathcal{V}}
of the covering given by a strict orbifold atlas.

we give an independent proof here for the sake of completeness.
We will use the fact that the action of
G_{I}
on
V_{I}
given as in (
3.3.2
) by

{\alpha}_{I}(g,x)=(I,I,x,g):g^{-1}*x\mapsto x

yields
actions of

G_{I}
and
G_{J}
on the morphism space
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
as follows:

\displaystyle G_{I}\times{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})\;\to\;{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J}),\qquad

\displaystyle(g,m)\;\mapsto\;g\circ m:={\alpha}_{I}(g,s(m))\circ m,

\displaystyle G_{J}\times{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})\;\to\;{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J}),\qquad

\displaystyle(g,m)\;\mapsto\;m\circ g:=m\circ{\alpha}_{J}(g,g*t(m)).

Further,
G_{I\wedge J}
acts by conjugation on
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
via
m\mapsto m^{g}:=g\circ m\circ g^{-1}
.
Notice that the precise morphism in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
represented by
g
in the notation
g\circ m
(respectively
m\circ g
) depends only on the source
s(m)
(respectively the target
t(m)
).

Next observe that the rule (
4.2.8
) applied with
I=J
implies that every morphism
(I,K,x,g)
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with
x\in V_{I\vee K},g\in G_{I\wedge K}
may be uniquely decomposed as a product

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle(I,K,x,g):

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{I}\bigl(g|_{H_{x}},(g|_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{x}})^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x)\bigr)\circ(I,K,x,g|_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{x}}),

\displaystyle=g|_{H_{x}}\circ m,\quad m:=(I,K,x,g|_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{x}})\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}.

Similarly, taking
J=K
, we have

\displaystyle(I,K,x,g):

\displaystyle=(I,K,(g|_{H_{x}})^{-1}*x,g|_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{x}})\circ{\alpha}_{K}\bigl(g|_{H_{x}}\rho_{K}(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle=m^{\prime}\circ g|_{H_{x}},\quad m^{\prime}:=(I,K,(g|_{H_{x}})^{-1}*x,g|_{I\wedge K{\smallsetminus}H_{x}})\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}.

Thus, for
m=(I,J,x,g)\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
and
\ell\in H_{x}
, we have

(4.2.16)

\displaystyle\ell\circ m=m^{\ell}\circ\ell,\quad\mbox{where }\quad m^{\ell}=(I,J,\ell^{-1}*x,\ell^{-1}g\ell)\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}.

In particular, this conjugation action takes
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
to itself.
Further,

(4.2.17)

\displaystyle m_{1}^{\ell}\circ m_{2}^{\ell}=(m_{1}\circ m_{2})^{\ell},\quad\forall\;\;\ell\in G_{H_{x}},\;\;x:=s(m_{1}),

since the morphisms on both sides have the same source and target and
lie in the nonsingular groupoid
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
.
More generally, we claim that if
m\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
and
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in G_{H_{x}}
then

(4.2.18)

\displaystyle(\ell_{1}\circ m)\circ\ell_{2}=\ell_{1}\circ(m_{1}\circ\ell_{2})=:\ell_{1}\circ m_{1}\circ\ell_{2}\in{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}.

This follows easily from the fact that a morphism
m=(I,J,x,g)
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is uniquely determined by its source
s(m)=g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x)
,
target
t(m)=\rho_{J}(x)
, and group element
g\in G_{I\wedge J}
. (For example, if
J\subset I
then
x=g*s(m)
.)
A similar argument shows that

(4.2.19)

\displaystyle(\ell\circ m_{1})\circ m_{2}=\ell\circ(m_{1}\circ m_{2}),\qquad m_{1},m_{2}\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}},\ell\in G_{H_{s(m_{1})}}.

Finally we claim that if

m_{1}=(I,J,x,g),\quad m_{2}=(J,K,y,h)\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}},\qquad\ell\in G_{H_{x}}

are morphisms in
\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
then

(4.2.20)

\displaystyle(m_{1}\circ\ell)\circ m_{2}=m_{1}\circ(\ell\circ m_{2})=:m_{1}\circ\ell\circ m_{2}.

To prove this, notice first that the two sides of (
4.2.20
) clearly have the same source and target.
Further the formulas in (
4.2.8
) show that if
J
is not minimal they have the same group element, namely
(g\ell h)|_{I\wedge K}=(\ell gh)|_{I\wedge K}
,
since
g\in G_{I\wedge J{\smallsetminus}H_{x}}
commutes with
\ell
.
If
J\subset I\subset K
,
then the group element
for
(m_{1}\circ\ell)\circ m_{2}
(resp.
m_{1}\circ(\ell\circ m_{2})
) is
k_{1}(g\ell h)|_{J}
(resp.
k_{2}(g\ell h)|_{J}
, where (
4.2.9
) shows that
k_{1},k_{2}\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}J}
satisfy

\rho_{I}(y)=h*k_{1}*(\ell*x),\qquad\rho_{I}(y)=\ell*h*k_{2}*x.

But these two conditions are the same because
\ell\in G_{H_{x}}
commutes with the elements
h,k_{i},g\in G_{I{\smallsetminus}H_{x}}
.
A similar argument establishes (
4.2.20
) in the case
J\subset I\subset K
.

To prove associativity in
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}

(4.2.21)

\displaystyle\Bigl((I,J,x,g)\circ(J,K,y,h)\Bigr)\circ(K,L,z,k)=(I,J,x,g)\circ\Bigl((J,K,y,h)\circ(K,L,z,k)\Bigr),

note that these composites are defined only if the sets
I,J,K,L
are nested and
H_{x}=H_{y}=H_{z}\subset I\wedge J\wedge K\wedge L
. Thus by (
4.2.15
) it suffices to prove

\bigl((\ell_{1}\circ m_{1})\circ(\ell_{2}\circ m_{2})\bigr)\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})=(\ell_{1}\circ m_{1})\circ\bigl((\ell_{2}\circ m_{2})\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})\bigr),

where
\ell_{i}\in G_{H_{x}}
and
m_{i}\in{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}
for
i=1,2,3
. But

\displaystyle\bigl((\ell_{1}\circ m_{1})\circ(\ell_{2}\circ m_{2})\bigr)\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})

\displaystyle=\bigl((\ell_{1}\circ m_{1}\circ\ell_{2})\circ m_{2}\bigr)\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})\;\;\mbox{by }\eqref{eq:QM2}

\displaystyle=(\ell_{1}\circ\ell_{2}\circ m_{1}^{\ell_{2}})\circ m_{2}\bigr)\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})\;\;\mbox{by }\eqref{eq:QQ04}

\displaystyle=\bigl((\ell_{1}\circ\ell_{2})\circ(m_{1}^{\ell_{2}}\circ m_{2})\bigr)\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})\;\;\mbox{by }\eqref{eq:QM3}

\displaystyle=\bigl((\ell_{1}\circ\ell_{2})\circ(m_{1}^{\ell_{2}}\circ m_{2})\circ\ell_{3}\bigr)\circ m_{3})\;\;\mbox{by }\eqref{eq:QM2}

\displaystyle=(\ell_{1}\circ\ell_{2})\circ\ell_{3}\circ(m_{1}^{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\circ m_{2}^{\ell_{3}})\circ m_{3}\;\;\mbox{by }\eqref{eq:QM2}.

A similar argument shows that

(\ell_{1}\circ m_{1})\circ\bigl((\ell_{2}\circ m_{2})\circ(\ell_{3}\circ m_{3})\bigr)=\ell_{1}\circ(\ell_{2}\circ\ell_{3})\circ m_{1}^{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\circ(m_{2}^{\ell_{3}}\circ m_{3}).

Since composition in the group
G_{H_{x}}
and in the category
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
is associative, the
composition operation in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is also associative.

Step 6:

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an étale, proper groupoid.

The above steps show that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is a well defined groupoid. Further
the formulas given for composition are étale, because the element
k
belongs to a finite group and so is locally constant as
x,y
vary. (See Remark
4.2.5
.) Therefore the map
\pi:X_{\mathcal{V}}\to|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is open by Lemma
3.1.8
, and so, by Lemma
3.2.5
(iv) to see that
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is Hausdorff it suffices to show that the relation
\sim_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
on
X_{\mathcal{V}}
has closed graph in
X_{\mathcal{V}}\times X_{\mathcal{V}}
. Because this property is preserved by taking inverses, it suffices to prove this for the components
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
with
I\subset J
.
But for each
J
the set
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{J},V_{J})\simeq V_{J}\times G_{J}
is a finite union of disjoint components indexed by
g\in G_{J}
, and for each such component
{\rm graph}(s\times t)=\{(g^{-1}*(y),y)\ |\ y\in V_{J}\}
, which is closed in
V_{J}\times V_{J}
.
Further, if
I\subset J
, every morphism
(I,J,y,g)\in{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
decomposes uniquely as the composite

(J,J,y,g)\circ(I,J,g^{-1}*y,{\rm id}),\quad y\in{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ},\ g\in G_{J{\smallsetminus}I},

of a morphism
V_{J}\to V_{J}
given by the action of
g\in G_{J}
on
V_{J}
, followed by a morphism given by the projection
\rho_{IJ}
, where, by hypothesis,

{\rm graph}(\rho_{IJ})\subset V_{J}\times V_{I}
is closed. Hence the graph of

{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
is a finite union (indexed by
G_{J}
) of closed sets.
Hence
s\times t:{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}\to X\times X
has closed graph as claimed.

Step 7:

Completion of the proof.

It follows from the explicit description in (
4.2.7
) of the morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
that each pair
(V_{J},G_{J})
is a local uniformizer for
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
. Thus
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is the union of the realizations
|V_{J}|
of these local uniformizers. The rest of (i) is proved in Step 6.
Thus it
remains to prove (ii) and (iii).

Observe first that
G:=\prod_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}
acts on
X_{\mathcal{V}}=\bigsqcup_{I}V_{I}
via the map

\displaystyle G\times V_{I}\to V_{I},\quad(g,I,x)\mapsto(I,g|_{I}*x).

Define
{\alpha}:G\times X_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}
by

(4.2.22)

\displaystyle{\alpha}(g,(I,x))=(I,I,x,g|_{I}),\mbox{ so that }(s\times t)({\alpha}(g,(I,x)))=\bigl((I,g|_{I}^{-1}*x),(I,x)\bigr).

Then

\displaystyle{\alpha}(g,(I,h|_{I}^{-1}*x)))\circ{\alpha}(h,(I,x))

\displaystyle=(I,I,h|_{I}^{-1}*x,g|_{I})\circ(I,I,x,h|_{I})

\displaystyle=(I,I,x,h|_{I}g|_{I})

\displaystyle={\alpha}(hg,(I,x)),

so that by Lemma
3.3.3
(iii) there is an associated inner action of
G
on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
. One can check using (
3.3.3
) and the formulas in (
4.2.8
) that it acts on morphisms by
42
42

42

It is easiest to check this first when
I\subset J
and then to use the formula for the inverse.

(4.2.23)

\displaystyle g*(I,J,y,h)=\bigl(I,J,g|_{I\vee J}*y,g|_{I\wedge J}h(g|_{I\wedge J})^{-1}\bigr)

This proves (ii).

Finally, observe that
the inclusions
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
,
are
G
-equivariant in the sense of Definition
3.3.1
, and hence
induce functors

{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\times G},\quad{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\times G}

by Lemma
3.3.8
(iii).
Composing these with the functor
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
of
Lemma
3.3.8
(v) we obtain functors

{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}\to{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}.

Note that in general the functor
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is not an equivalence of groupoids because the
isotropy group
{\rm Mor}_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}}((I,x),(I,x))
of
the object
(I,x)\in{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}
contains the whole group
\prod_{j\notin I}G_{j}
rather than being a subgroup of
G_{I}
. Nevertheless, by Lemma
3.3.8
(ii,iv), we know
|{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}|=|{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}|\simeq|{{\mathcal{Q}}}|/G
while, because
G
acts on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
by inner automorphisms, we have
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\times G}|\simeq|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
by Lemma
3.3.3
(iii).
Since these categories all have the same object space,
|{\iota}^{\times G}|:|{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is a homeomorphism.
This proves (iii).
∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

(i)

The groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is
not
a groupoid completion of
{\iota}({{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G})
(cf. Definition
3.2.7
) because, as we saw at the very end of the above proof, the
isotropy groups
of elements in
{\iota}({{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G})
are much too large.

(ii)

By Step 4 of the above proof, we may identify
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
with the (nonfull!) subcategory
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
that has the same object space
X_{\mathcal{V}}
as
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and that is generated as a groupoid by the projections
\rho_{IJ}:{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\to V_{IJ}\subset V_{I}
that relate the different components of
X_{\mathcal{V}}
. Thus we have removed the morphisms that depend explicitly on the group actions, except for those needed to form the groupoid completion.

{\Diamond}

The following lemma shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the groupoid completion
43
43

43

See Definition
3.2.7
of its subcategory
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
that has the same objects but morphisms between
V_{I},V_{J}
only for
I\subset J
. Indeed, it is the groupoid completion in a very strong sense: any functor from
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
to a groupoid
{\mathcal{Z}}
must extend to
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
. (Contrast this with the example in Remark
3.2.10
.) This lemma is an essential ingredient of the proof of Theorem
5.2.6
below.

Lemma 4.2.7
.

Let

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
be as in Proposition
4.1.1
, and denote by

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
the subcategory with the same object space but morphisms from
V_{I}
to
V_{J}
only if

I\subset J
. (Thus
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}={\iota}^{\times G}({{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G})
.)
Then every functor
\phi:{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
from
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
to an
arbitrary groupoid has a unique extension to a functor
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Proof.

Denote an arbitrary element in
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{I},V_{J})
by
m_{IJ}
and for
I\supsetneq J
define

(4.2.24)

\displaystyle\phi(m_{IJ}):=\bigl(\phi(m_{IJ}^{-1})\bigr)^{-1}.

Since
(s\times t)(m^{-1})=(t\times s)(m)
, it follows from the assumptions that
\phi
is compatible with the structure maps
s,t
of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
as well as preserving identities and inverses. Hence it remains
to show that if
m_{IJ}\circ m_{JK}=m_{IK}
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
then

(4.2.25)

\displaystyle\phi(m_{IJ})\circ\phi(m_{JK})=\phi(m_{IK}).

We discuss the various cases in turn.

\bullet

If
I\subset J\subset K
then the identity
m_{IJ}\circ m_{JK}=m_{IK}
is a relation in
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
so that (
4.2.25
) holds because
\phi|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
is a functor.

\bullet

If
K\subset J\subset I
then

m^{\prime}_{KI}:=m_{IK}^{-1}=m_{JK}^{-1}\circ m_{IJ}^{-1}=:m_{KJ}^{\prime}\circ m_{JI}^{\prime}

is a relation in
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and so is preserved by
\phi
. Therefore because
\phi(m^{-1})=\bigl(\phi(m)\bigr)^{-1}
we have

\bigl(\phi(m_{IK})\bigr)^{-1}=\bigl(\phi(m_{JK})\bigr)^{-1}\circ\bigl(\phi(m_{IJ})\bigr)^{-1}=\bigl(\phi(m_{IJ})\circ\phi(m_{JK})\bigr)^{-1},

where the second equality is a relation in
{\mathcal{Z}}
,
which implies (
4.2.25
) in this case as well.

\bullet

If
I\subset K\subset J
then we can rewrite the relation
m_{IJ}\circ m_{JK}=m_{IK}

as
m_{IJ}=m_{IK}\circ m_{KJ}^{\prime}
, where
m_{KJ}^{\prime}=m_{JK}^{-1}
. Then, because the latter is a relation in
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
we have

\phi(m_{IJ})=\phi(m_{IK})\circ\phi(m_{KJ}^{\prime})=\phi(m_{IK})\circ\phi(m_{JK}^{-1})=\phi(m_{IK})\circ(\phi(m_{JK}))^{-1},

which easily implies
(
4.2.25
).
By inverting this relation as in the second bullet, we find that (
4.2.25
) also holds if
J\subset K\subset I
.

\bullet

Finally if
J\subset I\subset K
we rewrite
m_{IJ}\circ m_{JK}=m_{IK}

as
m_{JK}=m_{JI}^{\prime}\circ m_{IK}
where
m_{JI}^{\prime}=(m_{IJ})^{-1}
, and then argue as above.
By inverting, we may also obtain (
4.2.25
) in the case
K\subset I\subset J
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.8
.

We saw in Proposition
4.2.4
(iii) that the functor
{\iota}^{\times G}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
induces a homeomorphism
|{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}|/G\to|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
. It follows that each morphism
m\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
decomposes as a product of a morphism in
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
with a morphism given by the action of
G
. This product can be written in either order since

(I,J,x,g)=(I,I,\rho_{I}(x),g)\circ(I,J,x,{\rm id})=(I,J,g^{-1}*x,{\rm id})\circ(J,J,\rho_{J}(x),g),\quad g\in G_{I\wedge J}.

In terms of the map
\overline{{\alpha}}_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}:G\times X_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}},(g,(I,x))\mapsto(I,I,g*x,g)
in Lemma
3.3.3
(iii
′
) that defines the group action, the above identity can be written

(4.2.26)

\displaystyle\overline{{\alpha}}(g,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(x))\circ(I,J,x,{\rm id})=(I,J,g^{-1}*x,{\rm id})\circ\overline{{\alpha}}(g,g^{-1}*\rho_{J}(x)).

{\Diamond}

Finally we show that the category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
has a natural system of good neighbourhoods.

Lemma 4.2.9
.

The category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
carries a natural system of good neighbourhoods
as in Definition
3.4.6
.

Proof.

Recall that
a collection of open neighbourhoods
(U(x))_{x\in X}
of the points
x\in X

of an étale proper category is called a
good system of neighbourhoods
if

-

for every
x\in X
the target map
t:s^{-1}({\rm cl}_{X}(U(x))\to X
is proper;

-

each
U(x)
is equipped with the natural action of the isotropy group
G_{x}
at
x
;

-

for any pair
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and each connected component
{\Sigma}
of
{\rm Mor}(U(x),U(x^{\prime}))
the maps
s:{\Sigma}\to U(x)
and
t:{\Sigma}\to U(x^{\prime})
are étale bijections onto their images.

Given
|y|\in|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
, first note that
if
U(|y|)
is any neighbourhood of
|y|\in|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
such that
{\rm cl}(U(|y|))\subset\bigcap_{|y|\in|V_{J}|}\,|V_{J}|
,
then the properness condition holds by Lemma
3.2.5
(iii).
We also suppose that, with
H_{|y|}=\bigcap_{|y|\in|V_{J}|}J
as in Lemma
4.2.1
(i), for one (and hence any) lift
x\in V_{H_{|y|}}
of
y
to
H_{|y|}
, the components of the inverse image
\pi^{-1}(U(|y|)\cap V_{H_{|y|}}

are freely permuted by the group
G_{H_{|y|}}/G_{x}
, where
G_{x}
is the isotropy group at
x
. It follows that for any lift
y\in V_{J}
of
|y|
the components of
\pi^{-1}(U(|y|)\cap V_{J}
are freely permuted by the group
G_{J}/G_{x}
, and we pick
U(y)

to be the component of
\pi^{-1}(U(|y|)\cap V_{J}
that contains
y
. Then
U(y)
is invariant under the action of the isotropy group
G_{y}
. To check the last condition,
suppose first that
U(x)\subset V_{I}
,

U(x^{\prime})\subset V_{J}
where
I\subset J
, and consider the component of
{\rm Mor}(U(x),U(x^{\prime}))
containing
m=(I,J,y,g)
. Then
t(m)=y\in U(x^{\prime})\subset{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}
and
s(m)=g*\rho_{I}(y)
, where
\rho_{I}:{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\to V_{I}
quotients by the free action of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}
. But by assumption the images of
U(x^{\prime})
by
G_{J{\smallsetminus}I}
are disjoint. Therefore given
s(m)
and
g
(both uniquely determined by
m
), there is at most one possible choice for
y
. Since the argument when
J\subset I
is similar, this completes the proof.
∎

4.3.
Bundles over
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

In order to model the category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E
in (
-
‣
2.1.2
).
we will consider the product of the category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
as above with the trivial category with objects

E
and only identity morphisms. Here
we suppose that
E_{i}
is a vector space on which
G_{i}
acts, so that
G:=\prod_{i\in A}G_{i}
acts on
E:=\prod_{i\in A}E_{i}
by the product action (where
A:=\{1,\dots,N\}
). The statement in (ii) below should be contrasted with the fact that, when the maps
\rho_{IJ}
have closed graph, the space
|{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}^{\times G}|\simeq|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is Hausdorff by Proposition
4.1.1
(i). Again, we restate the relevant results from §
4.1
for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
be as in Proposition
4.2.4
, and
consider the product category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E=(Q\times E,{{{\bf Q}}}\times{\bf E})

as follows:

Q\times E={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\subset A}V_{I}\times E,\quad{{{\bf Q}}}\times{\bf E}=\bigsqcup_{I\subset J}{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\times E,}

with groupoid completion
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E=({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})\times E
. Suppose further that
G
acts on
E
as above.
Then:

(i)

The category
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E
is étale, and
G
acts on both
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E
and its groupoid completion
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E
by the product action on objects and morphisms.

(ii)

If the action of
G
in
E
is trivial, then

|({{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E)^{\times G}|=|({\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E)^{\times G}|
is Hausdorff. On the other hand,
if
G_{j}
acts nontrivially on
E_{j}
for some
j
,
and for some
J
with
j\in J
and some
H\subset(J{\smallsetminus}\{j\})
there is a point
x\in{\rm Fr}({\widetilde{V}}_{HJ})\subset V_{J}
that does not lie in
{\widetilde{V}}_{FJ}
for any
F\subset(J{\smallsetminus}\{j\})
, then
the realization

|({{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E)^{\times G}|=|({\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E)^{\times G}|
is not Hausdorff.

(iii)

There is a bundle
{\rm pr}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with fiber
E
that supports a
G
-action (given on objects as in (i))
such that the functorial inclusion
{\iota}:{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}={\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
defined in Proposition
4.2.4
(iii) lifts to a
G
-equivariant functor

{\iota}_{E}:({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
that induces a homeomorphism

|({\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E)^{\times G}|=|({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\times G}|\;=\;|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E|/G\simeq|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}|/G.

Further, under the conditions detailed in (ii) above,
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}|/G
is not Hausdorff.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the fact that
the projection maps
\rho_{IJ}
are equivariant
with respect to the projection
G_{J}\to G_{I}
.

To prove (ii), first note that if the action of
G
on
E
is trivial then

|({\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E)^{\times G}|=(|{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}|/G)\times E=|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|\times E
by Proposition
4.2.4
(iii), and so is Hausdorff.
To prove the second claim in (ii), we show that under the stated conditions there is a sequence
(x_{k},e)\in Q\times E
whose image in
|({\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E)^{\times G}|
has two different limits.
Consider a point
x\in V_{J}
that lies in the frontier

{\rm Fr}({\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}):={\rm cl}({\widetilde{V}}_{HJ})\cap{\rm cl}(V_{J}{\smallsetminus}{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ})
of
{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}
but does not lie in
{\widetilde{V}}_{FJ}
for any
F\subset(J{\smallsetminus}\{j\})
.
44
44

44

For example, we might take
H=J{\smallsetminus}\{j\}
and
x\in{\rm Fr}({\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}){\smallsetminus}\bigcup_{F\subset H}{\widetilde{V}}_{FJ}
(assuming this set is nonempty).

By taking
I=J
in (
4.2.2
), we see that for
F\subset J
,
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
only contains the partial actions of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}F}
on
{\widetilde{V}}_{FJ}
.
Therefore if
g\in G_{j}{\smallsetminus}\{{\rm id}\}
there is no morphism in
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
with source
(J,x)
and target
(J,g*x)
.
Thus
(J,x)\not\sim_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}(J,g*x)
. Now
choose a convergent sequence
x_{k}\to x
, where
x_{k}\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}
.
Then we have
(J,x_{k})\sim_{{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}}(J,g*x_{k})
since the action of
G_{J{\smallsetminus}H}
on
{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}
is included in the morphisms of
{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}
.
We then have

(J,x_{k},e)\sim_{\mathcal{C}}(J,g*x_{k},e),\quad(J,x_{k},e)\sim_{\mathcal{C}}(J,g*x_{k},g*e).

But if we choose
(g,e)\in G_{j}\times E_{j}
so that
g*e\neq e
we have

(J,x,e)\not\sim_{\mathcal{C}}(J,g*x,e).

Thus the sequence
|(x_{k},e)|
has two limits
|(x,e)|
and
|(g*x,e)|
in
|{\mathcal{C}}|
, as claimed.

To prove (iii), consider the category
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}=(X_{\mathcal{V}}^{E},{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})
with

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle X_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}:={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}\,V_{I}\times E,\quad\ {\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}:=\bigsqcup_{I,J\in{\mathcal{I}},I\sim J}}\,{\widetilde{V}}_{(I\wedge J)(I\vee J)}\times G_{I\wedge J}\times E,

where

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle(s\times t)\,(I,J,y,g,e)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\,(I,\,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y),g^{-1}*e),\;(J,\rho_{J}(y),e)\bigr)

\displaystyle{\rm id}_{(I,x,e)}:=(I,I,x,{\rm id},e),

\displaystyle\quad(I,J,x,g,e)^{-1}:=(J,I,g^{-1}*x,g^{-1},g^{-1}*e),

and (where defined) composition is given by

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle(I,J,x,g,e)\circ(J,K,y,h,e^{\prime}):=\bigl(I,K,\rho_{I\vee K}(v),k(hg)|_{I\wedge K},e^{\prime}\bigr)

where
v,k
are defined in (
4.1.4
). Then
{\rm pr}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is a bundle with projection functor given by

(I,x,e)\mapsto(I,x),\quad(I,J,y,g,e)\mapsto(I,J,y,g),

and structural map

\mu:{\bf X}\,{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{{\rm pr}}(X\times E)\to X\times E,\quad\bigl((I,J,y,g),(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{IJ}(y),e)\bigr)\mapsto(J,y,g*e).

Then
G
acts on the object space
X_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}=Q\times E
by the product action as in (i) above, and acts on the morphism space
{\bf X}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
by the following lift of the action on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
given in (
4.2.23
):

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle g*(I,y,e)

\displaystyle=(I,g|_{I}*y,g*e),

\displaystyle g*\bigl(I,J,y,h,e\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(I,J,g|_{I\vee J}*y,g|_{I\wedge J}\,h\,g|_{I\wedge J}^{-1},\,g*e\bigr).

We now define
{\iota}_{E}:{\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
to be the identity map on objects and the inclusion on morphisms.
This is equivariant, as claimed. Hence the final claim in (iii) follows from (ii) and the fact that

|({{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E)^{\times G}|\simeq|{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E|/G|
by Lemma
3.3.8
(iii). ∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

Notice that the
G
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
is
not
an inner action, and hence is not lifted from the inner
G
-action on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
by the process of Lemma
3.3.6
. For example, because the group
G_{A{\smallsetminus}I}
acts trivially on
V_{I}\subset X_{\mathcal{V}}
, the lifted inner action is also trivial on
V_{I}\times E_{A}
. But the action of
G_{A{\smallsetminus}I}
described in (
4.3.4
) need not be trivial on the
E_{A}
factor in
V_{I}\times E_{A}
. This is the reason for the conditions on the kernel of the functor
\tau
in Proposition
4.3.3
.

{\Diamond}

We next explain the formal properties of the
G
-equivariant functor
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
whose existence is claimed in Theorem
2.1.2
(v). Recall from Proposition
4.1.2
(ii) that

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}=\widehat{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
is the groupoid completion of the nonsingular étale category
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
.
It supports an action of
G
by Lemma
4.2.3
.

Proposition 4.3.3
.

In the situation of Lemma
4.3.1
, suppose given
a bundle
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
over
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

with induced inner
G
-action as in Definition
3.3.4
.
Suppose further that there is a
functor
{\tau}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
such that

-

the induced map on object spaces is
G
-equivariant

-

its restriction to each fiber
(I,y)\times E
is linear with kernel that includes the subspace
E_{A{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
, where
H_{y}=\min\{H\ \big|\ y\in{\widetilde{Q}}_{HI}\}
;

-

{\mathcal{P}}\circ{\tau}={\iota}\circ{\rm pr}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
,

where
{\iota}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the functorial inclusion from Proposition
4.1.3
.

Then:

(i)

{\tau}
extends uniquely to a functor
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

(ii)

Moreover

\tau

factors as the composite
\tau=\tau_{E}\circ{\iota}_{E}
in the following
commutative diagram of functors

\textstyle{({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})\times E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\scriptstyle{\;\hskip 8.19447pt{\iota}_{E}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\tau_{E}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{P}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\iota}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\;=}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}},}

where
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
is as in Lemma
4.3.1
(iii).

(iii)

Both functors
\tau_{E}
and
\tau=\tau_{E}\circ{\iota}_{E}
are
G
-equivariant. In particular,
\tau(I,x,g*e)=g*\tau(I,g^{-1}*x,e).

Proof.

We saw in Lemma
4.2.1
(i) that the category
{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
3.2.9
(iv); namely it is a poset in which each equivalence class has a unique minimum with morphisms to all other elements. Since the product
{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}\times E
also has this property, the functor
{\tau}
extends by Lemma
3.2.9
(iv). This proves (i).

Towards (ii),
consider the full subcategory

({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E

with the following object space:

(4.3.5)

\displaystyle(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ ={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I}}\bigl\{(I,x,e)\in V_{I}\times E\ \big|\ e\in E_{H_{x}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\ ={\textstyle\bigsqcup_{I}\;\bigl(\bigcup_{K\subsetneq H\subset I}}\bigl({\widetilde{V}}_{HI}{\smallsetminus}{\widetilde{V}}_{KI}\bigr)\times E_{H}\bigr)

where
H_{x}
is as in (
4.2.2
). Thus

(4.3.6)

\displaystyle H_{x}=\min\bigl\{H\ |\ |x|\in|V_{H}|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|\bigr\}=\min\bigl\{H\ |\ |x|\in|V_{H}|/G=|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}|/G\bigr\}=:H_{|x|}

where the above equality holds because the sets
V_{H}
are
G_{H}
-invariant.
Below
we denote by
{\rm pr}_{E}^{\prime}
the (non continuous) projection map
X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E\to(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E)^{\prime}
that maps the fiber
(I,x)\times E
at
(I,x)
to

(I,x)\times E_{H_{x}}
with kernel
E_{A{\smallsetminus}H_{x}}
. Note that this extends to a
G
-equivariant functor
{\rm pr}_{E}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\prime}
.

Denote by
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}
the corresponding full subcategory of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
with objects
(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E)^{\prime}
.
Since the group
G_{A{\smallsetminus}I}
acts trivially on
\bigl\{(I,x,e)\ \big|\ e\in E_{H_{x}}\bigr\}\subset V_{I}\times E_{I}
, the action of
G
on
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}
is an inner action given by
lifting the inner action on
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
as follows:

(4.3.7)

\displaystyle\overline{{\alpha}}_{E^{\prime}}:(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E)^{\prime}\times G\to{\rm Mor}_{({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\quad((I,x,e),g)\mapsto(I,I,g|_{I}*x,g|_{I},g|_{I}*e).

Hence, because the only morphisms in
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}
that are not in
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\prime}
come from the
G
-action, the functoriality of the lifting construction (see Remark
3.3.7
) implies that
any functor
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{W}}
that

(a)

covers the inclusion
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
on the base, and

(b)

is given by a
G
-equivariant map
(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E)^{\prime}\to W
on objects that is fiberwise linear,

extends to a functor
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{W}}
.

Now consider the statement in the proposition. Since the categories
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
have the same object space, the only claim that requires proof is that the map
X_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\to W
on object spaces induced by
\tau
does extend to a functor
\tau_{E}
between these categories.
In other words we have to show that the fact that
\tau
is
G
-equivariant on objects implies that it respects the additional morphisms in

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
corresponding to the group action.
But the hypotheses on the functor
\tau
imply that the induced map on objects factors through the projection
{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{E}:X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E\to(X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E)^{\prime}
.
Thus
\tau
induces a functor
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{W}}
that satisfies the conditions (a), (b) above and hence extends to a

G
-equivariant functor

\tau^{\prime}_{E}:({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{W}}
.
We now define
\tau_{E}:=\tau_{E}^{\prime}\circ{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{V}},E}
, where
{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{V}},E}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}\to({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}
is the extension of
{\rm pr}_{E}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\prime}
to
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E}
. This satisfies both (ii) and (iii).
∎

Remark 4.3.4
.

The
G
-equivariance of
\tau
implies that it extends to a functor

\tau^{G}:({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}},

that by Proposition
4.3.3

factors as

\displaystyle({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\times G}\longrightarrow({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\times G}\;\longrightarrow(({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime})^{\times G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\iota}^{\times G}}}{{\longrightarrow}}({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau^{\prime}_{E}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}},

where
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}
has objects as in (
4.3.5
) and

{\iota}^{\times G}
is the functor described in Lemma
3.3.8
(v),
which exists because, as noted above, the
G
action on
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}
is an inner action.
We saw in the preceding proposition that
\tau^{\prime}_{E}:({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
covers the identity map on the base
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and that the
G
action on both spaces is the lift of the inner action on the base. Therefore, there are enough morphisms
m
in the base for the
G
-equivariance of
\tau_{E}^{\prime}
to be captured in the identity

(4.3.8)

\displaystyle\mu_{W}\bigl(m,\tau^{\prime}_{E}(w)\bigr)=\tau^{\prime}_{E}\bigl(\mu_{E}(m,w)\bigr),\quad m\in{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}},\;w\in{\rm Obj}_{({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{E})^{\prime}},\;P(w)=s(m).

where
\mu_{\bullet}
is the structure function of the relevant bundle that expresses how morphisms of the base lift to morphisms on the total space; see Definition
3.4.1
.

{\Diamond}

We now use the results of Proposition
4.3.3
to construct multisections of the bundle

{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with properties as described in Proposition
4.1.6
.
As explained at the beginning of §
5.1
, a multisection
{\Lambda}
of a bundle
{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}

is a functor
{\Lambda}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}

with a
local section structure
near each
x_{0}\in X
as follows: for each
x_{0}\in X
there is an open set
U(x_{0})\subset X

and finitely many sc
+
-sections
{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{s}}_{k}:U(x_{0})\to W
with associated positive rational numbers
{\sigma}_{1},\dots,{\sigma}_{k}
such that
\sum_{i}{\sigma}_{i}=1
and

{\Lambda}(w)={\textstyle\sum_{\{i\in\{1,\dots k\}\,|\,{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(P(w))=w\}}{\sigma}_{i}.}

The support
{\rm supp\,}({\Lambda})
is given by

{\rm supp\,}({\Lambda})=\{w\in W\ |\ {\Lambda}(w)>0\}.

If
{\Lambda}
is a multisection of
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
and
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is any functor, then
the formula
{\Lambda}^{\prime}:={\Lambda}\circ\Psi
defines a
pullback multisection
of the pullback bundle

\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
defined in Lemma
3.4.4
(ii).
The first part of the next lemma shows that there is a notion of the
pushforward of a multisection
of

\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
,
provided that the functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is sufficiently well behaved.
(Compare similar results in
[
HWZ
, Thm.13.4.1]
). The second part is exactly what is needed
to show that the functor
\tau
in Proposition
4.3.3
can be used to push forward multisections.
In the applications in §
5.3
and §
5.4
we will, of course, need to
take into account the extra analytic conditions required to control compactness and smoothness.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

(i)

Let
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be an equivalence,
let
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
a bundle with pullback

\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
, and denote by
\Psi:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}

the lift of
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
from Lemma
3.4.4
.
If

{\Lambda}^{\prime}:\psi^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}

is a multisection, then the following defines a multisection of
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
:

-

for each
w\in W
there is
w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
and a morphism in
{\mathcal{W}}
from
w
to
\Psi(w^{\prime})
, and we define

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle\psi_{*}({\Lambda}^{\prime})(w):={\Lambda}^{\prime}(w^{\prime}).

(ii)

Suppose that
{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
are two bundles and that the functor
\Psi:{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is the lift of a functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
that is the identity map on
X^{\prime}=X
and is such that
\Psi({\mathcal{W}}^{\prime})
is a full subcategory of
{\mathcal{W}}
that is invariant under the morphisms
(m,w)\in{\bf W}
in
{\mathcal{W}}
in the sense that any morphism in
{\mathcal{W}}
with source or target in the image
\Psi(W^{\prime})
is in fact contained in the image
\Psi({\bf W}^{\prime})
.
Then, given a multisection

{\Lambda}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
,
the following formula defines a multisection of
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
:

(4.3.10)

\displaystyle\Psi_{*}({\Lambda}^{\prime})(w):

\displaystyle=\sum_{w^{\prime}\in\Psi^{-1}(w)}{\Lambda}^{\prime}(w^{\prime}),

where the sum is defined to be
0
when
\Psi^{-1}(w)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Consider (i). To see that
\psi_{*}({\Lambda}^{\prime}):W\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is well defined, we note first that every
w\in W
is equivalent w.r.t.
\sim_{{\mathcal{W}}}
to some element
\Psi(w^{\prime})
since

|\Psi|:|{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}|\to|{\mathcal{W}}|
is surjective, and second that the injectivity of
|\Psi|
and the fact that
{\Lambda}^{\prime}
is constant on the equivalence classes of
\sim_{{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}}
imply that
{\Lambda}(w)
does not depend on the choice of
w^{\prime}
such that

\Psi(w^{\prime})\sim_{{\mathcal{W}}}w.

Further,
\psi_{*}({\Lambda})
is a functor because, by construction, it is constant on the equivalence classes of
\sim_{{\mathcal{W}}}
.
Finally, because
\psi_{*}({\Lambda})
is a functor, every morphism
m:x_{0}\mapsto x_{1}
in
{\mathcal{X}}
extends to a locally invertible étale map and has a family of fiberwise lifts
that take a local section structure for
{\Lambda}
at
x_{0}
to one at
x_{1}
. Hence,
because
|\psi|:|{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}|\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
is surjective, it suffices to check that there is a
local section structure near every point
x_{0}\in\psi(X^{\prime})
. To this end, choose
x_{0}^{\prime}\in\psi^{-1}(x_{0})
and
U(x_{0}^{\prime})
so small that

\psi^{\prime}|_{U(x_{0}^{\prime})}
is injective. Then, because
\psi^{\prime}
is étale,
U(x_{0})=\psi^{\prime}(U(x_{0}^{\prime}))
is a neighbourhood of
x_{0}
and
the local section structure
\bigl({\mathfrak{s}}_{i}^{\prime}:U(x_{0}^{\prime})\to W^{\prime},{\sigma}_{i}^{\prime}\bigr)
at
x_{0}^{\prime}
pushes forward to the local section structure

\bigl({\mathfrak{s}}_{i}=\Psi\circ{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}\circ\psi^{-1}:U(x_{0})\to W,{\sigma}_{i}^{\prime}\bigr)
at
x_{0}
.
This completes the proof of (i).

In (ii), the map on the base is the identity, while the map on the fiber need be neither injective not surjective.
Nevertheless, the given formula for
\Psi_{*}({\Lambda}^{\prime})
defines a functor on
{\mathcal{W}}
because the image
\Psi({\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}})
contains all morphisms in
{\mathcal{W}}
with source or target in
\Psi(W)
. Note also that
the numbers
\Psi_{*}({\Lambda}^{\prime})(w)
sum to
1
over each fiber of
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
, and that because
\psi
is the identity on objects, each family
({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}_{i})
of local sections at
x
for
{\Lambda}^{\prime}
gives rise to a family
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i}:=\Psi\circ{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}^{\prime})
of local sections for
{\Lambda}
.
∎

It remains to prove Proposition
4.1.6
that
we now restate for the convenience of the reader.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

Let the bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, group
G
, and functor
\breve{\tau}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
with extension
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}{\Gamma}}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
be as
in Proposition
4.1.5
.

(i)

Each
e\in E
induces a multisection functor
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}

given by

(4.3.11)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}(w)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{|G|}\ \#\{h\in G\,|\,\tau(P_{\mathcal{V}}(w),h*e)=w\},

where
\#
denotes the number of elements in a finite set.

(ii)

This multisection
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}
is globally structured in the sense of Definition
3.4.5
by the sections
({\mathfrak{s}}_{g})_{g\in G}
and correspondence
{\kappa}:{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\to\{b:G\to G,{\rm bijection}\}
given by

(4.3.12)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}_{g}(I,x)

\displaystyle=\tau(I,x,g*e),\ \quad{\kappa}(I,J,y,g)(h)=g*h.

(iii)

The triple
({\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e},({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}},{\kappa})
is structurable in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.6]
, and determines a unique structured multisection
[{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e},{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{S}},\tau]
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.13.3.8]
, where
(U(x)_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}
is
the system of good neighbourhoods in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
defined in Lemma
4.2.9
.

Proof.

To prove (i)
we must show that if
the bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, group
G
, and functor
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}

are as in Proposition
4.3.3
, then each

e\in E
determines a multisection functor
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}

given by

(4.3.13)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}(w)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{|G|}\#\{h\in G\,|\,\tau(P_{\mathcal{V}}(w),h*e)=w\},

where
|G|
denotes the order of the group
G
.

For fixed
e\in E
, consider the functor
{\Lambda}^{{\smallsetminus}G}_{e}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
defined by setting

\displaystyle{\Lambda}^{{\smallsetminus}G}_{e}(x,e^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1/|G|\;\mbox{ if }e^{\prime}\in G*e,\\
0\;\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

This is a
G
-equivariant functor with support
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times\{G*e\}
, and hence clearly is a multisection of the bundle

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
. Because
{\Lambda}_{e}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
is
G
-invariant, we may also consider it as a multisection of
the bundle
({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\times G}\to({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G})^{\times G}
(see Lemma
3.3.8
and
Proposition
4.2.4
(iii)). Next note that because it is
G
-equivariant,
\tau
extends to a functor

\tau^{G}:({\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E)^{\times G}\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, and it is immediate that its image
is invariant under the morphisms of
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
in the sense defined in Lemma
4.3.5
(ii). Hence
there is a well defined pushforward multisection

(\tau^{G})_{*}({\Lambda}^{{\smallsetminus}G}_{e})
, and it follows by comparing (
4.3.10
) with (
4.3.13
) that

(\tau^{G})_{*}({\Lambda}^{{\smallsetminus}G}_{e})={\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},e}.

This completes the proof of (i).

To prove (ii), note that because
{\kappa}
is locally constant, it suffices to check that (
4.3.12
) holds; namely we require that that for all morphisms
m
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}

{\mathfrak{s}}_{{\kappa}(m)(h)}(t(m))=\mu\bigl(m,{\mathfrak{s}}_{h}(s(m))\bigr)\in W_{t(m)}.

With
m=(I,J,y,g)
we therefore require that

{\mathfrak{s}}_{gh}(J,\rho_{J}(y))=\mu\bigl(m,{\mathfrak{s}}_{h}(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y))
. Equivalently, since

{\mathfrak{s}}_{h}(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y))=\tau(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y),h*e)
, we require
that

\tau(J,\rho_{J}(y),gh*e)=\mu\bigl((I,J,y,g),\tau(I,g^{-1}*\rho_{I}(y),h*e)\bigr)

Since every morphism in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the composite of a morphism
(I,J,y,{\rm id})
in

{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
with a morphism
(J,J,y,g)
given by the
G
action in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, it suffices to check the above identity for these two types of morphisms. But this identity holds for the morphisms in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}
since
\tau
is a functor
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times G\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
by Proposition
4.3.3
(i), and it holds for the morphisms that give the
G
action since
\tau
is
G
-equivariant by
Proposition
4.3.3
(iii).

Finally the claims in (iii) hold by Proposition
3.4.11
.
∎

5.
Proofs of Main Theorems

This section proves Theorem
2.1.2
and Corollary
2.1.3
. Thus we consider a sc-Fredholm section functor
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
of a strong bundle
{{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}}
with compact solution set
S:=|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
.
We begin in §
5.2
by constructing suitable étale data as described in §
4.1
from a family of local uniformizers for the ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
that covers a neighbourhood of the compact zero set of the polyfold-Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
. We then apply the constructions in §
4
to obtain the bottom row of the diagram (
-
‣
2.1.2
).
This is the content of Theorem
5.2.6
.
In Theorem
5.3.1
we construct the pullback bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
of the strong bundle
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
, and establish the existence and properties of the right hand square in (
-
‣
2.1.2
). Finally, under the assumption that the local uniformizers are chosen so that they extend to local stabilizations as in Lemma
5.4.1
that are compatible with suitable compactness controlling data, Theorem
5.4.2
constructs the left hand square in (
-
‣
2.1.2
).
The proof of Theorem
2.1.2
is given in §
5.4
.
Corollary
2.1.3
then follows by applying Proposition
4.1.6
.

5.1.
Fundamental classes induced by Fredholm sections over polyfolds

In this subsection we consider a compact moduli space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
without formal boundary and of expected dimension
d
(given by the Fredholm index of local models). For example,
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
might be the Gromov-Witten moduli space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{g,k}(M,J,A)
of
J
-holomorphic curves in homology class
A\in H_{*}(M)
in a closed symplectic manifold
M
, of fixed genus
g\geq 0
and with
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
marked points, with
d=\dim M+2c_{1}(A)+2k+6g-6
.
In that setting, a Kuranishi atlas
{\mathcal{K}}
for
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
consists of
an interconnected family of local models without boundary, and we showed in
[
MW3
]
how to construct a fundamental class
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{\mathcal{K}}\in\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
by compatibly perturbing the local models.
The main purpose of this section is to construct an analogous rational Čech homology class
45
45

45

See the Appendix for a brief discussion of rational Čech homology.

[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{f}\in\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
for polyfold descriptions of
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
as in Theorem
5.1.4
.
We also briefly discuss the situation when
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
has nonempty formal boundary.

Remark 5.1.1
.

(Čech homology and Fundamental classes)

We use rational Čech homology here due to its identification with singular homology for finite simplicial complexes and its tautness property explained in
[
MW2
, Remark 8.2.4]
and Appendix
A
.
This allows for a perturbative construction of
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]
as follows:
Suppose the moduli space is identified
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}\simeq S\subset Y
with a compact subset
S
of a metric space
Y
. Then we can construct a fundamental class
[S]\in\check{H}_{d}(S;{\mathbb{Q}})
and push it forward to
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]\in\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
via
\phi:S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
.
To construct
[S]
we choose a nested sequence of open subsets
S\subset B_{k}\subset B_{k-1}\subset Y
such that
\bigcap_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}B_{k}=S
.
For example, the balls
B_{k}:=\{p\in Y\,|\,\inf_{s\in S}d(p,s)<1/k\}
of radius
1/k
provide such nested neighbourhoods of
S
.
Then these inclusions induce a system of maps
\check{H}_{d}(S;{\mathbb{Q}})\to\check{H}_{d}(B_{k+1};{\mathbb{Q}})\to\check{H}_{d}(B_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
, and the meaning of tautness is that their inverse limit equals the Čech homology

\check{H}_{d}(S;{\mathbb{Q}})\;\simeq\;\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,\check{H}_{d}(B_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
.

We can use this limit to define a fundamental class

[S]:=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,(\phi_{k})_{*}[Z_{k}]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(S;{\mathbb{Q}})

by constructing continuous maps
\phi_{k}:Z_{k}\to B_{k}
from sets
Z_{k}
(such as those in Lemma
5.1.3
) that carry natural
fundamental classes
[Z_{k}]\in\check{H}_{d}(Z_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Existence of this limit follows if we can ensure compatibility
(\phi_{k})_{*}[Z_{k}]=(\iota_{k\ell})_{*}\bigl((\phi_{\ell})_{*}[Z_{\ell}]\bigr)\in\check{H}_{d}(B_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
for
k<\ell
, where
\iota_{k\ell}:B_{\ell}\to B_{k}
is the inclusion map.
This in turn can be achieved by constructing cobordisms
Z_{k\ell}
with boundary
\partial Z_{k\ell}\simeq Z^{-}_{k}\sqcup Z_{\ell}
and continuous maps
\phi_{k\ell}:Z_{k\ell}\to B_{k}
that restrict to
\phi_{k}
resp.
\iota_{k\ell}\circ\phi_{\ell}
on the boundary.

{\Diamond}

Definition 5.1.2
.

A
polyfold model without boundary

{\mathcal{E}}=\bigl(f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}},\psi\bigr)

of Fredholm index
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for a compact Hausdorff space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
consists of the following data:

\bullet

{{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}}
is a strong bundle over an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
with empty boundary
\partial X=\emptyset
.

\bullet

f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is an oriented sc-Fredholm section functor of constant Fredholm index
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

\bullet

\psi:|f^{-1}(0)|\to{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
is a homeomorphism.

Here the homeomorphism
|f^{-1}(0)|\to{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
guarantees that the zero set
|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is compact.
On the other hand, the homeomorphism
|f^{-1}(0)|\to{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
then induces a metrizable topology on
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
since
|f^{-1}(0)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
is a subset of the metrizable space
|{\mathcal{X}}|
by
[
HWZ
, Thm.7.3.1]
.
Thus rational Čech homology
\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
is well defined, and we will apply its tautness properties as
explained in
[
MW2
, Remark 8.2.4]
and Appendix
A
to construct the fundamental class
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]\in\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Suppose that
{\partial}{\mathcal{X}}=0
and
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
is an oriented sc-Fredholm section functor of constant Fredholm index
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with compact zero set
|f^{-1}(0)|
and
\Lambda:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is a sc
+
-multisection such that
\Lambda\circ f:X\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a compact, oriented, tame branched, ep
+
-subgroupoid as in
[
HWZ
, Thms.15.3.7, 15.4.1]
.
Then the solution set
|S_{\Lambda\circ f}|:=|{\rm supp\,}({\Lambda}\circ f)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|

carries a natural fundamental class
[{\Lambda}\circ f]\in\check{H}_{d}(|S_{\Lambda\circ f}|;{\mathbb{Q}})
.
In particular, if
M:=|S_{\Lambda\circ f}|
is an oriented manifold, and
\Lambda\circ f\equiv\theta\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is constant, then the fundamental class is
[{\Lambda}\circ f]=\theta[M]
.

Moreover, consider two such sc
+
-multisections
\Lambda^{0},\Lambda^{1}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
whose solution sets
|S_{\Lambda^{0}\circ f}|,|S_{\Lambda^{1}\circ f}|\subset B
are contained in a common open subset
B\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
. Suppose that
\Theta:[0,1]\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a compact, oriented, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid with support
|{\rm supp\,}\Theta|\subset[0,1]\times B
and restrictions
\Theta|_{[0,{\varepsilon})\times{\mathcal{X}}}(t,x)={\Lambda}^{0}(f(x))
,
\Theta|_{(1-{\varepsilon},1]\times{\mathcal{X}}}(t,x)={\Lambda}^{1}(f(x))
for some
{\varepsilon}>0
.
If the orientation of
{\Lambda}^{1}\circ f=\Theta|_{\{1\}\times{\mathcal{X}}}
given by boundary restriction of
\Theta
as in
[
HWZ
, Def.9.3.8]
agrees with the orientation induced by
f
via the solution set convention in §
2.3
(generalized to multisections in
[
HWZ
, §9]
), and the orientation of
{\Lambda}^{0}\circ f=\Theta|_{\{0\}\times{\mathcal{X}}}
given by restriction of
\Theta
is opposite to the orientation induced by
f
, then

(\phi^{0})_{*}[{\Lambda}^{0}\circ f]=(\phi^{1})_{*}[{\Lambda}^{1}\circ f]\in\check{H}_{d}(B;{\mathbb{Q}}),

where
\phi^{i}:|S_{\Lambda^{i}\circ f}|\to B
denotes the inclusion map for
i=0,1
.

Proof.

To explain what is meant by a “natural fundamental class” we first describe the structure of
the support
{\rm supp\,}(\theta)=\{x\in X\ |\ \theta(x)>0\}
of the functor
\theta:={\Lambda}\circ f
.
By
[
HWZ
, Def.9.1.2]
, each point
x\in{\rm supp\,}(\theta)
has a local branching structure.
This means that
there is an open neighbourhood
U(x)
of
x\in X
and a finite collection of open
smooth
n
-dimensional submanifolds
M_{x,i}\ni x,i\in I_{x}
of
X
, (called local branches) together with weights
{\sigma}_{x,i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{>0}
such that each inclusion
M_{x,i}\to U(x)
is proper, and

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle\theta(y)={\textstyle\sum_{i\in I_{x}:y\in M_{x,i}}{\sigma}_{x,i}\quad\mbox{ for all }\ y\in U(x).}

In particular
U(x)=\bigcup_{i\in I_{x}}M_{x,i}
.
We define the
branch locus

{\rm Br}
of
S:=|{\rm supp\,}(\theta)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
to consist of all
z\in S\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
that do not have a neighbourhood in
S
that is entirely contained in one of the sets
|M_{x,i_{0}}|
. By
[
HWZ
, Def.9.2.2,Lem.9.2.3]
, the complement
S{\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}
is the image in
|{\rm supp\,}(\theta)|
of the set of
good points
in
{\rm supp\,}(\theta)
. Note that by definition, given a local branching structure
(M_{x,i})_{i\in I_{x}}

each good point
y
has a neighbourhood
V(y)\subset{\rm supp\,}(\theta)
such that
V(y)\cap M_{x,i}=V(y)\cap M_{x,j}
for all
i,j\in I_{x}
for which
y\in M_{x,i}\cap M_{x,j}
.
Further
{\rm Br}
is closed and nowhere dense in
S
by
[
HWZ
, Lem.9.2.7]
.

With this understood, we require that the fundamental class
[\theta,{\mathfrak{o}}]
has the following property:
for each local branching structure

(M_{x,i})_{i\in I_{x}}
over
U(x)
and good point
y\in U(x)
,
the restriction of
[\theta,{\mathfrak{o}}]
to
|V(y)|
is given by

(5.1.2)

\displaystyle{\textstyle\rho_{S,|V(y)|}([\theta,{\mathfrak{o}}])=\sum_{i\in I_{x},y\in M_{x,i}}{\sigma}_{x,i}\,\mu_{|V(y)|}\in\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}\bigl(|V(y)|\bigr)}

where
\mu_{|V(y)|}
denotes the pushforward by
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}:V(y)\to|V(y)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
of the fundamental class
\mu_{V(y)}
of the oriented
n
-manifold
V(y)
described in (
A.3
). To see that
the condition in (
5.1.2
) makes sense, notice firstly that the map
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}:V(y)\to|V(y)|
is proper
because (as in
[
HWZ
, Prop.9.1.12]
) we can choose
V(y)
to be a subset of a local uniformizer for
{\mathcal{X}}
, and secondly that
the pushforward class
\mu_{|V(y)|}
is defined for proper maps
by the discussion before (
A.5
). Another important point about equation (
5.1.1
) is that it uses the Çech homology theory
\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(\cdot)
that is analogous to locally finite singular homology and is dual to compactly supported rational Çech cohomology. Thus it equals the standard Çech homology
\check{H}_{n}(\cdot)
on compact spaces, but has very different behavior on noncompact spaces.

We next claim that for each local branching structure

(M_{x,i})_{i\in I_{x}}
over
U(x)
the condition (
5.1.2
) determines the restriction of

[\theta,{\mathfrak{o}}]
to
|U(x)|{\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}
. Indeed,
|U(x)|{\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}
is a (possibly infinite) union of connected components
|C|
,
where
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(|C|)\cap U(x)=:C
is the union of the submanifolds
V(y)
with
|y|\in|C|
.
Thus
C
can be identified with
an open subset of an oriented
n
-manifold.
The set
C
may have a finite number of connected components
(C_{i})_{i\in I_{x,|C|}}
(where
I_{x,|C|}
indexes the relevant local branches), but, because
|C|
is disjoint from the branch locus, each such component satisfies
|C_{i}|=|C|
. This
allows us to show that the restriction of

[\theta,{\mathfrak{o}}]
to
|C|
is simply a multiple of
\mu_{|C|}
. Indeed, because restricting to an open subset
V^{\prime}\subset V
takes the fundamental class of
V
to that of
V^{\prime}
,
this
restriction is
\sum_{i\in I_{x,|C|}}{\sigma}_{x,i}\,\mu_{|C|}
.

Next observe that because

S
is compact and the map
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}:X\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
is open,
S
has a finite open cover by sets
|U_{i}|
, where each
U_{i}\subset X
supports a local branching structure. Assume for some
k\geq 0
that we have constructed the fundamental class
[\theta]_{k}
over
W_{k}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}|U_{j}|

so that (
5.1.2
) holds for each
y
with
|V(y)|\subset W_{k}
,
and for
W:=|U_{k+1}|
define

[\theta]_{k,W}\in\check{H}_{n}(W_{k}\cap W)
by restriction,
[\theta]_{k,W}:=\rho_{W_{k},W_{k}\cap W}([\theta]_{k})
.
As noted above, the requirement (
5.1.2
)
specifies the restriction of
[\theta]
to

W{\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}
.
We will prove the following:

(a)

there is a unique class
[\theta]_{W}\in\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(W)
such that

\rho_{W,W{\smallsetminus}Br}([\theta])=[\theta]_{W{\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}}
.

(b)

\check{H}^{\infty}_{n+1}(W_{k}\cap W)=0
, and

(c)

\rho_{W_{k},W_{k}\cap W}([\theta]_{k})=\rho_{W,W_{k}\cap W}([\theta]_{W})\in\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(W_{k}\cap W)
.

Granted this, the Mayer–Vietoris sequence in (
A.6
) implies that there is a unique class

[\theta]_{k+1}\in\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(W_{k+1})
that restricts to
[\theta]_{k}\oplus[\theta]_{W}\in\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(W_{k})\oplus\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(W)
.
Then
[\theta]_{k+1}
satisfies (
5.1.2
) for each
y
with
|y|\in|W_{k+1}|
, so that the construction of
[\theta]
is completed by a finite number of these steps.

As a preliminary step towards (a), we prove that the homology of the branch locus
{\rm Br}
of
S
vanishes for
q\geq n
; indeed

(5.1.3)

\displaystyle\check{H}^{\infty}_{q}({\rm Br}\cap W_{k})=0,\quad\forall q\geq n,\ k.

To see this, we first claim that
\check{H}^{\infty}_{q}(A)=0,q\geq n
for every closed, proper (i.e.
A\neq Y
) subset
A
of a connected
n
-manifold
Y
. This holds because, by the
universal coefficient theorem
[
Ma
, Cor.4.18]
, we may identify

\check{H}^{\infty}_{q}(A)
with the dual
{\rm Hom}(\check{H}_{c}^{q}(A),{\mathbb{Q}})
of the corresponding cohomology group,
and
\check{H}_{c}^{q}(A)=0
for these
A
by
[
Ma
, Ex.1,p78]
.
But each intersection

{\rm Br}\cap W_{k}

is a finite union of such subsets
A
, since
{\rm Br}
is closed and nowhere dense in
S
by
[
HWZ
, Lem.9.2.7]
, and each
W_{k}
is a finite union of open subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Therefore, (
5.1.3
) holds by an inductive argument using the Mayer-Vietoris sequence in (iv).

Claim (a) now follows by applying the exact sequence (
A.5
) with
i=n
,
A={\rm Br}\cap W
and
Y=W
.
Further (b) holds because of
the exactness of

\check{H}^{\infty}_{n+1}\bigl({\rm Br}\cap(W_{k}\cap W)\bigr)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{n+1}(W_{k}\cap W)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{n+1}\bigl((W_{k}\cap W){\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}\bigr),

and the fact that
(W_{k}\cap W){\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}
is a disjoint union of connected
n
-manifolds so that its homology vanishes in dimension
>n
.
Similarly, to prove (c) it suffices to check that the two classes

\rho_{W_{k},W_{k}\cap W}([\theta]_{k}),\ \rho_{W,W_{k}\cap W}([\theta]_{W})
in

\check{H}^{\infty}_{n}(W_{k}\cap W)
have the same restriction to
(W_{k}\cap W){\smallsetminus}{\rm Br}
.
But, just as in the first paragraph of this proof, it follows from (
5.1.2
) that for each connected component
|C|
of this space
the class
[\theta]_{W}
has the form
{\sigma}_{C}|\mu_{C}|
, where
{\sigma}_{C}
is the sum of the weights
{\sigma}_{x,i}

of the local branches that map to
|C|
. A similar claim holds for
[\theta]_{k}
.
Thus the restriction to
|C|
of each of these two classes is
{\sigma}_{C}|\mu_{C}|
. This completes the definition of
[\theta]

and hence of
[{\Lambda}\circ f]
.

To prove the identity

(\phi^{0})_{*}[{\Lambda}^{0}\circ f]=(\phi^{1})_{*}[{\Lambda}^{1}\circ f]\in\check{H}_{d}(B;{\mathbb{Q}})
in the second paragraph, note that
by (
A.5
) there is a commutative diagram with exact rows

\textstyle{\check{H}^{\infty}_{d+1}\bigl(|{\rm supp\,}(\Theta)|\cap\bigr((0,1)\times B\bigr)\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}\bigl(|{\rm supp\,}(\theta_{0})|\sqcup|{\rm supp\,}(\theta_{1})|\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}\bigl((|{\rm supp\,}(\Theta)|\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{\infty}_{d+1}\bigl((0,1)\times B\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}\bigl(\{0,1\}\times B\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}\bigl([0,1]\times B\bigr)}

where the vertical maps are induced by the inclusion
\phi:|{\rm supp\,}(\Theta)|\to[0,1]\times B
and
\theta_{i}:=\Lambda^{i}\circ f
.
The result will follow if we show that the boundary map

{\delta}:\check{H}^{\infty}_{d+1}\bigl(|{\rm supp\,}(\Theta)|\cap\bigr((0,1)\times B\bigr)\bigr)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}\bigl(|{\rm supp\,}(\theta_{0})|\sqcup|{\rm supp\,}(\theta_{1})|\bigr)

takes the fundamental class of
|{\rm supp\,}(\Theta)|\cap\bigr((0,1)\times B
to the pair
(-[\theta_{0}],[\theta_{1}])
. But by the naturality property
(
5.1.2
) and condition (a) above, it suffices to check this on each component of the set of good points; and it holds in this case because, for each open subset
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the map
{\delta}:\check{H}_{d+1}^{\infty}\bigl((0,1)\times U\bigr)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}\bigl(\{0,1\}\times U\bigr)
takes
\mu_{(0,1)\times U}
to
(-\mu_{\{0\}\times U},\mu_{\{0\}\times U}).
Since
\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}(Y)=\check{H}_{d}(Y)
for compact spaces
Y
, this
completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 5.1.4
.

Every polyfold model without boundary
\bigl(f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}},\psi\bigr)

for a compact space
{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}

induces a well defined
fundamental class

(5.1.4)

[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{f}=\psi_{*}\bigl(\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,(\phi_{k})_{*}[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]\bigr)\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}};{\mathbb{Q}}).

In particular, this limit exists and is independent of the choice of compactness controlling data
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
, nested sequence of open subsets
B_{k}\subset B_{k-1}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
such that
\bigcap_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}B_{k}=|f^{-1}(0)|
, and sc
+
-multisections
\Lambda_{k}
with the following properties.

(i)

Each
\Lambda_{k}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a sc
+
-multisection as in
[
HWZ
, Def.13.2.1]
that is transverse, supported in
{\mathcal{U}}\cap\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(B_{k})
, and bounded
N(\Lambda_{k})<1
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.3.7]
.

(ii)

\Lambda_{k}\circ f:X\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a compact, oriented, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid of
{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §9]
. Its solution set
S_{k}:=|{\rm supp\,}({\Lambda}_{k}\circ f)|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|

carries a fundamental class
[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]\in\check{H}_{d}(S_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
, that is pushed forward to
(\phi_{k})_{*}[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]\in\check{H}_{d}(B_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
by the inclusion map
\phi_{k}:S_{k}\to B_{k}\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
.

Proof.

A nested sequence
B_{k}\subset B_{k-1}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
with
\bigcap_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}B_{k}=|f^{-1}(0)|
exists since
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is metrizable, so we can use balls around
|f^{-1}(0)|
intersected with
|{\mathcal{U}}|
.
For any choice of nested sequence, denote
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}:=\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(B_{k})\subset{\mathcal{U}}\subset X
. Then Remark
3.5.2
guarantees that
(N,{\mathcal{U}}_{k})
controls compactness of
f
.
Now
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.3.7]
guarantees the existence of
(N,{\mathcal{U}}_{k})
-regular sc
+
-multisections
\Lambda_{k}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
. Their domain support in
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}

[
HWZ
, Def.13.2.2.]
guarantees that
\Lambda_{k}(f(x))=0
for all
x\in X{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
,
so that the realization
C_{k}
of the “perturbed solution set”
|S_{\Lambda_{k}\circ f}|=|\{x\in X\,|\,\Lambda_{k}(f(x))>0\}|
is compact: indeed, it is closed and contained in
\bigl|\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\,|\,f(x)\in W[1],N(f(x))<1\}\bigr|\bigr)
by the bound
N(\Lambda_{k})<1
, which is shorthand for
N(\Lambda_{k}(w))<1
whenever
\Lambda_{k}(w)\neq 0
. Further

|S_{\Lambda_{k}\circ f}|\subset B_{k}
.

Next,
|S_{\Lambda_{k}\circ f}|
is given a smooth structure by the fact that
\Lambda_{k}\circ f:X\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid; see
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.3.7]
. In addition,
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.4.1]
transfers the orientation of
f
to a canonical orientation of this branched ep
+
-subgroupoid (by applying the solution set convention in §
2.3
to each branch). Thus
|S_{\Lambda_{k}\circ f}|
has a natural fundamental class
[{\Lambda}_{k}\circ f]\in\check{H}_{d}(|S_{\Lambda_{k}\circ f}|;{\mathbb{Q}})
by Lemma
5.1.3
, which can be pushed forward by the continuous inclusion map
\phi_{k}:S_{k}\to B_{k}\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
.

It remains to prove that the limit
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,(\phi_{k})_{*}[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(|f^{-1}(0)|;{\mathbb{Q}})
exists and is independent of the choices involved.
Here the main technical work is to construct a cobordism given any choice of compactness controlling data
(N^{\prime},{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})
and two
(N^{\prime},{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})
-regular sc
+
-multisections
\Lambda^{i}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
, following the arguments of
[
HWZ
, Cor.15.3.10]
.

As a first step, notice that

\widetilde{f}(t,x):=(t,f(x))
is a sc-Fredholm section of the strong bundle
[0,1]\times{\mathcal{W}}\to[0,1]\times{\mathcal{X}}
with compact solution set
|\widetilde{f}^{-1}(0)|=[0,1]\times|f^{-1}(0)|
, and
\widetilde{N}(t,w):=N^{\prime}(w)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}:=[0,1]\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
control compactness of
\widetilde{f}
.
Moreover, the orientation of
f
and natural orientation of
[0,1]
induce an orientation of
\widetilde{f}
by the product convention in §
2.3
.
Next, instead of the general multisection extensions in
[
HWZ
, §14]
, we can directly construct a sc
+
-multisection
\Lambda^{\prime}:[0,1]\times{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
which extends
\widetilde{\Lambda}|_{\{i\}\times{\mathcal{W}}}=\Lambda^{i}
given on the boundary
\partial|[0,1]\times{\mathcal{X}}|=\{0,1\}\times|{\mathcal{X}}|
and satisfies properties (1) and (2) of
(\widetilde{N},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}})
-regularity.
For that purpose let
\beta:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be a smooth function with
\beta|_{[0,\frac{1}{4}]}\equiv 1
,
1>\beta|_{(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{3})}>0
,
\beta|_{[\frac{1}{3},1]}\equiv 0
.
Then we obtain a sc
+
-multisection
\Lambda^{\prime}:[0,1]\times{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
by extending

\Lambda^{\prime}(t,w):=\Lambda^{0}(\beta(t)^{-1}w)
for
t<\frac{1}{3}
and

\Lambda^{\prime}(t,w):=\Lambda^{1}(\beta(1-t)^{-1}w)
for
t>\frac{2}{3}

with the trivial multisection for
\frac{1}{3}\leq t\leq\frac{2}{3}
, that is
\Lambda^{\prime}(t,0_{x})=1
and
\Lambda^{\prime}(t,w)=0
for all
w\neq 0_{P(w)}
. To check the sc
+
-smoothness and
(\widetilde{N},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}})
-regularity, let
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i},\sigma_{i})_{i\in I}
be local sections and weights representing
\Lambda^{0}
. Then
\Lambda^{\prime}|_{[0,\frac{2}{3}]\times{\mathcal{W}}}
is locally represented by
(\beta{\mathfrak{s}}_{i},\sigma_{i})_{i\in I}
and we can check that (1)
N^{\prime}(\beta{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})<1
and (2)
{\rm supp\,}(\beta{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})\subset[0,1]\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
.
Analogously,
\Lambda^{\prime}|_{[\frac{1}{3},0]\times{\mathcal{W}}}
inherits local section structures from
\Lambda^{1}
, and both of these section structures for
\Lambda^{\prime}
match with a trivial section structure
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(x)=0_{x}
with weights
\sum\sigma_{i}=1
representing
\Lambda^{\prime}
on
[\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3}]\times{\mathcal{W}}
.

Moreover,
\Lambda^{\prime}
already satisfies the transversality conditions (3),(4) in
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.3.7]
over the boundary of
[0,1]\times{\mathcal{X}}
. Indeed, for a local section structure
({\mathfrak{s}}_{i},\sigma_{i})_{i\in I}
representing
\Lambda^{0}
, we have
\Lambda^{\prime}
for
t<\frac{1}{4}
represented by the local sections
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}_{i}(t,x)={\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(x)
and weights
\sigma_{i}
. Then
{\rm T}_{(\widetilde{f},\Lambda^{\prime})}(t,x)
is shorthand for the collection of linearized operators
{\rm D}(\widetilde{f}-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}_{i})(t,x):{\rm T}_{(t,x)}[0,1]\times X\to W_{x}
for each
i\in I
with
\widetilde{f}(t,x)={\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}_{i}(t,x)
. For
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{4}
those are the operators
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\rm T}_{x}X\to W_{x},(\delta t,\delta x)\mapsto{\rm D}(f-{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})(x)\delta x
for each
i\in I
with
f(x)={\mathfrak{s}}_{i}(x)
. So surjectivity follows from transversality of
\Lambda^{0}\circ f
and the kernels are
\ker{\rm D}(\widetilde{f}-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}_{i})(t,x)={\mathbb{R}}\times\ker{\rm D}(f-{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})(x)
.
The latter satisfy the general position requirement
[
HWZ
, Def.5.3.9]
since the reduced tangent space
46
46

46

For readers tracing references in
[
HWZ
]
it might help to note that
E_{x}\subsetneq E
in
[
HWZ
, Def.2.4.7]
is defined on the prior page. In our setting, the partial cone is
C=[0,\infty)\oplus W
for which
E_{(0,w)}=\{0\}\oplus W
.
is
{\rm T}_{(0,x)}([0,1]\times X)=\{0\}\times{\rm T}_{x}X
. Hence suitable complements of
\ker{\rm D}(\widetilde{f}-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}_{i})
are given by complements of
\ker{\rm D}(f-{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})(x)
.
Analogously, transversality and general position for
t\geq\frac{3}{4}
follows from the properties of
\Lambda^{1}
.

Now the proof of
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.3.9]
provides an
(\widetilde{N},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}})
-regular sc
+
-multisection
\widetilde{\Lambda}:[0,1]\times{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
which extends
\widetilde{\Lambda}|_{\{i\}\times{\mathcal{W}}}=\Lambda^{i}
by adding to
\Lambda^{\prime}
finitely many contributions supported in the interior.
In fact, the additional contributions can be chosen with support over
(\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4})\times{\mathcal{X}}
so that we have

\Theta|_{[0,{\varepsilon})\times{\mathcal{X}}}(t,x)={\Lambda}^{0}(f(x))
,
\Theta|_{(1-{\varepsilon},1]\times{\mathcal{X}}}(t,x)={\Lambda}^{1}(f(x))
for
{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{4}
.
The resulting functor
\Theta:=\widetilde{\Lambda}\circ\widetilde{f}:[0,1]\times X\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid, and
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.4.1]
transfers the orientation of
\widetilde{f}
to a canonical orientation of this branched ep
+
-subgroupoid by applying the solution set convention in §
2.3
to each branch.
And as in Remark
2.3.2
the orientation of
\widetilde{f}|_{\{1\}\times X}
induced by boundary restriction of the product orientation of
\widetilde{f}
agrees with the orientation of
f
, and the orientation of
\widetilde{f}|_{\{0\}\times X}
is opposite to the orientation of
f
. Thus with the solution set orientation convention we obtain oriented branched ep
+
-subgroupoid identifications
{\Lambda}^{1}\circ f=\Theta|_{\{1\}\times{\mathcal{X}}}
and
({\Lambda}^{0}\circ f)^{-}=\Theta|_{\{0\}\times{\mathcal{X}}}
, so that Lemma
5.1.3
identifies the resulting fundamental classes

(5.1.5)

(\phi^{0})_{*}[\Lambda^{0}\circ f]=(\phi^{1})_{*}[\Lambda^{1}\circ f]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(|{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}|;{\mathbb{Q}}).

Here
\phi^{i}:|S_{\Lambda^{i}\circ f}|\to B
denotes the inclusion maps for
i=0,1
.

We will use this to prove existence of the limit

\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,(\phi_{k})_{*}[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]
for the system of homeomorphisms

(\iota_{k\ell})_{*}:\check{H}_{d}(B_{\ell};{\mathbb{Q}})\to\check{H}_{d}(B_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
induced by the inclusions
\iota_{k\ell}:B_{\ell}\to B_{k}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
for
k<\ell
and any choice of
(N,{\mathcal{U}}_{k})
-regular sc
+
-multisections
\Lambda_{k}
.
The inclusion
{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell}=\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(B_{\ell})\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
guarantees that
\Lambda_{\ell}
is also
(N,{\mathcal{U}}_{k})
-regular. Comparing it to
\Lambda_{k}
in (
5.1.5
) for
(N^{\prime},{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})=(N,{\mathcal{U}}_{k})
yields

(\phi_{\ell,k})_{*}[\Lambda_{\ell}\circ f]=(\phi_{k})_{*}[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(B_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})
, where the continuous map
\phi_{\ell,k}:|S_{\Lambda_{\ell}\circ f}|\to B_{k}
is given by composing
\phi_{\ell}:|S_{\Lambda_{\ell}\circ f}|\to B_{\ell}
with
\iota_{k\ell}
.
So this identity becomes
(\iota_{k\ell})_{*}(\phi_{\ell})_{*}[\Lambda_{\ell}\circ f]=(\phi_{k})_{*}[\Lambda_{k}\circ f]
, as required for the limit.

To prove independence of choices we need to identify the inverse limits arising from different choices, indexed by
i=0,1
, of compactness controlling data
(N^{i},{\mathcal{U}}^{i})
, nested sequence
B^{i}_{k}\subset B^{i}_{k-1}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}^{i}|
with
\bigcap_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}B^{i}_{k}=|f^{-1}(0)|
, and
(N^{i},{\mathcal{U}}^{i}_{k})
-regular multisections
\Lambda^{i}_{k}
for
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}_{k}:=\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(B^{i}_{k})
.
We can compare both limits to one arising from the compactness controlling data
(\max\{N^{0},N^{1}\},{\mathcal{U}}^{0}_{k}\cap{\mathcal{U}}^{1}_{k})
, the nested sequence
B^{0}_{k}\cap B^{1}_{k}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}^{0}_{k}\cap{\mathcal{U}}^{1}_{k}|
, and corresponding multisections.
Thus it suffices to prove equality of the induced fundamental class for choices as above where either just the multisections differ, or we can (after shifting notation) assume
N^{0}\leq N^{1}
,
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}\subset{\mathcal{U}}^{0}
, and
B^{1}_{k}\subset B^{0}_{k}
.
In both situations we can identify the limits by ensuring that the inclusion maps
\iota^{01}_{k}:B^{1}_{k}\hookrightarrow B^{0}_{k}
yield

(5.1.6)

(\phi^{0}_{k})_{*}[\Lambda^{0}_{k}\circ f]=(\iota^{01}_{k})_{*}(\phi^{1}_{k})_{*}[\Lambda^{1}_{k}\circ f]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(B^{0}_{k};{\mathbb{Q}})\qquad\text{for sufficiently large}\;k.

To do so first note that for fixed
k
, the multisection
\Lambda^{1}_{k}
is also
(N^{0},{\mathcal{U}}^{0}_{k})
-regular,
so that just the maps
\phi^{1}_{k}:|\Lambda^{1}_{k}\circ f|\to|{\mathcal{U}}^{1}_{k}|
and
\phi^{1,0}_{k}:|\Lambda^{1}_{k}\circ f|\to|{\mathcal{U}}^{0}_{k}|
differ by
\phi^{1,0}_{k}=\iota^{10}_{k}\circ\phi^{0}_{k}
. Thus we obtain

(\phi^{1,0}_{k})_{*}[\Lambda^{1}_{k}\circ f]=(\iota^{10}_{k})_{*}(\phi^{1}_{k})_{*}[\Lambda^{1}_{k}\circ f]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(|{\mathcal{U}}^{0}_{k}|;{\mathbb{Q}}).

Now it remains to compare the classes arising from the two
(N^{0},{\mathcal{U}}^{0})
-regular multisections
\Lambda^{1}_{k},\Lambda^{0}_{k}
. Here the prior cobordism construction applies, so that (
5.1.5
) yields

(\phi^{0}_{k})_{*}[\Lambda^{0}_{k}\circ f]=(\phi^{1,0}_{k})_{*}[\Lambda^{1}\circ f]\;\in\;\check{H}_{d}(|{\mathcal{U}}^{0}_{k}|;{\mathbb{Q}}).

Combining both identities proves (
5.1.6
) and thus establishes independence of the fundamental class
[{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}]_{f}
from the choices in this construction.
∎

Remark 5.1.5
.

The above construction can be adapted to the more general case when the base polyfold
{\mathcal{X}}
has formal boundary, so that the zero set
{\mathcal{M}}:=|f^{-1}(0)|
of a polyfold-Fredholm section
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
has formal boundary
{\partial}{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}=|f^{-1}(0)|\cap|{\partial}{\mathcal{X}}|
. In this case, as stated in Corollary
2.1.3
, there is a relative fundamental class
[{\mathcal{M}}]_{f}
constructed as above that is an element of the Çech homology group
\check{H}^{\infty}_{d}({\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\smallsetminus}{\partial}{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}})
. Further details are left to the interested reader.

5.2.
The construction of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
from
{\mathcal{X}}

Consider an ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}=(X,{\bf X})
as in Definition
2.1.1
.
This subsection constructs the ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
of Theorem
2.1.2
which is covered by local uniformizers
X_{\mathcal{V}}=\bigsqcup_{J}V_{J}
and
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(V_{J},V_{J})=G_{J}\times V_{J}
, along with a functor
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}
which induces a homeomorphism

|\psi|:|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}|V|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
to a neighbourhood of a given compact solution set
S\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
. The construction proceeds in the following steps:

\bullet

In Lemma
5.2.1
we find a finite collection of local uniformizers
(U_{i},G_{i})_{i=1,\dots,N}
for
{\mathcal{X}}
(see Definition
3.2.1
) whose realizations
\bigcup_{i}|U_{i}|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
cover the compact solution set
S\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
.

\bullet

Lemma
5.2.2
compiles these local uniformizers into data
(U_{J},G_{J},\rho_{IJ})_{J\subset A}

which satisfies the conditions (a)-(c) of §
4.1
for étale data, except for the (separation) property. Such data consists of translation groupoids
(U_{J},G_{J}\times U_{J})
for each
J\subset A:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
and étale maps
\rho_{IJ}:U_{J}\to U_{I}
for
I\subset J
. It is related to the original ep-groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}
by so-called footprint maps
\psi_{J}:U_{J}\to|{\mathcal{X}}|
which lift homeomorphisms
\text{\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle U_{J}\!$}/\lower 3.44444pt\hbox{$\!\scriptstyle G_{J}$}}\simeq\bigcap_{i\in J}|U_{i}|=:F_{J}
. The
\rho_{IJ}
can then be thought of as lifts of the inclusions
F_{J}\subset F_{I}
.

\bullet

Lemma
5.2.5
constructs subsets
V_{J}\subset U_{J}
from a cover reduction
F^{\prime}_{J}\subset F_{J}
of
S\subset\bigcup_{J\in A}F^{\prime}_{J}
such that the restrictions
(V_{J},G_{J},\rho_{IJ}|_{{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}})_{J\subset A}
with
{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}=\rho_{IJ}^{-1}(V_{I})\subset V_{J}
form étale data as in §
4.1
. This cover reduction is done to meet the separation properties:
