(c)

its restriction to each fiber
(I,y)\times E
is linear with kernel that includes the subspace
E_{A{\smallsetminus}H_{y}}
, where
H_{y}=\min\{H\ \big|\ y\in{\widetilde{Q}}_{HI}\}
;

(d)

{\mathcal{P}}\circ{\tau}={\iota}\circ{\rm pr}:{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
, where
{\iota}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the inclusion.

For (a), we must check that
\tau
is compatible with the morphisms from
V_{I}\times E\to V_{J}\times E
with
I\subset J
.
Further, by
Lemma
3.4.3
(ii), it suffices to check the condition in Definition
3.4.2
(ii). Namely, we are given the map
\tau:Q\times E\to W_{\mathcal{V}}
on objects and must check that

{\tau}(\mu_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}(m,e))=\mu_{\mathcal{V}}({\iota}(m),w),\quad w:={\tau}(s(m),e)\in W_{\psi(s(m)},

where
\mu_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}},\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
are the structure maps of
the two bundles as in Definition
3.4.1
.
Since
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}=\Psi^{*}({\mathcal{W}})
is the pullback of
the original bundle
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
over the equivalence
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
, it suffices to check that

\psi\circ{\tau}(\mu_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}(m,{\underline{e}}))=\mu(\psi({\iota}(m)),w),

where
\mu
is the structure map of
{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
and
w
is as before.
We next simplify the left hand side using the fact that the structure map
\mu_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}

of the trivial bundle
{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E\to{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is simply
55
55

55

Recall from Lemma
4.2.1
that the morphisms in the poset
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
do not involve any group elements, though those in its groupoid completion
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
do.

\mu_{{{\mathcal{Q}}}}:{{\bf Q}}\,{\vphantom{\times}}_{s}{\times}_{P}({{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E)\to{{\mathcal{Q}}}\times E,\ (I,J,{\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\mapsto(J,{\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})=\bigl(t(I,J,{\underline{x}}),{\underline{e}}\bigr).

In other words, it is the product of the target map in
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
with
{\rm id}_{E}
.
Thus, we must check that
for every morphism
m=(I,J,{\underline{x}},{\rm id})\in{{\mathcal{Q}}}
with
I\subset J
we have

(5.4.8)

\displaystyle\psi\bigl(\tau(J,{\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu\bigl(\psi(I,J,{\underline{x}},{\rm id}),\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)

where
\tau_{I}
is as in (
5.4.6
).

If we write
I=(i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots)\subset J=(j_{1}<j_{2}\dots)
where
i_{1}=j_{\ell_{1}}
, and
(J,{\underline{x}})=(x_{j_{1}},m_{j_{1}j_{2}}\dots)
, then (
5.4.6
) defines

\psi\bigl(\tau(J,{\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)=\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})

as an element in
W_{x_{j_{1}}}
.
Further, because the reduction
(F^{\prime}_{I})_{I\subset A}
is compatible with the supports
(C_{i})_{i\in A}
of the partition of unity
(|{\beta}_{j}|)_{j\in A}

as in
Lemma
5.2.4
(iv),

{\beta}_{j_{k}}(x_{j_{k}})=0
if
{\underline{x}}\in{\widetilde{V}}_{IJ}\subset V_{J}
and
j_{k}\in J{\smallsetminus}I
. Indeed, because
|x_{j_{1}}|=|x_{j_{2}}|=\dots=|x_{j_{|J|}}|
for all
{\underline{x}}\in V_{J}
,
{\beta}_{j_{k}}(x_{j_{k}})=0
for all
j_{k}\notin H_{|{\underline{x}}|}=\min\{H\ |\ {\underline{x}}\in{\widetilde{V}}_{HJ}\}
. Moreover, this set
H_{|{\underline{x}}|}=H_{x_{j_{1}}}
as defined in Proposition
4.3.3
. Thus, if we denote the projection
E\to\prod_{i\in H_{|{\underline{x}}|}}E_{i}
by
{\underline{e}}\mapsto{\underline{e}}|_{H}
we have

(5.4.9)

\displaystyle\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})=\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}|_{H_{|{\underline{x}}|}}),\quad H_{|{\underline{x}}|}:=H_{x_{j_{1}}}.

Using this, we check that

(5.4.10)

\displaystyle\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})=\mu\bigl((m^{J}_{j_{1}j_{\ell_{1}}})^{-1},\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)

where
m^{J}_{j_{1}j_{\ell_{1}}}=m_{j_{1}j_{2}}\circ\dots\circ m_{j_{\ell_{1}-1}j_{\ell_{1}}}
as in (
5.2.6
).
Further, when
{\underline{g}}={\rm id}
we have

\tau_{IJ}({\underline{x}},{\rm id},{\underline{e}})=\tau_{I}(\rho_{I}({\underline{x}}),{\underline{e}})
, where
\rho_{I}({\underline{x}})=\rho_{IJ}({\underline{x}})=(x_{j_{\ell_{1}}},m^{J}_{j_{\ell_{1}}j_{\ell_{2}}},\dots)
as in (
5.2.6
).
On the other hand, when
I\subset J
,

\mu\bigl(\psi(I,J,{\underline{x}},{\rm id}),w\bigr)=\mu\bigl((m^{J}_{j_{1}j_{\ell_{1}}})^{-1},w\bigr),\quad w\in W_{x_{j_{\ell_{1}}}}

by the definition of
\psi:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
in Theorem
5.2.6
(ii).
Therefore, when
w=\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})
we obtain

\displaystyle\mu\bigl(\psi(I,J,{\underline{x}},{\rm id}),\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu\bigl((m^{J}_{j_{1}j_{\ell_{1}}})^{-1},\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\quad\mbox{ by }\eqref{eq:checkfunct1}

\displaystyle=\psi\bigl(\tau(J,{\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr),

which proves (
5.4.8
). Thus (a) holds.

The above proof also establishes condition (c), since this follows from (
5.4.9
). Further (d)
holds since
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is given by E
(J,{\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\mapsto(J,{\underline{x}},\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}))
and hence lies over the inclusion
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Thus it remains to check (b), namely that the induced map on objects
\tau_{J}:V_{J}\times E\to W
is
G
-equivariant for each
J
.

The following calculation shows that this follows from the equivariance of the local stabilizations
\tau_{i}
and the functoriality of the construction.
Indeed, in the notation of (
5.4.6
) we have for
{\underline{g}}\in G_{J}
and
{\underline{x}}\in V_{J}

(5.4.11)

\displaystyle\tau_{J}({\underline{g}}*{\underline{x}},{\underline{g}}*{\underline{e}})

\displaystyle\quad=\tau_{J}\bigl(g_{j_{1}}*x_{j_{1}},(g_{j_{1}})^{-1}\circ m_{j_{1}j_{2}}\circ g_{j_{2}},\ldots,g_{j_{|J|}}*x_{j_{|J|}})\,,\,(g_{j_{1}}*e_{j_{1}},\ldots,g_{j_{|J|}}*e_{j_{|J|}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad={\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{j_{1}})\textstyle{\sum_{n=1}^{|H_{\underline{x}}|}\mu\Bigl(\bigl((g_{j_{1}})^{-1}\circ m^{J}_{j_{1}j_{h_{n}}}\circ g_{j_{h_{n}}}\bigr)^{-1},\beta_{h_{n}}(x_{h_{n}})\,\tau_{h_{n}}(g_{h_{n}}*x_{h_{n}},g_{h_{n}}*e_{j_{h_{n}}})\Bigr)}

\displaystyle\quad={\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{j_{1}})\textstyle{\sum_{n=1}^{|H_{\underline{x}}|}\mu\Bigl(\bigl((g_{j_{1}})^{-1}\circ m^{J}_{1h_{n}}\circ g_{h_{n}}\bigr)^{-1},\beta_{h_{n}}(x_{h_{n}})\,g_{h_{n}}*\tau_{h_{n}}(x_{h_{n}},e_{j_{h_{n}}})\Bigr)}

\displaystyle\quad={\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{j_{1}})\textstyle{\sum_{n=1}^{|H_{\underline{x}}|}\mu\bigl((m^{J}_{1h_{n}})^{-1}\circ g_{j_{1}},\beta_{h_{n}}(x_{h_{n}})\,\tau_{h_{n}}(x_{h_{n}},e_{j_{h_{n}}})\bigr)\ =\ g_{j_{1}}*\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}),}

where the last equality holds by (
5.3.4
).
On the other hand, if
{\underline{g}}\in G_{A{\smallsetminus}J}
then
{\underline{g}}*{\underline{e}}-{\underline{e}}\in E_{G_{A{\smallsetminus}H_{{\underline{x}}}}}
and
{\underline{g}}*{\underline{x}}={\underline{x}}
so that

\tau_{J}({\underline{g}}*{\underline{x}},{\underline{g}}*{\underline{e}})=\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{g}}*{\underline{e}}-{\underline{e}})+\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})=\tau_{J}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}).

This completes the proof of condition (b).

Therefore all conditions in Proposition
4.1.5
hold, and
\tau
has a unique
G
-equivariant extension to a functor
\tau:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}\times E\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}
. This proves (ii) and (iii).

Proof of (iv):
It remains to check the
(N_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}})
-regularity properties (1)–(3) in (iv).

To check (1) we estimate for
{\underline{o}}=(I,{\underline{x}}=(x_{1},m_{12},\ldots,m_{(|I|-1)|I|},x_{|I|}))\in X_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\underline{e}}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})\in E=E_{1}\times\ldots\times E_{N}

\displaystyle N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau_{\underline{e}}({\underline{o}}))

\displaystyle=N(\Psi(\tau_{\underline{e}}({\underline{o}})))=N(\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})))

\displaystyle\leq N\bigl({\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{1})\beta_{i_{1}}(x_{1})\tau_{i_{1}}(x_{1},e_{i_{1}})\bigr)+\textstyle\sum_{n=2}^{|I|}N\bigl(\mu\bigl(m_{(n-1)n}^{-1}\circ\ldots\circ m_{12}^{-1},{\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{1})\beta_{i_{n}}(x_{n})\tau_{i_{n}}(x_{n},e_{i_{n}})\bigr)\,\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\sum_{n=1}^{|I|}{\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{1})\beta_{i_{n}}(x_{n})N\bigl(\tau_{i_{n}}(x_{n},e_{i_{n}})\bigr)\;\leq\;\textstyle\sum_{n=1}^{|I|}\|e_{i_{n}}\|_{E_{i}},

where we used the fact that
N
is compatible with the bundle morphisms encoded in
\mu
, and that
0\leq\beta_{i}\leq 1
. This proves (1) with the
G
-invariant norm
\|{\underline{e}}\|:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{E_{i}}
on
E
. Moreover,
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\prime}_{\tau}:=\{(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})\,\|\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|e_{i}\|_{E_{i}}<1\}
guarantees the usual compactness control estimate
N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau_{\underline{e}}({\underline{o}}))<1
.

Note that (2) holds, namely
{\rm supp\,}\tau_{\underline{e}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}=\psi^{-1}({\mathcal{U}})
, because the bump function
{\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}
that occurs in the formula for
\tau_{I}
was chosen to have support in
|{\rm cl}_{X}({\mathcal{U}})|\subset|V|
.

Checking (3) asks us to establish the transversality (and general position along
\partial{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
) of the map
F_{\tau}:X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E\to W_{\mathcal{V}}
,
({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})\mapsto f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}})-\tau({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})
on a product neighbourhood of
X_{\mathcal{V}}\times\{0\}
. For that purpose we will first establish that the differential, restricted to the reduced tangent space,
{\rm D}^{R}F_{\tau}({\underline{o}},\underline{0}):={\rm D}F_{\tau}({\underline{o}},\underline{0})|_{{\rm T}^{R}_{\underline{o}}X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E}
is surjective for every unperturbed solution
f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}})=\tau({\underline{o}},\underline{0})=0_{\underline{o}}
. (See
[
HWZ
, p.46 and Def.2.4.7]
for the notion of reduced tangent space at boundary points. In the interior, we denote the tangent space by
{\rm T}^{R}:={\rm T}
.)
To check this at
{\underline{o}}=(I,{\underline{x}})
, note that

{\rm D}^{R}F_{\tau}({\underline{o}},\underline{0}):{\rm T}^{R}_{\underline{o}}X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E={\rm T}^{R}_{\underline{x}}V_{I}\times E\to W_{\psi({\underline{o}})}\simeq({\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}})_{\underline{o}}

is the linear operator

\displaystyle(\underline{X},{\underline{e}})

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;{\rm D}^{R}f(\psi({\underline{o}}))({\rm d}\psi({\underline{o}})\underline{X})-{\rm D}^{R}\tau_{I}(\cdot,\underline{0})({\underline{x}})\underline{X}-{\rm D}\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},\cdot)(\underline{0}){\underline{e}}

\displaystyle\;=\;{\rm D}^{R}f(\psi({\underline{o}}))({\rm d}\psi({\underline{o}})\underline{X})-\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})

since
\tau_{I}
is linear in the second factor, in particular
\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},\underline{0})=0
.
Since
\psi_{I}
is a local sc-diffeomorphism to a neighbourhood of
o:=\psi({\underline{o}})
(and at boundary points restricts to local sc-diffeomorphisms between the boundary and corner strata),
surjectivity of
{\rm D}^{R}F_{\tau}({\underline{o}},\underline{0})
is equivalent to surjectivity of

(5.4.12)

{\rm T}_{o}X\times E\;\to\;W_{o},\quad(Y,{\underline{e}})\;\mapsto\;{\rm D}^{R}f(o)Y-\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}).

To check surjectivity of this operator we will use of the compatibility between cover reduction and partition of unity achieved in Lemma
5.2.4
: Since
|o=\psi({\underline{o}})|\in F^{\prime}_{I}
and
F^{\prime}_{I}\cap{\rm supp\,}|\beta_{j}|=\emptyset
for
j\notin I
we have
\sum_{i\in I}|\beta_{i}|(|o|)=1
and can thus choose an index
i_{n}\in I
with
|\beta_{i_{n}}|(|o|)>0
.
Now recall that we are working at a solution
{\underline{o}}=(I,{\underline{x}})
given by some tuple
{\underline{x}}=(x_{1},m_{12},\ldots,m_{(|I|-1)|I|},x_{|I|})
of objects
x_{n}\in U_{i_{n}}
and morphisms
m_{(n-1)n}\in{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(x_{n-1},x_{n})
between them, so that
|\psi({\underline{o}})=o=x_{1}|=\ldots=|x_{|I|}|
. In this notation we found an index
1\leq n\leq|I|
with
\beta_{i_{n}}(x_{n})=|\beta_{i_{n}}|(|x_{n}|)>0
.
For this index we also know from Lemma
5.4.1
(3) that the reduced linearization of

f-\tau_{i_{n}}\,:\;X\times E_{i_{n}}\to W
at
(x_{n},0)
is surjective.
Here we used functoriality of
f
to deduce
f(x_{n})=0
from
f(x_{1}=\psi({\underline{o}}))=0
.
More precisely, there is a sc-diffeomorphism
m:=t\circ s_{x_{n}}^{-1}:\text{Nbhd}(x_{n})\to\text{Nbhd}(x_{1})
between neighbourhoods of
x_{n},x_{1}\in X
, which is given by a local inverse
s_{x_{n}}^{-1}
of the source map near
s\bigl(m_{(n-1)n}^{-1}\circ\ldots\circ m_{12}^{-1}\bigr)=x_{n}
. It lifts to sc-isomorphisms
M_{x}:=\mu(s_{x_{n}}^{-1}(x),\,\cdot\,):W_{x}\to W_{m(x)}
between the fibers of the bundle
W\to X
.
With this structure, local functoriality of
f
can be expressed by
M_{x}(f(x))=f(m(x))
. This specifies to
M_{x_{n}}(f(x_{n}))=f(x_{1})=0
, which implies
f(x_{n})=0
since
M_{x_{n}}
is invertible, and the relationship between linearized operators
M_{x_{n}}\circ{\rm D}f(x_{n})={\rm D}f(x_{1})\circ{\rm d}m(x_{n})
.
With that we can compute the image of the operator (
5.4.12
) applied to
{\rm T}_{o=x_{1}}^{R}X={\rm d}m(x_{n})({\rm T}_{x_{n}}^{R}X)
and the subspace
\ldots\{0\}\times E_{i_{n}}\times\{0\}\ldots\subset E

\displaystyle{\rm D}f(x_{1})({\rm T}_{o}^{R}X)-\tau_{I}({\underline{x}},\ldots\{0\}\times E_{i_{n}}\times\{0\}\ldots)

\displaystyle=M_{x_{n}}\bigl({\rm D}f(x_{n})({\rm T}_{x_{n}}^{R}X)\bigr)-M_{x_{n}}\bigl(\beta_{i_{n}}(x_{n})\tau_{i_{n}}(x_{n},E_{i_{n}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=M_{x_{n}}\bigl({\rm D}f(x_{n})({\rm T}_{x_{n}}^{R}X)-\tau_{i_{n}}(x_{n},\beta_{i_{n}}(x_{n})E_{i_{n}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(M_{x_{n}}\circ{\rm D}(f-\tau_{i_{n}})(x_{n},0)\bigr)({\rm T}_{x_{n}}^{R}X\times E_{i_{n}})\;=\;W_{x_{n}}.

Here we used the fact that
M
is an isomorphism,
\beta_{i_{n}}(x_{n})\neq 0
, and
{\rm D}^{R}(f-\tau_{i_{n}})(x_{n},0)
is surjective. This prove surjectivity for (
5.4.12
) and thus for the linearizations of
F_{\tau}=f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau_{I}
at any point in the unperturbed solution set
f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}\subset X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E
.

Now the implicit function theorem
[
HWZ
, 3.1.22, 3.1.26]
guarantees transversality of solutions of
F_{\tau}
in a neighbourhood of
f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}
. We can moreover apply
[
HWZ
, Thm 3.1.22]
on all local boundary and corner strata intersecting
f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}
, where
F_{\tau}
restricts to a transverse section since its reduced linearizations are surjective, and we can conclude that the reduced linearizations (the linearizations of restrictions to boundary and corner strata) are surjective in a neighbourhood of
f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}
.

In fact, our claim is that we can find a neighbourhood
O_{\tau}\subset E
of
0
so that the reduced linearizations of
F_{\tau}:X_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}\to W_{\mathcal{V}}
are surjective at all solutions – and thus all solutions are transverse and in general (hence good) position, as explained in the proof of Lemma
5.4.1
.
We will prove this by contradiction, assuming that
E\ni{\underline{e}}_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
has solutions
f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}}_{k})=\tau({\underline{o}}_{k},{\underline{e}}_{k})
at which
{\rm D}^{R}F_{\tau}({\underline{o}}_{k},{\underline{e}}_{k})
fails to be surjective.
From the already established controls (1) of the size
N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau)
and (2) the support of
\tau
we can deduce that
{\underline{o}}_{k}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
N_{\mathcal{V}}(f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}}_{k}))=N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau({\underline{o}}_{k},{\underline{e}}_{k}))\leq 1
for sufficiently large
k
, and thus
|{\underline{o}}_{k}|
lie in the set

|\{{\underline{o}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\,|\,N_{\mathcal{V}}(f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}}))\leq 1\}|
whose closure in
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is compact, since
(N_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}})
control compactness as in Definition
3.5.1
. So we can find a convergent subsequence
|{\underline{o}}_{k}|\to|{\underline{o}}_{\infty}|
. And since the groupoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is proper by Theorem
5.2.6
(i), we can in fact find a further subsequence with limit
{\underline{o}}_{k}\to{\underline{o}}_{\infty}\in X_{\mathcal{V}}
. This satisfies
f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}}_{\infty})=\lim_{k\to\infty}f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{o}}_{k})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau({\underline{o}}_{k},{\underline{e}}_{k})=\tau({\underline{o}}_{\infty},\underline{0})=0
by continuity of
f_{\mathcal{V}}
and
\tau
.
Now the subsequence
({\underline{o}}_{k},{\underline{e}}_{k})\to({\underline{o}}_{\infty},0)
for sufficiently large
k
must lie in the neighbourhood of
f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)\times\{0\}
on which surjectivity of
{\rm D}^{R}F_{\tau}
was already established above – in contradiction to the assumption.

This argument provides a neighbourhood
O_{\tau}\subset E
of
0
with the claimed transversality properties. To make it
G
-invariant, we replace it by
\bigcap_{g\in G}g*O_{\tau}
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.1.3
.

To simplify notation, we will denote
X_{\mathcal{V}}:={\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}={\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}}
and
W_{\mathcal{V}}:={\rm Obj}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}}=\psi^{*}W
.
Then here is the first statement to be proved:

(i)
Each
{\underline{e}}\in E
induces sc
+
-multisection functors
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
given by

{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}(w):=\tfrac{1}{\#G}\ \#\{{\underline{h}}\in G\,|\,\tau(P_{\mathcal{V}}(w),{\underline{h}}*{\underline{e}})=w\}

and
{\Lambda}_{{\underline{e}}}:=\Psi_{*}{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
given by pushforward
[
HWZ
, Lemma 11.5.2]
.
These multisections are structurable in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §13.3]
, and
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}
is globally structured in the sense of
Definition
3.4.5
.

Proof of (i):
It follows from Theorem
5.4.2
that we are in the situation of
Proposition
4.3.6
. Hence there are multisection functors
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}
that have all the required properties, except possibly that they may not be sc
+
. However, they are sc
+
because
the maps
\tau_{i}
that define the local stabilizations as in Lemma
5.4.1
have been chosen to be sc
+
. This proves (i).

(ii)
There is a comeagre (and thus dense) subset
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}\subset O_{\tau}
such that
for all
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
the functors

\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:={\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}\circ f_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
and
\Theta_{\underline{e}}:={\Lambda}_{{\underline{e}}}\circ f:{\mathcal{X}}|_{V}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
are transversal and in general position over the boundary in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §15.2]
.
This gives both the structure of compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoids
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §9.1]
.

The following is a version of the proof of
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.3.7]
which is simplified by our prior construction of a global equivariant Fredholm stabilization.
Recall from Theorem
5.4.2
(iv) that we found compactness controlling data
(N_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}})
for
f_{\mathcal{V}}
and a
G
-invariant neighbourhood
O_{\tau}\subset\{{\underline{e}}\in E\,|\,\|{\underline{e}}\|<1\}
of
0
such that, formulated with the global section structure
\bigl({\mathfrak{s}}_{h}({\underline{x}})=\tau({\underline{x}},h*{\underline{e}})\bigr)_{h\in G}
in (i),

(1)

N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}))\leq\|{\underline{e}}\|
is bounded by a
G
-invariant norm on
E
. In particular,

{\underline{e}}\in O_{\tau}
guarantees
N_{\mathcal{V}}({\mathfrak{s}}_{h}({\underline{x}}))<1
for all
h\in G
and
{\underline{x}}\in X_{\mathcal{V}}
;

(2)

\tau|_{X_{\mathcal{V}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times E}\equiv 0
and in fact (taking closures)
{\rm supp\,}{\mathfrak{s}}_{h}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}
for all
h\in G
;

(3)

X_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}\to W_{\mathcal{V}},({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\mapsto f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})-\tau({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})
is transverse and in general position on the boundary, in the sense that the reduced linearizations are onto at all solutions.

If
\partial X_{\mathcal{V}}=\emptyset
, then
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}\subset O_{\tau}
will be the set of regular values of the projection

(5.4.13)

\displaystyle\Pi\,:\;\widetilde{S}:=\bigl\{({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\in X_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}\,\big|\,f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})=\tau({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr\}\;\to\;O_{\tau},\qquad({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})\mapsto{\underline{e}}.

If
\partial X_{\mathcal{V}}\neq\emptyset
, then we will need to intersect with the regular values of
\Pi
restricted to boundary and corner strata.
In either case, we need to make sure that
\widetilde{S}
is second countable, so we can apply Sard’s Theorem in countably many charts. This will follow from the compactness controls in (1),(2), and the fact that the realization map
X_{\mathcal{V}}\to|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is globally finite-to-one.
While
\widetilde{S}\subset X_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}
is not closed under morphisms in
{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}{\smallsetminus}{\rm Mor}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\smallsetminus}G}}
, we can still consider its realization
|\widetilde{S}|=\pi_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times E}(\widetilde{S})\subset|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times E|=|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|\times E
.
Since
\tau
vanishes outside of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times E
, we have
\widetilde{S}{\smallsetminus}({\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau})\subset f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(0)\times O_{\tau}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}
and thus

\displaystyle\bigl|\widetilde{S}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\bigl\{({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}\,\big|\,f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})=\tau({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\bigr\}\bigr|

\displaystyle\subset\bigl|\bigl\{{\underline{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\,\big|\,f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})\in W_{\mathcal{V}}[1],N_{\mathcal{V}}(f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}}))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|\times O_{\tau}\;=:\,B_{f_{\mathcal{V}}}\times O_{\tau}

where we used
f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})=\tau({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\in W_{\mathcal{V}}[1]
and
N_{\mathcal{V}}(f_{\mathcal{V}}(x))=N_{\mathcal{V}}(\tau({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}}))\leq\|{\underline{e}}\|<1
.
Now the closure
{\rm cl}(B_{f_{\mathcal{V}}})\subset|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is compact by the compactness control property in Definition
3.5.1
. Since
|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is metric, this makes
{\rm cl}(B_{f_{\mathcal{V}}})
a compact metric space and hence second countable.
Its preimage
\pi_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}^{-1}({\rm cl}(B_{f_{\mathcal{V}}})\subset X_{\mathcal{V}}
inherits second countability by
Remark
5.2.8
.
Finally, we can conclude that
\widetilde{S}\subset\pi_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}}^{-1}({\rm cl}(B_{f_{\mathcal{V}}})\times E
is a subset of a product of second countable spaces and thus itself second countable.

Moreover,
\widetilde{S}
inherits the structure of a smooth manifold with boundary and corners by the implicit function theorem
[
HWZ
, 3.1.22, 3.1.26]
. And its boundary and corner strata, identified by the degeneracy index
d_{\widetilde{S}}:\widetilde{S}\to{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
are given by the boundary strata of the M-polyfold
X_{\mathcal{V}}\times O_{\tau}
, that is
d_{\widetilde{S}}({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})=d_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}({\underline{x}})
(see the transversality part of
[
HWZ
, Thm.5.3.10]
, which does not require a compact solution set).

Next,
\widetilde{S}
has finite dimension given by the Fredholm index of
f
plus
\dim E
. So
\Pi:\widetilde{S}\to O_{\tau}
is smooth since it is the restriction of a sc-smooth map to a sub-M-polyfold of finite dimension, where sc-smoothness coincides with smoothness. We can now apply Sard’s Theorem to any restriction of
\Pi
to the domain
F\subset\widetilde{S}
of a local chart for
\widetilde{S}
or its boundary and corner strata.
Here the corner strata – connected components of
\{s\in\widetilde{S}\,|\,d_{\widetilde{S}}(s)=l\}
for
l\geq 2
– might not strictly be submanifolds, but can still be covered with countably many local charts to
{\mathbb{R}}^{\dim\widetilde{S}-l}
(given by intersections of local faces as in
[
HWZ
, Prop.2.5.16]
).
Sard’s Theorem now assures that the regular values of each restriction
\Pi|_{F}
are a comeagre set
56
56

56

The complement of a comeagre set is of Lebesgue measure
0
, or, equivalently, a countable union of nowhere dense subsets.

and we define
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
to be the intersection of all these comeagre sets.
This proves that
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}\subset O_{\tau}
is comeagre since countable intersections preserve the comeagre property. In addition,
O_{\tau}
is a Baire space by the Baire category theorem for locally compact Hausdorff spaces, and this ensures that every comeagre subset is dense.

Now
{\underline{e}}\in E
is a regular value of
\Pi:\widetilde{S}\to O_{\tau}
if and only if for all
{\underline{x}}\in f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}({\underline{e}})
the projection
\ker{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau)({\underline{o}},{\underline{e}})\supset{\rm T}_{({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})}F\to E
is onto, i.e. if for every
Y\in E
there exists
X\in{\rm T}_{\underline{x}}X_{\mathcal{V}}
so that
{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}})X=\tau({\underline{x}},Y)
, or equivalently

{\rm im\,}\tau({\underline{x}},\cdot)\subset{\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}})
.
This implies

{\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau)({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})={\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}})+{\rm im\,}\tau({\underline{x}},\cdot)={\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}}),

and thus surjectivity of
{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau)({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})
(which holds at all points
({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\in\widetilde{S}
) implies surjectivity of
{\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}})
for regular values
{\underline{e}}
of
\Pi
.

To establish general position at solutions
{\underline{x}}\in f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}({\underline{e}})\cap\partial X_{\mathcal{V}}
it is important to recall that, because
\widetilde{S}
is the solution of an equation in general position, the boundary and corner strata
F\subset\partial\widetilde{S}
are given by intersection with the boundary and corner strata of
X_{\mathcal{V}}\times E
. Therefore, since
E
has no boundary, each boundary or corner stratum
F
has the form

\widetilde{S}\cap(F_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}\times E)
for some local boundary or corner stratum
F_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}\subset\partial X_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Now our notion of regularity
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
also guarantees that
{\underline{e}}
is a regular value of
\Pi|_{F}:F\to O_{\tau}
for each boundary or corner stratum (or chart thereof)
F\subset\widetilde{S}
. That means for all
{\underline{x}}\in f_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}({\underline{e}})\cap F
the projection
\ker{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau)({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\supset{\rm T}_{({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})}F\to E
is onto, where
{\rm T}_{({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})}F=\ker{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau)({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\cap({\rm T}_{\underline{x}}F_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}\times E)
.
As above, this is equivalent to

{\rm im\,}\tau({\underline{x}},\cdot)\subset{\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}})|_{{\rm T}_{\underline{x}}F_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}}
, so that surjectivity of
{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau)({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})|_{{\rm T}_{\underline{x}}F_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}\times E}
(which holds at all points
({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})\in\partial\widetilde{S}
) implies surjectivity of
{\rm im\,}{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}}))({\underline{x}})|_{{\rm T}_{\underline{x}}F_{X_{\mathcal{V}}}}
for regular values
{\underline{e}}
of
\Pi|_{F}
.

Summarizing, we have defined
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}\subset O_{\tau}
and shown that it is comeagre (and hence dense) via Sard’s Theorem. Then we identified
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
with the set of
{\underline{e}}\in O_{\tau}
for which

f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,{\underline{e}})
is transverse and in general position.
The latter perspective implies
G
-invariance of
O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
, since the
G
-equivariance of
f_{\mathcal{V}}
and
\tau
near a solution
({\underline{x}},{\underline{e}})
and the fact that the action of
G
is étale imply that

\bigl({\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,g*{\underline{e}}))(g*x)\bigr)\circ L_{g}=M_{g}\circ{\rm D}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-\tau(\cdot,e))(x),

where
L_{g}:{\rm T}_{x}X_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\rm T}_{g*{\underline{x}}}X_{\mathcal{V}}
is the linearized action and the sc-isomorphism
M_{g}
is given by

M_{g}:=\mu_{\mathcal{V}}\bigl({\alpha}(g*{\underline{x}},g),\cdot\bigr):(W_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}\to(W_{\mathcal{V}})_{g*{\underline{x}}}.

Here
L_{g}
intertwines the tangent spaces of boundary and corner strata since the action of
G
is given by local sc-diffeomorphisms. Thus, given
g\in G
, the regularity of
{\underline{e}}
is equivalent to regularity of
g*{\underline{e}}
.
Thus for
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
all elements in the global section structure
\bigl({\mathfrak{s}}_{h}=\tau(\cdot,h*{\underline{e}})\bigr)_{h\in G}
for
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}
in (i), when subtracted from
f_{\mathcal{V}}
, are transverse and in general position. This makes the perturbation
(f,{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}})
transverse and in general position in the sense of
[
HWZ
, §15.2]
, so that
[
HWZ
, Thm.15.2.22, 15.2.25]
imply that
\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:={\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}\circ f_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
is a tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.9.1.2]
.
In fact,

{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}=\bigcup_{h\in G}(f_{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathfrak{s}}_{h})^{-1}(0)

has a global branching structure given by the submanifolds
M_{h}=(f_{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathfrak{s}}_{h})^{-1}(0)\subset(X_{\mathcal{V}})_{\infty}
with weights
\sigma_{h}=\frac{1}{\#G}
.
Here the inclusion in the smooth part
(X_{\mathcal{V}})_{\infty}
follows from the regularization property
[
HWZ
, Def.3.1.16]
of the Fredholm section
f_{\mathcal{V}}
and the sc
+
property of
{\mathfrak{s}}_{h}
by iterating the argument

{\underline{x}}\in(X_{\mathcal{V}})_{k}\cap(f_{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathfrak{s}}_{h})^{-1}(0)\quad\Rightarrow\quad f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})={\mathfrak{s}}_{h}({\underline{x}})\in(W_{\mathcal{V}})_{k+1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\underline{x}}\in(X_{\mathcal{V}})_{k+1}.

Finally,
\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}
is compact in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.9.1.6]
since

|{\rm supp\,}\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}|=\bigcup_{h\in G}|M_{h}|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|
is a closed subset of the precompact subset

\bigl|\bigl\{{\underline{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\,\big|\,f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})\in W_{\mathcal{V}}[1],N_{\mathcal{V}}(f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}}))\leq 1\bigr\}\bigr|
.
Here we again used compactness control by
({\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}},N_{\mathcal{V}})
and properties (1),(2) to deduce

{\underline{x}}\in M_{h}\Rightarrow f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})={\mathfrak{s}}_{h}({\underline{x}})\in W_{\mathcal{V}}[1]
with
N(f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}}))=N({\mathfrak{s}}_{h}({\underline{x}}))<1
, and
M_{h}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}
since
{\underline{x}}\notin{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}}\Rightarrow f_{\mathcal{V}}({\underline{x}})\neq 0,{\mathfrak{s}}_{h}({\underline{x}})=0\Rightarrow{\underline{x}}\notin M_{h}
.

These results transfer to the pushforward
{\Lambda}_{e}
by Theorem
5.3.1
(iv) as follows: We proved above that for each
{\underline{e}}\in O^{\pitchfork}_{\tau}
the sc
+
-multisection
{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}
is an
(N_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{V}})
-regular perturbation of
f_{\mathcal{V}}
.
This is what gives
\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}
the structure of a compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid.
Now Theorem
5.3.1
(iv) guarantees that
{\Lambda}_{{\underline{e}}}:=\Psi_{*}{\Lambda}_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}:{\mathcal{W}}|_{V}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}
(as well as its trivial extension to
{\mathcal{W}}
) is an
(N,{\mathcal{U}})
-regular perturbation of
f
.
This in turn gives
\Theta_{\underline{e}}
the structure of a compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid.
Alternatively, we could observe that

\Theta_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}\;=\;\Lambda_{{\mathcal{V}},{\underline{e}}}\circ f_{\mathcal{V}}\;=\;\Psi^{*}\Lambda_{\underline{e}}\circ\psi^{*}f\;=\;\Lambda_{\underline{e}}\circ f\circ\psi\;=\;\Theta_{\underline{e}}\circ\psi\;:\;{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{\geq 0}

is given by composition with the equivalence
\psi
in the sense of
[
HWZ
, Def.10.1.1]
from Theorem
5.2.6
(ii). Then one can check in
[
HWZ
, §9.1]
that the conditions for a compact, tame branched ep
+
-subgroupoid are preserved by
\psi
since it is a local sc-diffeomorphism on objects and induces a homeomorphism
|\psi|:|{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{V}}|\to|V|\subset|{\mathcal{X}}|
.

This completes the proof of (ii).
With that, (iii) and (iv) are special cases of Theorem
5.3.1
(iv).
∎

Appendix A
A note on rational Čech homology

The rational Čech cohomology
\check{H}^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
of a paracompact Hausdorff space
X
can be defined as the direct limit of the cohomology groups of a sequence of simplicial complexes given by the nerves of appropriate open covers of
X
.
57
57

57

See
[
H
, p.257]
for a sketch of this construction.
As is shown for example in Eilenberg–Steenrod
[
ES
, Ch IX]
,
the resulting cohomology theory satisfies all the standard axioms together with a continuity property sometimes called tautness: If
X\subset Y
is contained in an ambient paracompact Hausdorff space
Y
and
U_{i+1}\subset U_{i}\subset Y
is a nested sequence of open sets such that

X=\bigcap_{i}U_{i}
, then
\check{H}^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})=\underset{\to}{\lim}\,\check{H}^{*}(U_{i},{\mathbb{Q}})
is the direct limit.

Correspondingly,
[
ES
, Ch IX]
defines the Čech homology groups to be the inverse limit of the homology groups of the nerves. However, these groups fail the exactness axiom in general, because inverse limits even of vector spaces are not well behaved unless these spaces are finite dimensional. More precisely, if

(i_{j}:W_{j+1}\to W_{j})_{j\geq 1}
is a sequence of linear maps between
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces, consider the map

\textstyle d:\prod_{j\geq 1}W_{j}\to\prod_{j\geq 1}W_{j},\quad(w_{j})\mapsto(w_{j}-i_{j}(w_{j+1})),

and define

(A.1)

\displaystyle\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,W_{j}:=\ker d,\qquad\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,W_{j}={\rm coker\,}d.

Then the resulting homology groups
fail exactness because
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,W_{j}
does not vanish for an arbitrary system

(i_{j}:W_{j+1}\to W_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces and linear maps. (For example, consider the case when
W_{j}={\mathbb{Q}}\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\cdots
is the sum of countably many copies of
{\mathbb{Q}}
and
i_{j}
is the shift operator
i_{j}(r_{1},r_{2},\dots)=(0,r_{1},r_{2},\dots)
for each
j
.)
This is not a problem if we restrict to (locally) compact spaces, since then we can assume that the nerves are finite, so that their homology groups are finite dimensional and
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,^{1}
vanishes. In this case, there are other various definitions of rational Čech (or Alexander–Spanier) homology (see for example Massey
[
Ma
]
or Spanier
[
Sp
, Ch 6.6]
) which give the same result by the uniqueness theorem in Spanier
[
Sp
, Ch 6, Ex D]
.

Since polyfolds are not locally compact, we cannot use this version of Čech homology in the proof of
Theorem
5.1.4
since here we take an inverse limit in a polyfold setting.
Most refinements of this theory (such as Steenrod homology) are concerned with dealing with problems arising from the use of more general coefficient groups and are defined in the locally compact setting. The solution of this problem in the case of
{\mathbb{Q}}
coefficients, is apparently ‘well-known’ but not easy to chase down in the literature. The idea is to avoid the problems with

\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,

by

defining the rational Čech homology of a paracompact Hausdorff space
X
to be the dual of its rational cohomology.

With this definition, it is still true that the homology of
X
is the inverse limit of the homology of an appropriate sequence of nerves.
Indeed, it follows from the definitions that given any direct system
(i_{j}:V_{j}\to V_{j+1})_{j\geq 1}
of linear maps between
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces with corresponding inverse dual sequence

(A.2)

\displaystyle i_{j}^{*}:V_{j+1}^{*}\to V_{j}^{*}\qquad V^{*}_{j}:={\rm Hom}(V_{j},{\mathbb{Q}}),

there is an isomorphism

\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,V_{j}^{*}:=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,{\rm Hom}(V_{j},{\mathbb{Q}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\rm Hom}(\underset{\to}{\lim}\,V_{j},{\mathbb{Q}}).

The key point here is that, if the
V_{j}
are vector spaces, then
\underset{\to}{\lim}\,V_{j}=(\oplus_{j}V_{j})/K
where
\oplus_{j}V_{j}
has elements that are finite sums

\sum{\lambda}_{i}(j_{i},v_{i}),\quad\mbox{ with }\;\;v_{i}\in V_{j_{i}},\ {\lambda}_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}},

and

K
is the vector subspace generated by the elements
(j,v)-(j+1,i_{j}(v)).

Thus the elements in
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,V_{j}^{*}
are sequences
({\alpha}_{j}\in V_{j}^{*})
such that

i_{j}^{*}({\alpha}_{j+1})={\alpha}_{j},\quad\forall\ j,\quad\mbox{ or equivalently }\;\;{\alpha}_{j+1}\circ i_{j}={\alpha}_{j}.

Further, the argument in
[
ES
, Ch IX,Thm7.6]
shows that this homology theory does satisfy the exactness axiom because of the following fact.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
(i_{j}^{*}:V_{j+1}^{*}\to V_{j}^{*})_{j\leq 1}
be the dual of a direct system

(i_{j}:V_{j}\to V_{j+1})_{j\geq 1}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces and linear maps as in (
A.2
). Then

\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,V_{j}^{*}=0
.

Proof.

Denote by
i_{k,j}
the composite
i_{k}\circ\ldots\circ i_{j}:V_{j}\to V_{k+1}
and
let
W_{j}:=\{v\in V_{j}\ |\ \exists\ k\geq j:i_{k,j}(v)=0\}\subset V_{j}
and let
\overline{V}_{i}:=V_{i}/W_{i}
.
Then
i_{j}
restricts to a map
i_{j}:W_{j}\to W_{j+1}
, and evidently
\underset{\to}{\lim}\,W_{i}=0
. Therefore
\underset{\to}{\lim}\,V_{i}\simeq\underset{\to}{\lim}\,\overline{V}_{i}
.

Since the maps
\overline{V}_{i}\to\overline{V}_{i+1}
are injective, their duals
\overline{V}_{i+1}^{*}\to\overline{V}_{i}^{*}
are surjective. Hence
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,\overline{V}_{j}^{*}=0
. Indeed, given
({\alpha}_{j})\in\prod\overline{V}_{j}^{*}
, choose
{\beta}_{1}=0
and then inductively choose
{\beta}_{j+1}\in\overline{V}_{j+1}^{*}
so that

{\alpha}_{j}={\beta}_{j}-i_{j}^{*}({\beta}_{j+1})
.
Then
d(({\beta}_{j}))=({\alpha}_{j})
so that
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,\overline{V}_{j}^{*}=0
by (
A.1
).

The exact sequence

0\to\overline{V}_{j}^{*}\to V_{j}^{*}\to W_{j}^{*}\to 0

induces

0\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,\overline{V}_{j}^{*}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,V_{j}^{*}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,W_{j}^{*}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,\overline{V}_{j}^{*}=0\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,V_{j}^{*}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,W_{j}^{*}\to 0.

But
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,W_{j}^{*}=0
. To see this, note that if
({\alpha}_{j})\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,W_{j}^{*}
,
and
\exists\ w_{j},{\alpha}_{j}(w_{j})\neq 0
then, given
k
such that
i_{k,j}(w_{j})=0
, we have

{\alpha}_{j}(w_{j})=i_{k,j}^{*}({\alpha}_{k})(w_{j})={\alpha}_{k}\circ i_{k,j}(w)=0,

a contradiction. Hence
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,V_{j}^{*}\simeq\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,\overline{V}_{j}^{*}
.

It remains to check that
\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}^{1}\,W_{j}^{*}=0
, which holds iff

{\textstyle d:\prod_{j}W_{j}^{*}\to\prod_{j}W_{j}^{*},\quad({\beta}_{j})}\mapsto({\beta}_{j}-i_{j}^{*}({\beta}_{j+1}))\quad\mbox{ is surjective.}

We can write
W_{j}=\oplus_{j<k}W_{j}^{k}

where

w\in W_{j}^{k}{\smallsetminus}\{0\}\ \Longrightarrow\ i_{\ell,j}(w)\neq 0,j<\ell<k,\ \mbox{ and }\ i_{k,j}(w)=0.

Moreover we may choose the summands
W_{j}^{k}
for
j=1,2,3
in turn, so that
i_{j}=\oplus\,i_{j}^{k}
, where
i_{j}^{k}:W_{j}^{k}\to W_{j+1}^{k}

and
W_{j}^{k}=0
for
j\geq k
.
Note that
i_{j}^{k}:W_{j}^{k}\to W_{j+1}^{k}
is injective for
j+1<k
, and is the zero map for
j+1=k
.
In other words, the sequence
(i_{j}:W_{j}\to W_{j+1})_{j\geq 1}
decomposes into a direct sum of sequences,

(i_{j}^{k}:W_{j}^{k}\to W_{j+1}^{k})_{1\leq j<k}
,
each of finite length.

Hence the dual sequence
(i_{j}^{*}:W_{j+1}^{*}\to W_{j}^{*})
is a direct product of sequences of finite length

0=(W_{k}^{k})^{*}\to(W_{k-1}^{k})^{*}\to\cdots\to(W_{1}^{k})^{*},\qquad k\geq 1.

But for each of these sequences the operator
d^{k}
is surjective: indeed given
({\alpha}_{j}^{k})_{1\leq j<k}
with
{\alpha}_{j}^{k}\in(W_{j}^{k})^{*}

we may choose
{\beta}_{j}^{k}\in(W_{j}^{k})^{*}
for
j=k-1,k-2,\dots
by setting
{\beta}_{k-1}^{k}={\alpha}_{k-1}^{k}
, and inductively,

{\beta}_{j}^{k}={\alpha}_{j}^{k}+(i_{j}^{k})^{*}({\beta}_{j+1}^{k}).

Then
d^{k}(\prod_{j}{\beta}_{j}^{k})=\prod_{j}{\alpha}^{k}_{j}
, so that
d=\prod d^{k}
is surjective.
∎

The above discussion justifies the claims made about Čech homology in Remark
5.1.1
. However the proof of Lemma
5.1.3
requires detailed knowledge about the version of this homology theory that holds for locally compact spaces and is the dual of cohomology with compact supports.
This theory, denoted
\check{H}_{*}^{\infty}
, is carefully developed in
[
Ma
, Ch.4]
.
Here are some of its important properties that are established in
[
Ma
, Ch4]
; many are similar to those of locally finite singular homology. We assume throughout that
Y
is
a locally compact Hausdorff space.

(i)

If
Y
is a connected orientable
n
-manifold, then

(A.3)

\displaystyle\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(Y)=0,i>n,\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\check{H}_{n}^{\infty}(Y)={\mathbb{Q}}

with generator
\mu_{Y}
given by the fundamental class;
further

\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})=0
unless
i=n
;

(ii)

if
U\subset Y
is open, there is an induced restriction

(A.4)

\displaystyle\rho_{Y,U}:\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(Y)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(U)

that is compatible with composition: for
V\subset U\subset Y,\ \rho_{Y,V}=\rho_{U,V}\circ\rho_{Y,U}
.

(iii)

If
f:A\to Y
is continuous and proper, then there is an induced pushforward
f_{*}:\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(A)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(Y)
; moreover, given a proper inclusion
{\iota}:A\to Y
, there is a functorial long exact sequence

(A.5)

\displaystyle\cdots\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\iota}_{*}}}{{\to}}\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{Y,Y{\smallsetminus}A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(Y{\smallsetminus}A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\partial}}}{{\to}}\check{H}^{\infty}_{i-1}(A)\to\cdots

(iv)

if
Y=U\cup V
where
U,V
are open, there is an exact Mayer–Vietoris sequence of the form:

(A.6)

\displaystyle\cdots\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i+1}(U\cap V)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(Y)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(U)\oplus\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(V)\to\check{H}^{\infty}_{i}(U\cap V)\to\cdots
